### I / 一

===Original===
He came over the top of the down as the last light failed and could almost have cried with relief at sight of the wood below. He longed to fling himself down on the short stubbly grass and stare at it, the dark comforting shadow which he had hardly hoped to see. Thus only could he cure the stitch in his side, which grew and grew with the jolt, jolt of his stumble down hill. The absence of the cold wind from the sea that had buffeted him for the last half hour seemed like a puff of warm air on his face, as he dropped below the level of the sky. As though the wood were a door swinging on a great hinge, a shadow moved up towards him and the grass under his feet changed from gold to green, to purple and last to a dull grey. Then night came.

A hedge sprang up before his eyes at the distance of a dozen yards. His confused tired senses became aware of the smell of last year’s blackberry leaves wet with past rain. For a moment the scent swathed him in a beautiful content and left him with an ache for time in which to rest here. The grass grew long before he reached the hedge, and a little later his feet were heavy with wet earth and he knew that he was on a path. It was his feet rather than his mind that knew it. They made a rambling progress, now in the muddy centre of the way, now in the grass at the right hand edge, now scraping the outer fringe of the hedge on the other side. His mind was a confusion of scents and sounds, the far hush of the sea, a memory of rattling pebbles, the smell of the wet leaves and the trampled marl, the salt sweep of the wind that he had left behind on the top of the hill, voices, imaginary footsteps. They were jumbled together like the pieces of a puzzle, and they were half forgotten because of his fatigue and fear.

===Chinese===
他翻过丘陵的顶脊时，天边最后一点光正在熄灭，一望见下面的树林，他几乎要因松了口气而哭出声来。他真想一头扑倒在短而粗硬的草茬上，久久盯着那片浓荫——那黑暗而抚慰人的影子，他本来几乎不敢指望还能见到。也只有这样，才治得好他胁腹的剧痛；随着下山时跌跌撞撞、一下又一下的颠簸，那痛愈来愈厉害。半个钟点来一直抽打着他的海上寒风消失了，在他落到天际线以下时，竟像有一股暖风拂过面颊。仿佛树林是一扇装在巨大铰链上的门，一片阴影向他迎面移来，脚下的草色由金黄转成青绿，转成紫褐，最后化为一派黯淡的灰。夜随之降临。

走出十几码，一道树篱蓦地横在眼前。混乱而疲惫的感官，辨出了去年落下的黑莓叶被先前的雨水浸湿的气味。刹那间，这气息把他裹进一种美妙的满足里，又留给他一阵隐痛：多想有时间就此歇下。到走近树篱时，草已长得深了；又过了片刻，双脚叫湿泥坠得沉了，他明白自己是走上了一条小路。明白这一点的，是他的双脚，而不是他的头脑。脚步曲曲折折地挪着，一时走在路中央的烂泥里，一时踩上右手边的草缘，一时又蹭着另一侧树篱的外缘。他的脑子成了一团气味与声响的乱麻：远处大海沉静的低语，鹅卵石咔啦作响的记忆，湿叶和被踩烂的泥灰土的气味，留在身后丘顶上那带咸味的劲风，人声，以及想象出来的脚步声。它们像拼图的碎块一样杂乱地堆在一处，又因他的疲惫与恐惧被忘掉了一半。

===Original===
The fear in his mind told him that paths were dangerous. He whispered it out loud to himself, “Dangerous, dangerous,” and then because he thought that the low voice must belong to another on the path beside him, he scrambled panic stricken through the hedge. The blackberry twigs plucked at him and tried to hold him with small endearments, twisted small thorns into his clothes with a restraint like a caress, as though they were the fingers of a harlot in a crowded bar. He took no notice and plunged on. The fingers grew angry, slashed at his face with sharp, pointed nails. “Who are you anyhow? Who are you anyhow? Think yourself mighty fine.” He heard the voice, shrill and scolding. She had a pretty face and a white skin. “Another day,” he said, because he could not wait. He had to leave the town. The last twigs broke and the night became darker under trees. Through the latticing of the leaves half a dozen stars came suddenly to view.

He stumbled against a tree and leant for a moment against it, allowing his legs to relax. Freed from some of the weight of his body, they seemed to ache more than ever. He tried to pull himself together and remember exactly where he was—no longer in Shoreham but in a wood. Had he been followed? He listened, hungry for silence, and was rewarded. Had he ever been followed? He had seen Carlyon in the bar of the Sussex Pad, but only in the mirror behind the harlot’s head. Carlyon had been standing sideways to him and was ordering a drink. Unless Carlyon saw him leave, he was safe. What a fool he had been to leave so suddenly. He should have gone quietly out and taken the girl with him. Fool, fool, fool, fool, the word droned on in his mind, a sleepy and mechanical reiteration. His eyes closed, then opened with a start, as a twig broke under his own foot. He might have been asleep now in a comfortable bed, all the more comfortable for being shared. She was pretty and had a good skin. He didn’t suppose he would have been asleep… He woke again two minutes later feeling cold. He had dreamed that he was again in the bar, looking in the mirror at Carlyon’s face, and in the dream the face had begun to turn. But was it only in the dream? He could not stay here and again he began to run, very stumblingly because of the roots of the trees.

===Chinese===
心里的恐惧告诉他：小路是危险的。他把这念头低声念出声来：“危险，危险。”跟着，又觉得这低语必定出自身旁路上的另一个人，便慌不择路地钻过了树篱。黑莓的枝条勾扯着他，想用小小的温存把他留住；细小的荆棘缠进他的衣裳，克制得近乎爱抚，仿佛拥挤酒吧里妓女的手指。他毫不理会，只顾往里冲。那些手指恼了，便用尖利的指甲朝他脸上抓去。“你算老几？你算老几？倒自以为很了不起了。”他听见了那声音，尖利，骂骂咧咧。她有一张漂亮脸蛋，皮肤白净。“改天吧，”他说，因为他等不及了。他必须离开那座镇子。最后几根枝条折断了，树下的夜色更浓。透过枝叶交错的隙缝，五六颗星星突然映入眼帘。

他一头撞在树上，便倚着歇了片刻，让双腿松泛松泛。卸去了几分身子的重量，它们倒好像疼得更凶了。他竭力定下神来，想准确记起自己身在何处——已经不在肖勒姆（Shoreham），而在一片树林里。有人跟踪他吗？他侧耳细听，如饥似渴地盼着寂静，也如愿了。他到底有没有被人跟踪？他在「萨塞克斯帕德」（the Sussex Pad）的酒吧里见到过卡莱昂（Carlyon），可只在那妓女头顶后方的镜子里。卡莱昂当时侧身站着，正在点酒。只要卡莱昂没看见他离开，他就是安全的。自己走得那么突然，真是个大傻瓜。他本该悄悄溜出去，把那姑娘也一并带走。傻瓜，傻瓜，傻瓜，傻瓜，这个词在脑海里嗡嗡低回，昏昏欲睡、机械呆板地反复着。他两眼阖上，又猛地睁开——一根细枝在他自己脚底下断了。此刻他本可以在一张舒服的床上睡着，有人同衾，就更舒服了。她漂亮，皮肤也好。他猜自己多半是睡不着的……两分钟后他又醒转过来，只觉得冷。他梦到自己又回到那间酒吧，在镜中望着卡莱昂的脸，梦里的那张脸已开始转过来了。可，难道只是在梦里？他不能在此久留，于是又跑了起来，被树根绊得踉踉跄跄。

===Original===
Oh, but he was tired, tired, tired. His wrist was hurting and felt damp and weak, slashed by the thorns in the hedge. If Carlyon had suddenly appeared now in front of him, he would have thrown himself down on his knees and cried. Carlyon wouldn’t do anything. Carlyon was a gentleman like himself. And one could always appeal to Carlyon’s sense of humour. “Hello, Carlyon, old man, it’s ages since I’ve seen you. Have you heard this one, Carlyon, old chap? Carlyon, Carlyon, Carlyon.” “And there shall be weeping and gnashing of teeth.” “How dare you teach my boy that stuff?” and then he’d beaten her. His father had always talked of him as “my boy,” as though his mother had not borne the pain. The damned old hypocritical bully. “Please God, give me a bear.” He hadn’t wanted a live puppy, which needed looking after. Am I going to faint, he asked himself? What’s this wood doing? Why a wood? Hansel and Gretel. There should be a cottage soon with a witch in it, and the cottage should be made of sugar. “I am so hungry,” he said aloud. “I can’t wait for Gretel.” But inside himself he knew only too certainly that there was no Gretel. He and Gretel had kissed under the holly tree on the common one spring day. Across a faintly coloured sky a few plump clouds had tumbled recklessly. And then time and again he was walking up narrow stairs to small rooms and untidy beds, and walking down again feeling dissatisfied, because he had never found Gretel there. How curious everything was. And now this wood… He saw a light glimmering in front at what seemed an infinite distance, and he began to run, remembering that Carlyon might be somewhere behind him in the dark. He had to get on, get on, get on. His feet stumbled, stumbled, and every stumble sent a shoot of pain up his arm to the shoulder, starting from his torn wrist, but not a stumble brought the light nearer. It shone mockingly ahead, very small and sharp and immeasurably knowing. It was as though the world had heaved upward, like a ship in a rough sea, and brought a star to its own lamp’s level. But as distant and as inaccessible as the star was the light.

===Chinese===
哦，可他累极了，累极了，累极了。手腕疼着，摸上去又湿又软，没了力气——是叫树篱上的荆棘划破的。倘若卡莱昂此刻突然出现在他面前，他会扑通跪倒，放声大哭。卡莱昂不会把他怎么样。卡莱昂跟他一样，也是个绅士。再说，人总可以向卡莱昂的幽默感求救嘛。“喂，卡莱昂，老兄，咱们多少年没见了。听过这一个笑话吗，卡莱昂，老伙计？卡莱昂，卡莱昂，卡莱昂。”“在那里必要哀哭切齿。”“你怎么敢教我儿子那种东西？”接着他就动手揍她。父亲总把他称作“我儿子”，仿佛他母亲不曾受过那份生养的痛。那该死的老伪善，老恶霸。“求求上帝，赐我一只熊。”他可不要活蹦乱跳的小狗崽，那得费心照料。我要昏过去了吗，他问自己？这片树林想干什么？为什么偏偏是树林？《汉塞尔与格蕾特尔》（Hansel and Gretel）。很快就该有一座小屋了，里面住着巫婆，小屋该是糖做的。“我好饿啊，”他大声说。“我等不及格蕾特尔了。”可他心里再确凿不过地知道：并没有什么格蕾特尔。他和格蕾特尔曾在某个春日，在荒原的冬青树下接过吻。淡淡着色的天空上，几朵饱满的云正肆意翻滚。再往后，他便一次又一次走上狭窄的楼梯，走进小房间、走向凌乱的床铺，又一次次悻悻走下来，因为他在那里从来没有找到过格蕾特尔。一切都多么奇怪。而眼前又是这片树林……他看见前方隐约有一点光在闪，距离仿佛没有尽头，便又跑了起来，心里记挂着卡莱昂也许正在黑暗中的什么地方跟着他。他得走下去，走下去，走下去。双脚绊了一步又一步，每绊一下，一阵刺痛就从撕裂的手腕顺着胳膊直窜到肩头，可没有哪一绊能让那光近些。那光在前头嘲弄地亮着，又小又锐，仿佛无所不知。就好像整个世界像风暴里的船一样向上拱起，把一颗星星抬到与自己的灯齐平的高度。而那光呢，同那颗星一样遥远，一样不可企及。

===Original===
He was almost on top of the light before he realised that its smallness was due to size and not to distance. The grey stones of a cottage suddenly hunched themselves up between the trees. To the man raising his head to see the ramshackle bulk, it was as though the uneven, knobbled shoulders of the place had shrugged themselves from the earth. The cottage had but one floor above the ground, and the window with which it faced the wood was of thick glass, slightly tinted, like the glass of liquor bottles. The stones of the place gave the impression of having been too hastily and formlessly piled upon one another, so that now with old age they had slipped, some this way, some that way, a little out of the perpendicular. An excrescence built clumsily upon one end might have been anything from some primitive sanitary arrangement to a pigsty or even a small stable.

He stood and watched it and swayed a little upon his feet. Soon he would go up and knock, but for the moment in spite of weariness and the pain from his wounded wrist, he was engaged in the favourite process of dramatising his actions. “Out of the night,” he said to himself, and liking the phrase repeated it, “out of the night.” “A hunted man,” he added, “pursued by murderers,” but altered that to “by worse than death.” He imagined himself knocking on that door. He saw it opening, and there would appear an old white-haired woman with the face of a saint. She would take him in, and shelter him. She would be like a mother to him and bind his wrist and give him food and drink, and when he had slept he would tell her everything—“I am a hunted man,” he would say, “pursued by worse than death.”

===Chinese===
他几乎走到那光的跟前，才明白它之所以小，是因为体积，而不是因为距离。一座小屋的灰色石身，蓦地在树木之间耸起了驼背。他抬头打量这摇摇欲坠的庞然大物，只觉得它那高低不平、骨节嶙峋的肩膀，像是从泥土里自己耸出来的。小屋在地上只有一层，朝向树林的那扇窗镶着厚玻璃，微微带一点颜色，像装酒的瓶子那种玻璃。垒屋的石头看上去堆得又仓促又没个章法，以致年深日久，全都滑移了，有的歪向这边，有的歪向那边，稍稍偏离了垂直。屋一头笨拙地接出一块赘疣似的东西，说它是简陋的茅厕也好，猪圈也好，甚至一间小马厩，都说得过去。

他站着打量它，脚下微微打晃。过一会儿他就要上前敲门了；可这一刻，尽管疲惫不堪，伤了的手腕又疼，他还在忙着自己最拿手的那套把戏——把一举一动都演成戏文。“自黑夜中来，”他对自己说，喜欢上了这个说法，便又念了一遍，“自黑夜中来。”“一个被追捕的人，”他补上一句，“被凶手们追捕，”随即又改成“被比死亡更可怕的东西追捕”。他想象自己敲响那扇门。他看见门开了，门里现出一位白发老妇，生着一张圣徒般的脸。她会收留他，庇护他。她会像母亲一样待他，替他包扎手腕，给他吃的喝的；等他睡过一觉，他就把一切告诉她——“我是一个被追捕的人，”他会这样说，“被比死亡更可怕的东西追捕。”

===Original===
He became afraid again of his own reiteration of the phrase “worse than death.” There was little satisfaction in an image which stood upon a fact. He looked behind him once into the dark from which he had come, half expecting to see Carlyon’s face luminous there, like a lighted turnip. Then he stepped nearer to the cottage.

When he felt the rough stones warm under his palm, he was comforted. At least it was something solid to have at one’s back. He turned and faced the wood, stared and stared, trying to pick out details and to see where each trunk stood. But either his eyes were tired, or else the darkness was too deep. The wood remained a black, forbidding immensity. He felt his way cautiously along the wall to the window and then, standing on tiptoe, tried to peer within. He could perceive only shadows, and the flame of a candle which stood on the ledge inside. He thought that one shadow in the room moved, but it might merely have been the effect of the flickering light. His mind cleared a little and gave room for cunning, and with cunning there crawled in uneasiness. He felt his way very cautiously along the wall towards the door, listening for any sound from the cottage on one side of him and from the wood on the other. It would be like his luck, he thought, his heart giving a sick jolt, if he had stumbled on a smuggler’s hole. It was just such a night, he knew, as he would have chosen himself to run a cargo, dark and moonless. Perhaps he had better move on and avoid the place, and even as the thought crossed his mind his fingers touched the wood of the door. His legs were weak as butter, his wrist was sending stab after stab of pain up his arm, and the edge of an approaching mist touched his consciousness. He could go no further. Better face what lay within the cottage than lie defenceless outside with Carlyon perhaps approaching through the wood. The vision of the white-haired old mother had been effaced very completely. He fumbled at the door, but he was unprepared for it to swing readily open, and he fell on his knees across the threshold in a silly sprawl.

===Chinese===
他又害怕起自己翻来覆去念叨“比死亡更可怕”这个调子来了。一个立在事实之上的形象，是给不了多少快慰的。他回头朝来路的黑暗里望了一眼，半是等着看见卡莱昂的脸在那里幽幽发亮，像一盏点亮了的芜菁灯。随后他朝小屋走近了些。

手掌贴上粗糙的石头，觉出一点温热，他心下稍安。背后至少有了个坚实的东西。他转过身去面对树林，盯了又盯，想分辨出些细节，看清每棵树干立在哪儿。可不知是眼睛累了，还是黑暗太深，树林始终是一片黑沉沉的、拒人千里的浩大。他沿着墙小心翼翼摸到窗前，踮起脚尖朝里张望。只望见些影子，还有立在里侧窗台上的一支蜡烛的火苗。他觉得屋里有个影子动了动，可那也许只是烛光摇曳的错觉。他脑子清醒了些，腾出地方来了心眼儿，而随着心眼儿，不安也悄悄爬了进来。他极其小心地沿着墙摸向门口，一侧耳朵提防着小屋的动静，另一侧提防着树林的声响。他心想，要是自己歪打正着闯进了一处走私者的巢穴，那才真像他的运气——想到这里，心口一阵发恶似的悸跳。他知道，今夜正是他自己会挑来走私一批货（run a cargo）的夜晚：天黑，无月。也许他顶好还是继续赶路，避开这地方——而这念头才掠过脑海，他的手指已经碰到了门板。他的双腿软得像黄油，手腕的疼一记接一记顺着胳膊往上扎，一片渐渐逼近的雾的边缘已经擦到他的知觉。他再也走不动了。与其毫无遮拦地倒在外头，听凭卡莱昂也许正穿过树林逼上来，倒不如去面对小屋里躺着的一切。那位白发老母亲的幻象，已经被抹得一干二净。他在门上摸索，却没料到那扇门竟会顺势敞开，便傻乎乎地一头跪跌在门槛上，狼狈不堪。

===Original===
He looked up. Clogged and dulled by that ever-approaching mist a voice had spoken to him. “Stay where you are,” it had said with a kind of quiet and unsurprised command. Now he saw at the other side of the room, wavering a little like a slim upstrained candle-flame, a woman. She was young, he recognised with an automatic leer, and white in the face but not frightened. What kept him still upon his knees, besides the complete physical weariness that made him unwilling to rise, was the gun which was aimed steadily at his chest. He could see the hammer raised.

“I say,” he said. “I say.” He was displeased at the dead sound of his own voice. He felt that it should be full of the mingled pathos of weariness and appeal. “You needn’t be afraid,” he tried again. “I’m done in.”

“You can stand up,” she said, “and let me look at you.” He rose shakily to his feet, with a feeling of immense grievance. This wasn’t the way for a woman to behave. She should be frightened, but she very damnably wasn’t. It was he who felt the fear, with his eye warily watching the gun.

===Chinese===
他抬起头来。有一个声音已经对他开了口——被那阵不断逼近的雾闷住，含混发钝。“待在原地别动，”那声音说，平静，不见惊讶，带着命令的口气。这时他才看见，屋子那头站着一个女人，微微地摇曳，像一枝细长的、笔直向上的烛焰。她很年轻——他带着下意识的轻佻眼风认出了这一点——脸色发白，却并不害怕。让他跪在那里一动不敢动的，除了那股子懒得起身的浑身疲乏，还有那支稳稳对准他胸口的枪。他看得见击锤已经扳起。

“我说，”他说。“我说。”自己的声音这样死气沉沉，他很不满。它本该满含着疲惫与哀恳交织的凄楚才对。“你不用害怕，”他又试了一遍，“我累垮了。”

“你可以站起来，”她说，“让我瞧瞧你。”他哆哆嗦嗦地站起身，满心都是天大的委屈。女人不该是这个做派的。她该害怕才是，可她偏偏该死地不怕。害怕的反倒是他，一边还警惕地盯着那支枪。

===Original===
“Now what do you want?” she asked. To his surprise there was no anger in her voice, but a quite genuine curiosity. It annoyed him to know that she was patently the mistress of the situation. It made him even in his weakness want to bully her, to teach her. If only he could get that gun…

“I want a hiding place,” he said. “I’m being followed.”

“Runners?” she asked. “Gaugers? You can’t stop here. You’d better go the way you came.”

===Chinese===
“那么，你要什么？”她问。出乎他的意料，她声音里没有怒气，倒有一种十足真切的好奇。他恼火地意识到，局面分明攥在她的手里。这让他即使在虚弱之中也想欺压她，教训她。只要他能夺到那支枪……

“我要一个藏身的地方，”他说。“有人在追我。”

“走私贩子（runners）？”她问。“还是缉私税吏（gaugers）？你不能待在这儿。你顶好从原路回去。”

===Original===
“But I can’t,” he said, “they’d get me. Look here, I’m on the side of the law. It’s not the officers who are after me.” His eyes fixed on the gun, he made a step forward, spreading out his hands in appeal, in a gesture which he had often seen made on the stage.

“Keep back,” she said, “you can’t stay here. Turn round and go out.”

“For the love of God,” he said. He had picked that expression also from the stage, but the girl could not be expected to know it. It sounded genuine, for his voice was full of real tears. He was tired out and wanted to sleep.

===Chinese===
“可我不能啊，”他说，“他们会抓住我的。你听我说，我可是站在法律这一边的。追我的可不是那些缉私官员。”他两眼死死盯着枪，向前迈了一步，摊开双手，摆出一副哀恳的姿势——那姿势他在戏台上常见人摆。

“退后，”她说，“你不能待在这儿。转过身，出去。”

“看在上帝份上，”他说。这话也是他从戏台上学来的，不过自然不能指望那姑娘知道。这话听着倒像真心，因为他的嗓音里已盛满了真实的眼泪。他累坏了，只想睡觉。

===Original===
“If you are being followed,” she said, as though speaking to a very stupid child, “you are wasting time here.”

“When I get you,” he said with sudden fury, “I’ll teach you charity. Call yourself a Christian”—his eyes filled with warm sentimental tears at a sudden vision of little grey churches, corn fields, stiles, honeyed distant bells in the dusk, robins in snow. “I’ll teach you,” he said again. The white serenity of her face infuriated him. “I’ll tell you what I’ll do to you.” With childish petulance he flung his mud at something beautiful and very distant, hated himself and enjoyed his hatred. He described what he would do to her in a brief, physiological sentence, and rejoiced at the flush which it fetched to her face. His outburst brought the mist down closer upon him. “You can join your fellows on the streets then,” he cried at her, determined to hurt before fainting should make him a powerless, shameful weakling at her mercy. For a moment he thought that she was going to shoot. He was too exhausted for fear now and felt only a vague satisfaction that he had made himself sufficiently hateful to drive her to action. Then the danger passed. “I told you to go,” was all she said, “I don’t know what you want here.”

===Chinese===
“既然有人追你，”她说，口气就像在对一个顶笨的孩子说话，“你在这儿就是在浪费时间。”

“等我逮住你，”他勃然大怒地说，“我要教教你什么叫仁慈。亏你还自称是基督徒”——话到此处，灰色的小教堂、麦田、栅门、暮色里远处蜜一样甜的钟声、雪地上的知更鸟，忽然一齐涌上眼前，他眼里蓄满了温热的、感伤的泪。“我要教教你，”他又说一遍。她脸上那种苍白的宁静把他激怒了。“我告诉你我要拿你怎么着。”他带着孩子气的耍赖，把烂泥掷向某个美丽而遥远的东西，恨他自己，又品着这恨的滋味。他用一句简短的、赤裸裸的生理字眼描述了他要对她干的事，并为这话在她脸上激起的红晕而得意。这一阵发作，让那片雾又朝他压近了些。“那你干脆上街找你的同类作伴去吧，”他朝她嚷道，打定主意要赶在昏厥把他变成一个任她摆布、软弱可耻的可怜虫之前，先伤着她。有那么一瞬，他以为她真要开枪了。他已疲惫得顾不上害怕，只模糊地感到一种满足：他总算把自己变得够可憎，逼得她不得不动手了。随后危险过去了。“我叫你走，”她只说了这一句，“我不知道你到这儿来想干什么。”

===Original===
He swayed a little on his feet. He could hardly see her now. She was a lighter wisp in a world of grey. “Look, he’s at the window,” he cried with sudden vehemence, and as the wisp moved he lunged forward.

He felt the gun within his hand and forced it upwards, struggling at the same time for the trigger. The girl had been taken by surprise and for the moment gave way.

With the muzzle pointing somewhere at the ceiling, he pulled the trigger. The hammer fell, but there was no explosion. The girl had fooled him with an unloaded weapon. “Now, I’ll teach you,” he said. He tried to wrench the gun away, the better to get at her, but his right wrist seemed to double up and collapse with the effort. He felt a hand press against his face, and his whole body grew weak, and he stumbled backwards. He hit against a table which he had not seen was in the room, so focused had his eyes been on the danger in front of him. He put out a hand to save himself, for his legs seemed made in numerous joints, which were now all folding in upon each other. Something fell to the floor with a brief dart of gold, like a disdainful guinea, and his fingers were scorched for a moment by flame.

===Chinese===
他脚下又晃了晃。此刻他几乎看不见她了。她只是灰色天地间一缕淡些的影子。“瞧，他在窗口！”他猛地厉声喊道，趁那影子一动，纵身扑上前去。

他觉出枪已攥在自己手里，便把它狠狠往上抬，同时死命去抢扳机。姑娘猝不及防，一时招架不住，退让了。

枪口不知指着天花板上的什么地方，他扣动了扳机。击锤落下，却没有炸响。姑娘拿一支没装子弹的枪耍了他。“好哇，这回我来教教你，”他说。他想把枪夺开，好更顺手地对付她，右腕却仿佛随着这一使劲对折起来，瘫了。他觉出一只手抵住自己的脸，周身的力气都泄了，踉踉跄跄向后倒去。他撞在一张桌子上——他根本没瞧见屋里还有这么一张桌子，因为他的两眼只顾盯住眼前的危险。他伸手去撑，两条腿倒好像生了许多关节，此刻正一节一节地对折起来。一样东西带着一道短促的金光跌落在地，像一枚不屑一顾的几尼金币，他的手指也霎时叫火苗燎了一下。

===Original===
The pain cleared his brain with the suddenness of an unseen hand wrenching a curtain aside. He looked behind him and found himself staring into a heavy bearded face, over which three other candles sent a straying luminance.

“But…” he cried, and never knew what he meant to add. He backed away in disgust from the body where it lay in its unlidded and unvarnished coffin. He had never met death before so startlingly face to face. His mother he had never seen in death, for his father had huddled her quickly away in earth with a cross and a bunch of flowers, and his father had been killed in a running fight at sea and dropped unobtrusively over the side, while he was learning to decline *oikia* at his school in Devon. He was frightened and disgusted and sick and somehow ashamed. It was, he felt remotely, indecorous to broil thus over a coffin, even though the coffin were of unvarnished deal. His eyes searched a deepening darkness, flickering with gold points where the candles shone, until they found a face which seemed now white rather with weariness than serenity. “I’m sorry,” he said, and then the lights were all extinguished.

===Chinese===
疼痛涤清了他的脑子，快得像一只看不见的手猛地把帘子扯开。他回头一看，发现自己正瞪着一张浓须覆盖的沉重大脸，三支别的蜡烛的光正从那张脸上游移而过。

“可是……”他喊出声来，却始终不知道自己原想再说什么。他厌恶地朝后退开，避开那具尸体——它躺在棺木里，没有棺盖，也不上漆。他还从未这样猝然地同死亡打个照面。母亲死时的样子他从未见过，因为父亲只用一副十字架、一束花，就匆匆忙忙把她葬进了土里了事；父亲自己则在海上一场且战且退的交火中丧命，被人不声不响地从船舷边抛了下去——那时他正在德文郡（Devon）的学校里，学着给 *oikia*（希腊语，房屋）一词变格。他又怕，又厌，又恶心，还莫名地羞愧。他隐隐觉得，这样趴在一口棺材上纠缠不清实在有失体统，哪怕那棺材不过是没上漆的松木钉的。他的目光在愈来愈深的黑暗里搜寻，烛光闪耀处浮动着点点金光，终于找到一张脸——那脸的白，此刻与其说是宁静，不如说是疲惫。“对不起，”他说。随后，灯烛全都熄灭了。

### II / 二

===Original===
Over a toppling pile of green vegetables two old women were twittering. They pecked at their words like sparrows for crumbs. “There was a fight, and one of the officers was killed.” “They’ll hang for that. But three of them escaped.” The vegetables began to grow and grow in size, cauliflowers, cabbages, carrots, potatoes. “Three of them escaped, three of them escaped,” one of the cauliflowers repeated. Then the whole pile fell to the ground, and Carlyon was walking towards him. “Have you heard this one?” he said. “Three of them escaped, three of them escaped.” He came nearer and nearer and his body grew in size, until it seemed as though it must burst like a swollen bladder. “Have you heard this one, Andrews?” he said. Andrews became aware that somewhere behind him a gun was being levelled, and he turned, but there were only two men, whose faces he could not see, laughing together. “Old Andrews, we won’t see his like again. Do you remember the time﻿ ﻿…” “Oh, shut up, shut up,” he called, “he was only a brute, I tell you. My father was a brute.” “Ring-a-ring-a-roses,” his father and Carlyon were dancing round him, holding hands. The ring got smaller and smaller and he could feel their breath, Carlyon’s cool and scentless, his father’s stale, tobacco-laden. He was gripped round the waist, and someone called out, “Three of them escaped.” The arms began to drag him away. “I didn’t do it,” he cried. “I didn’t do it.” Tears ran down his cheeks. He struggled and struggled against the pulling arms.

===Chinese===
在一堆摇摇欲坠的青菜上头，两个老太婆正叽叽喳喳。她们啄食着字眼，像麻雀争抢面包屑。“出了场斗殴，死了一个缉私官员。”“这够他们上绞架的了。不过有三个人逃掉了。”青菜开始生长，越长越大，成了花椰菜、卷心菜、胡萝卜、土豆。“三个人逃掉了，三个人逃掉了，”其中一颗花椰菜反复念叨。随后整堆菜轰然塌地，卡莱昂正朝他走来。“听说过这桩新鲜事吗？”他说。“三个人逃掉了，三个人逃掉了。”他越走越近，身躯越涨越大，仿佛一只胀鼓的膀胱，眼看就要迸裂。“听说过这桩新鲜事吗，安德鲁斯？”他说。安德鲁斯觉出背后不知何处有一支枪正朝他平举起来，他转过身，却只见两个看不清脸面的人凑在一处笑。“老安德鲁斯，再也见不着他这样的人了。还记得那一回……”“哦，住口，住口，”他喊道，“我告诉你们，他不过是个畜生。我父亲就是个畜生。”“围个玫瑰圈”，他父亲和卡莱昂手拉着手，围着他转圈起舞。圈子越缩越小，他都能觉出两人的呼吸：卡莱昂的又凉又没有气味，他父亲的陈腐不堪，满是烟草味。他被拦腰抱住，有人喊道：“三个人逃掉了。”那些胳膊开始把他往回拖。“不是我干的，”他哭喊，“不是我干的。”眼泪顺着面颊往下淌。他挣啊挣啊，挣不脱那些拖拽着他的手臂。

===Original===
He emerged slowly into a grey dispersing mist, cut by jagged edges. They grew towards his sight and became boxes, old trunks, dusty lumber. He found that he was lying upon a pile of sacking and there was a stale smell in the room of earthen mould. A pile of gardening implements leant up against one wall, and one upturned lidless trunk was full of little shrivelled bulbs. At first he thought that he was in the potting shed of his home. Outside should be a lawn and a tall pine, and presently he would hear the shuffling footsteps of the gardener. The old man always dragged his left foot behind him, so that there was no regular cadence to his steps. They had to be counted like an owl’s cry﻿—one twoooo﻿—one twoooo. How it was that he came to be lying in the potting shed in the grey light of early morning Andrews did not question. He knew very well the unwisdom of questioning it﻿—half indeed he was aware in what place he lay. I will play a little longer, he thought, and turned over and lay with his face to the wall, so that he might not notice the unfamiliar details of the room, shed, whatever it might be. Then he shut his eyes, because the wall he faced was stone and it should have been wood.

With his eyes shut all was well. He sniffed the warm scent of the mould comfortingly.

===Chinese===
他缓缓地从一片正在消散的灰色雾霭中浮出，雾上布满参差的裂口。那些裂口朝着他的视界生长，变成了箱子、旧衣箱、积灰的杂物。他发觉自己躺在一堆麻袋上，屋里有一股陈腐的泥土霉味。一垛园艺家什靠着墙立着，一只翻放着、没了盖子的大木箱里装满干瘪的球根。起初他以为自己是待在自家那间育苗棚里。外头该有一片草坪和一棵高高的松树，过一会儿他就会听见园丁拖沓的脚步声。老人总把左脚往后拖着走，脚步便没有固定的节律，只能像数夜枭的啼声那样数——一声，两呜——一声，两呜。至于他怎么会清早天色灰白时躺在这育苗棚里，安德鲁斯并没有追问。他很清楚追问是不智的——其实他有一半明白自己躺在什么地方。我再多玩一会儿，他想，随即翻过身来，脸朝着墙躺下，好不去留意这房间——棚子，随它是什么——里那些陌生的细处。接着他闭上了眼睛，因为他面对的墙是石头砌的，而它本该是木头的。

闭着眼睛，一切安好。他把泥土温热的气息深深吸进去，觉得安慰。

===Original===
The old man would grumble at his presence, complain that he had shifted a hoe, a spade, a fork. Then as certainly as night closed day he would take up a box lid full of seeds, rattle the seeds back and forth with a noise like small, quick hailstones and murmur, “Winkle dust.” Andrews screwed his eyes tighter, sniffed deeper. He remembered how the old man had been standing once beneath the pine at the end of the lawn. He was feeling his chin thoughtfully and staring up at the tapering dark slimness above him. “Three hundred years,” he was saying slowly to himself, “three hundred years.” Andrews had commented on the sweet elusive smell that came sifting through the air. “That’s age,” said the old man, “that’s age.” He spoke with such conviction that Andrews half expected to see him vanish himself into a faint perfume formed out of bulbs and damp turned earth. “They make coffins out of pines,” the old man continued, “coffins, that’s why you get the smell sometimes where there ain’t no pines. Up through the ground you see.”

===Chinese===
老人总要对他在场发几句牢骚，抱怨他挪动了锄头、铁锹、草叉。然后，像黑夜必然合上白昼一样，他会端起一只盛满种子的箱盖，把种子哗啦哗啦地来回摇，像一阵又小又急的冰雹，口中喃喃：“海螺粉。”安德鲁斯把眼睛闭得更紧，往深里嗅去。他记起有一回老人站在草坪尽头那棵松树底下，若有所思地捻着下巴，仰望头顶那渐渐收细的暗色树身。“三百年哪，”他缓缓地自言自语，“三百年哪。”安德鲁斯曾说起空气里筛下来的那股甜润而难以捉摸的气味。“那是年岁，”老人说，“那是年岁。”他说得那样笃定，安德鲁斯几乎指望看着他化作一缕幽香消散，那香气由球根和翻掘过的湿土酿成。“松木是打棺材的，”老人接着说，“棺材，所以有些地方明明没有松树，你也会闻到这股味儿。从地底下透上来的，你瞧。”

===Original===
The thought of coffins jerked Andrews’s eyes open. He saw again the candle fall and the bearded face looking up at him. It was sheer chance that he had not placed his hand full on that dead stubble. Three years swept past; the present scratched at his nerves. He jumped up and looked round. How long had he slept? What had that girl been doing in the meanwhile? He had been a weak fool to collapse and a sentimental fool to dream into the past. The present called for brisk action if he was to bring himself to safe haven, but remembering all the circumstances of the last few weeks, he wondered with a sick lurch at the heart whether there was any haven to which Carlyon would not penetrate.

In the wall opposite was a window cobwebbed and dusty. By piling two of the boxes together he was able to reach it, and he calculated that he could just squeeze his body through the opening. He was afraid to break the glass, because of the noise the act would cause, and his fingers felt cautiously and timidly at the catch, which was almost welded to its position with the rust of many years. He began to pick at the rust with his nails and by small fractions of an inch he was able to move the catch. The tiny noises he made fretted at his nerves and the very need of caution made him careless. He stood on tiptoe, partly with the excitement and a restlessness to be gone, partly that he might get a better purchase on the terribly reluctant catch. With a long drawn out squeak it twisted and left the window free; at the same moment the noise of a door-handle turning swung him round. He had hardly noticed the door of the room, so certain had he been that it was locked, until now when it opened and the girl stood there. Andrews felt acutely ridiculous balanced upon his boxes. Carefully and slowly, with his eyes fixed on her, he stepped down.

===Chinese===
棺材的念头猛地把安德鲁斯的眼睛拽开了。他又看见那支蜡烛跌落，看见那张仰面望着他的络腮胡脸。他的手没有整个按上那片死人的胡茬，纯属侥幸。三年光阴一扫而过；现在擦着他的神经。他跳起来四下张望。自己睡了多久？这工夫那个姑娘都在干什么？昏倒在地，他是懦弱的蠢货；梦回过去，他是感伤的蠢货。眼下若想把自己带到安全的避风港，就得雷厉风行；可一想起最近这几个星期的一切遭遇，他心里发呕似的一沉，疑惑到底有没有哪处避风港是卡莱昂钻不进去的。

对面的墙上有一扇窗，结满蛛网，蒙着灰。他把两只箱子摞起来，勉强够得着，估摸着身子能从窗口挤出去。他不敢砸玻璃，怕那动静惹祸，手指便怯生生地、小心翼翼地去摸窗闩——经年累月的锈把它几乎焊死在原处。他用指甲抠锈，一寸一寸地蹭着零头，总算把窗闩挪动了。弄出的细碎声响磨着他的神经，而恰恰是必须谨慎这桩事本身让他毛糙起来。他踮起脚尖，一半出于兴奋和急于脱身的焦躁，一半是想对那死活不肯松口的窗闩使上更大的劲。窗闩“吱——”地长吟一声，扭转开来，窗户松了；就在同一刻，门把手转动的声音让他猛地回身。那扇门他几乎没留意过——他满有把握它是锁着的——直到此刻门开了，姑娘站在那里。安德鲁斯高踞箱顶，觉得自己狼狈透顶。他双眼盯住她，小心而缓慢地爬了下来。

===Original===
She laughed, but without amusement. “What were you doing up there?” she asked. He felt furious with her at finding him in so ignominious a position.

“I was trying to escape,” he said.

“Escape?” she turned the word over on her lips as though it were a novel taste. “If you mean you wanted to go,” she said, “there was the door, wasn’t there?”

“Yes, and you with the gun,” he snapped back.

“Oh, that gun,” she laughed again, not scornfully this time but with a real merriment. “I haven’t an idea how to load it.”

===Chinese===
她笑了，却没有笑意。“你在上头干什么？”她问。被她撞见自己这副丢人现眼的架势，他恼羞成怒。

“我想逃出去，”他说。

“逃出去？”她把这个词搁在唇边翻来覆去，仿佛在尝什么新奇的味道。“你要是说想走，”她说，“门就在那儿，不是吗？”

“是啊，还有你手里那把枪，”他顶了回去。

“哦，那把枪啊，”她又笑了，这回不是讥诮，是真的乐了。“我压根不知道怎么装子弹。”

===Original===
He took a few steps towards her and looked less at her than at the open doorway behind her, which led, he saw, into the room of last night’s humiliation. He was certain that she was bluffing. She must have more than a coffin and a dead man in that room to give her the courage to face him so calmly﻿—so impudently he styled it to himself. So he advanced a little way, widening his vision of that room beyond.

“You mean that I can go?” he asked.

“I wouldn’t stop you,” she said. A note of anger was struggling with amusement in her voice and at last amusement won. “I didn’t invite you for the night.”

“Don’t talk so much,” he said angrily and flushed a little when she asked if he were listening for something. For he was listening intently and thought for a moment that he heard the squeak of a board and then again a man’s breathing. But he could not be sure. Suppose she had gone out during the night and found Carlyon﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
他朝她走了几步，眼睛倒不大看她，而是看她身后那扇敞开的门——他看得出来，门后就是昨晚让他受尽屈辱的那间屋子。他断定她是在虚张声势。那间屋里必定不止一口棺材和一个死人，她才敢这样沉着地面对他——用他心里给这沉着起的名字，叫厚颜无耻。于是他又朝前挪了挪，把那间屋更深处的景象多看进来一些。

“你是说，我可以走了？”他问。

“我没拦你的意思，”她说。她话音里恼意与逗趣较着劲，末了逗趣占了上风。“今晚可没人请你来过夜。”

“少说风凉话，”他没好气地说；她问他是不是在偷听什么动静，他微微涨红了脸。因为他确实在凝神细听，恍惚听见木板吱呀一响，接着又是一声男人的呼吸。可他不敢肯定。万一她夜里出去搬来了卡莱昂……

===Original===
“Look here,” he cried, unable to bear the suspense longer, “what have you done?”

“Done?” she said. “Done?” He watched her suspiciously, hating that habit she had of turning words over like a pancake, first this side, then that.

“Who have you fetched while I’ve been sleeping? I know your sort.”

===Chinese===
“你听着，”他喊道，再也受不了这份悬心，“你到底干了什么？”

“干了什么？”她说。“干了什么？”他满腹狐疑地盯着她，恨透了她的这个习惯——把词句当煎饼似的翻来覆去，先翻这面，再翻那面。

“我睡觉这会儿，你招了谁来？你们这路人我清楚。”

===Original===
“You are a man, aren’t you?” she said with sudden vehemence and was met with a purely mechanical leer and response. “Do you want me to prove it?” It was as though the young man’s face were a mask to which small strings were attached. She had pulled one and the mouth had opened and the lips had twisted a little at one corner. She felt a brief wonder as to what string would work the eyes that remained watching her suspiciously, a little frightened, completely unresponsive to the lips. Andrews himself was not unaware of those strings that put his speech and mouth in servitude to others. Always a little too late he would try to recall his words, not through any shame in their purport﻿—it would have been the same if they had been spoken in poetry, but because they had been dictated by another. So now that consciousness coming again a little too late made him try to cover up his previous words by others angrily spoken. “What do you mean anyway?”

===Chinese===
“你是个男人，对吧？”她突然激烈地说，得到的却是一种纯属机械的斜睨和回嘴。“要我证明给你看吗？”仿佛这张年轻的脸是一具系着几根细弦的面具。她拉动其中一根，嘴便张开了，嘴角也微微扭了一下。她一时好奇，不知哪一根弦才能操纵那双眼睛——那双眼睛仍在狐疑地盯着她，带点惊慌，对嘴唇的把戏全然无动于衷。安德鲁斯自己也并非不知道那些弦——它们把他的言语和嘴巴变成供他人驱使的奴隶。他总是慢半拍才想收回自己说的话，倒不是为话里的意思害臊——就算那些话是用诗吟出来的，他也一样要收回——而是因为它们是受别人支配才出口的。此刻，那意识又慢半拍地醒了过来，逼他气冲冲地另说一套，去遮掩先前的话。“你究竟什么意思？”

===Original===
“Do you think,” she said, “a man ever knows a woman’s sort? If I believed that,” she added, “I’d﻿ ﻿…” she looked at him with an amazed stare almost as though he had spoken the words. “You can go,” she added, “there’s no one to stop you. Why should I want you to stay?”

===Chinese===
“你以为，”她说，“男人真弄得清女人是什么路数？我要是信这个，”她又说，“我早就……”她惊愕地瞪着他，几乎倒像这话是他说的。“你可以走了，”她补上一句，“没有人拦你。我干嘛要留你？”

===Original===
That’s all very well, he thought. Is it bluff? The girl has plenty of nerve. It seemed unlikely after the way he had broken in the night before that she should not have tried to communicate with someone. And the whole neighbourhood just at present was riddled with runners and revenue men. He was uncertain how he stood with them, and he had no trust, like Carlyon, in his own elusiveness. However, she said he could go and she stood there waiting. What a devil the woman was﻿—forcing him to make a move. He no longer wanted to escape and stumble blindly into an unknown countryside. He wanted to lie down with his face to the wall and drowse. But she was waiting and waiting and he had to move. He moved slowly and softly towards the door, treading suspiciously like a cat in a strange house. When he reached the doorway he flung back the door as far as it would go lest anyone should be hiding behind it, ready to pounce on his turned back. Behind him he heard a laugh and swung round again. He felt tired and harassed and in no mood for mockery. A wave of self-pity passed across his mind and he saw himself friendless and alone, chased by harsh enemies through an uninterested world. Sympathy is all I want, he said to himself. Old white-haired women with kind wrinkled eyes stooped towards him, large laps and comfortable breasts mocked him with their absence. Little pricking tears rose to his eyes. I know I am a coward and altogether despicable, he said to himself with heavy self-depreciation, trying without much hope to underbid his real character, I know I haven’t an ounce of courage, that if Carlyon appeared now I’d go down on my knees to him, but all I want is a little sympathy. I could be made into a man if anyone chose to be interested﻿—if someone believed in me﻿ ﻿… But here his other self took a hand. He was, he knew, embarrassingly made up of two persons, the sentimental, bullying, desiring child and another more stern critic. If someone believed in me﻿—but he did not believe in himself. Always while one part of him spoke, another part stood on one side and wondered, “Is this I who am speaking? Can I really exist like this?”

===Chinese===
话说得倒轻巧，他想。是虚张声势吗？这姑娘胆子不小。昨晚他是那样破门闯进来的，她却竟没有试着去给什么人报信，这似乎说不通。何况眼下左近一带满是走私贩子和缉私官员，密得像筛子。他吃不准自己在他们眼里是何等角色，也没有卡莱昂那种对自己神出鬼没的自信。可是，她说他可以走，她就站在那里等着。这女人真是个魔头——逼得他非动身不可。他已经不想逃了，不想两眼一抹黑地栽进陌生的乡野。他只想脸朝着墙躺下，打个盹。可她等着，等着，他只得挪动。他缓缓地、轻轻地朝门口蹭，像生人家里的猫，一步一疑心。到了门边，他把门朝后甩到尽头，生怕门后藏着人，好扑向他的后背。身后传来一声笑，他又猛地回身。他又累又烦，没心思受人嘲弄。一阵自怜漫过心头，他看见自己孤苦伶仃、举目无亲，在一片冷漠的世界上被凶恶的仇敌追赶。我要的不过是同情，他自忖。那些白了头发、眼神慈祥、满脸皱纹的老妇人俯下身来，宽大的膝头、温暖的胸脯，都以它们的缺席嘲弄着他。细小的、扎人的泪珠涌上眼眶。我知道自己是个懦夫，卑鄙透顶，他重重地自贬着，没什么指望地想把真实的品性往低处报一报；我知道自己一两勇气也没有，这会儿卡莱昂要是出现，我马上就会给他下跪；可我要的只是一点同情。只要有人肯对我上心——只要有人信得过我……我就能被造就成一个真正的男人。可是说到这里，他身上那另一个自我插了手。他知道自己难堪地由两个人拼成：一个感伤、蛮横、欲壑难填的孩子，和另一个更冷峻的批评者。只要有人信得过我——可他并不信得过自己。他身上总是一部分在说话，另一部分就站在一旁纳闷：“说话的这个是我吗？我真的能这样存在吗？”

===Original===
“It’s easy for you to laugh,” he said bitterly. But am I really bitter, the other part wondered. Am I play acting still? And if I am play acting, is it I who act or another who pulls the strings? But what a Pharisee the other part of him was. It never took control of his mouth and spoke its own words﻿—hard, real, trustworthy. It only stood on one side and listened and taunted and questioned. So now it let his voice go on, genuine or play acting or dictated. “You don’t know what it feels like to be alone.” Watching the face that still smiled at him, not with hostility but with almost a friendly mocking, he became frightened at an unintended reality in his own words. He was indeed alone. Perhaps that other part of him remained silent, not through self-righteousness, but because it had no words to speak. There was nothing in him but sentimentality and fear and cowardice, nothing in him but negatives. How could anyone believe in him if he did not even exist?

===Chinese===
“你倒笑得轻松，”他辛酸地说。可我真的辛酸吗，那另一个自我纳闷。我还在演戏吗？若是在演戏，登台的是我，还是另有牵线的人？可他身上那另一个自我，又是个多么道貌岸然的法利赛人。它从不去管住他的嘴、亲口说出自己的话——坚硬的、实在的、可信的话。它只是站在一旁听着、讥诮着、盘问着。此刻它便放任他的声音继续下去，是真心、是做戏、还是受人摆布，随它去。“你不知道孤零零一个人是什么滋味。”他望着那张仍在冲他微笑的脸——不是怀着敌意，倒近乎友好的揶揄——被自己话音里那点无心的真实吓着了。他的确是孤零零一个人。也许他身上那另一个自我缄口不语，并非出于自义，而是因为它无话可说。他内里除了感伤、恐惧和怯懦，什么也没有；除了否定，什么也没有。倘若他压根不存在，又怎能指望谁信得过他？

===Original===
He was surprised, deep in the maze in which he chased himself, when she answered him. “I’ve been alone, too, the last two nights. I don’t mind the daytime, but I get a bit scared at night now he’s dead.” She nodded her head towards the room on the threshold of which he stood.

He looked across the room. The coffin still lay upon the kitchen table. The candles were no longer alight, but drooped in weary attitudes of self-depreciation.

“Husband?” he asked. She shook her head.

“Father?”

“Not exactly. He brought me up though. I can’t remember my father. I was fond of him,” she nodded her head again. “He was kind to me in his way. It’s a bit frightening being alone.”

===Chinese===
他正深深陷在自己追逐自己的迷宫里，不想她竟答了话。“前两夜我也是一个人。白天我不怕，可如今他死了，夜里我有点儿发怵。”她朝他正站在门槛上的那间屋子里点了点头。

他朝那间屋子望去。棺材还搁在厨房的桌子上。蜡烛已经熄了，蔫头耷脑，一副疲惫的自贬姿态。

“丈夫？”他问。她摇了摇头。

“父亲？”

“不算。不过是他把我带大的。我记不得我父亲了。我挺喜欢他，”她又点了点头。“他按他自己的那套待我好。一个人待着，怪叫人害怕的。”

===Original===
It was as though she had forgotten the circumstances of Andrews’s coming. They faced each other. She also seemed alone in a somewhat dark wood. She also was frightened she said, but there was a courage that added to Andrews’s shame in the candid hand she appeared to stretch through the dark to his companionship.

“It will be worse tonight,” she said. “I have to bury him today.”

“I should have thought,” Andrews answered, remembering the stubble on which he had nearly placed his hand, “it would be less﻿—frightening without a body in the house.”

“Oh no,” she said, “oh no,” looking at him with puzzled eyes. “I wouldn’t be afraid of him.” She came and stood in the doorway beside Andrews and looked across at the lidless coffin. “He must be terribly alone,” she said, “but there’s the peace of God in his face. Come and look,” she stepped across the room, and very reluctantly Andrews followed.

===Chinese===
她仿佛忘了安德鲁斯是怎么闯到这儿来的。两人面对面站着。她也像是独自迷失在一片幽暗的林子里。她说她也害怕，可她像是从黑暗中朝他伸来一只坦率的手，邀他与自己作伴；那份坦荡的勇气，只叫安德鲁斯愈发羞惭。

“今晚更难熬，”她说。“我今天得把他下葬。”

“我倒觉得，”安德鲁斯答道，想起自己险些按上去的那片死人胡茬，“屋里没有尸首，反倒不那么——吓人了吧。”

“哦，不，”她说，“哦，不，”用迷惑不解的眼睛望着他。“我不会怕他的。”她走过来，站在安德鲁斯身旁的门口，朝那具没有盖的棺材望过去。“他一定孤单得厉害，”她说，“可他脸上有上帝的平安。来看看吧。”她穿过屋子走过去，安德鲁斯万般不情愿地跟了上去。

===Original===
He could see little of the peace of which the girl had spoken in the face. The eyes were closed, and he had a sense, drawn from the coarse strong skin of the lids, that they must have been hard to shut. At any moment he felt the strain might become too great and the lids would turn up with a sudden click like a roller blind. Round the mouth were little cunning wrinkles that prowled outwards across the face in stealthy radiations. He looked at the girl to see if she were mocking him in her talk of God in connection with this bearded rapscallion, but she was looking down at the body with calm and passionless affection. He had a sudden inclination to say to her, “It’s you who have the peace of God, not he,” but refrained. It would sound melodramatic and she would laugh at him the more. It was only to suit his own ends or his own self-pity that he allowed himself the pleasure of melodrama.

It was while he was regarding the face and the triumphant cunning of the lines, growingly conscious at the same time of the girl’s fixity of thought, like a firm comforting wall beside his own shifting waters, that he heard faint stumbling steps. It was fear that made his ears sufficiently acute, the girl behind him had not moved. He twisted his eyes up from the dead man and faced her again.

===Chinese===
姑娘说的那种平安，他在那张脸上几乎看不出什么。眼睛是闭着的，可他从眼皮那粗硬厚实的皮肤得出一种感觉：这双眼睛生前必定很不容易闭上。他随时觉得那绷着的劲儿一支持不住，眼皮就会像卷帘似的“咔哒”一声猛地翻起来。嘴的四周绕着一圈狡黠的小皱纹，暗暗地、一折一折地在脸上向四周辐射开去。他瞅了瞅姑娘，看她把上帝扯到这么一个满脸胡须的无赖身上，是不是在耍笑他；可她正垂目望着遗体，那份爱怜平静而不带一丝波澜。他忽然想说：“拥有上帝平安的是你，不是他。”话到嘴边又咽了回去。那话说出来太像戏文，只会叫她更笑话他。也只有为了自己的私心，或是为了顾影自怜，他才容自己享用这种闹剧的滋味。

他正端详着那张脸和那些线条里得胜的狡黠，同时又越来越觉出姑娘心神的凝定——像一堵坚实而宽慰人的墙，立在他自己这潭摇晃的流水旁边——就在这时，他听见一阵隐隐约约、磕磕绊绊的脚步声。是恐惧把他的耳朵磨得这样尖；身后的姑娘一动未动。他把目光从死人脸上拧开，重新面对着她。

===Original===
“So you’ve been keeping me here?” he said. He was only half aware of the foolishness of his accusation. One reasoning part of him told him that he had been with her since he woke at most a matter of minutes, but reason somehow had seemed lacking in this house since he had entered it and seen what should have been a frightened girl holding him with a calm gun between the yellow-tipped candles. And since he had come to consciousness again five or ten minutes before, he had lived over again a boy’s life in Devon and had stood, he told himself with a sudden rush of sentiment, between the cunning and yet clumsy earth and the purposeful courage of the spirit. These experiences could not be confined to the small measure of minutes, and so with a sense of real grievance he accused her. “You’ve been keeping me here?”

“Keeping you?” she said. “What do you mean?” Suddenly the footsteps which had been very faint grew distinct in a stone shifting. Andrews’s mind pierced its maze of vague thinking in a flash of fear, and he half ran across the room to the door through which he had entered the night before. A sense of overwhelming desolation passed over him, a wonder whether he would ever know peace from pursuit, and he gave an unconscious whimper like a rabbit snared. The reality of the sound seemed to acquaint the girl with the measure of his fear.

===Chinese===
“原来你一直把我扣在这儿？”他说。他只模模糊糊觉出这指控有多荒唐。他身上讲道理的那部分告诉他，从醒来到现在他同她在一起至多不过几分钟的事；可打从他跨进这座房子、看见本该惊慌失措的姑娘却在黄尖烛光之间用一把沉着的枪制住他那时起，理性在这屋里不知怎么就使不上劲了。再说，自从五分钟或十分钟之前他重新有了知觉，他把一个德文郡男孩的岁月重活了一遍，还——他对自己说，一阵感伤陡然涌起——站在了狡黠而笨拙的大地与那怀着目标的精神勇气之间。这些经历无论如何装不进短短几分钟的刻度里。于是他满怀真切的不平，指控起她来。“你一直把我扣在这儿？”

“扣着你？”她说，“什么意思？”突然，那原本极轻的脚步声随着一块石头的挪动变得清晰起来。安德鲁斯的脑子在一阵恐惧的闪光中刺破了迷雾般的胡思乱想，他几乎是跑着穿过屋子，奔向他昨晚进来时走的那扇门。一阵压倒一切的荒凉漫过他全身，他疑心自己此生还能不能从追逐中得一日安宁，喉咙里不由自主地滚出一声呜咽，像落网的兔子。这声音的真切，倒让姑娘掂出了他恐惧的分量。

===Original===
“Don’t go out there,” she called to him.

He hesitated with his hand upon the latch. The girl was feeling her cheek with the tips of her fingers. “It’s only the woman come to tidy the place,” she said.

“I mustn’t meet her,” Andrews whispered, afraid that their voices would reach the path outside.

“If you go out there,” the girl said, “you’ll meet her. She’ll be coming from the well now. Better go back where you slept,” and then as he moved across the room, “no.” A slow flush crept from her neck across her face.

===Chinese===
“别往那边出去，”她朝他喊。

他的手搭在门闩上，迟疑起来。姑娘正用指尖摩挲着自己的面颊。“只是来收拾屋子的那个女人，”她说。

“我不能撞见她，”安德鲁斯压低了嗓子说，生怕他们的声音传到外面的小径上。

“你从那边出去，”姑娘说，“就正撞上她。她这会儿正从井边过来。还是回你睡觉的地方去吧。”他正要穿过屋子回去，她却说：“不。”一股红晕缓缓地从她脖子漫上面颊。

===Original===
“What’s the matter now?” he asked angrily.

“If she discovers you﻿—hiding﻿—she’ll think﻿—”

“God, you’re respectable,” he said with resentful amazement. It was as though the calm spirit that had watched the dead man had become tarnished by the latter’s own earthy cunning. Some yellow sunlight, clear and cold with frost, struck across the room from the window and fell across her face, belying the dull good sense of the words she spoke.

“No, but you can’t,” she said. “It’s not as if you were in any danger.”

===Chinese===
“又怎么了？”他没好气地问。

“她要是发现你——躲在这儿——她会以为——”

“老天，你可真正经，”他又惊又恼地说。仿佛方才凝望死人时的那份平静心神，也让死者自己那股土里土气的狡黠熏暗了。一抹黄色的阳光，霜冻般清澈而寒冷，从窗口斜切进屋，落在她脸上，与她这话里那股板正得近乎呆板的常识味儿对不上号。

“不行，可你不能，”她说。“又不是说你当真有什么危险。”

===Original===
He came close to her and put his hands upon her arms and pulled her close to him. “Listen to me,” he said, “I am in danger. I’d rather kill that old woman whoever she is than be talked about in Shoreham. I’m a coward, do you see, and it would be easier to kill her than the man who’d be after me. Now will you let me hide?” He loosened her and she pushed herself away from him. “There must be some other way,” she said. She suddenly began to speak rapidly. “You are my brother, do you see? You arrived last week hearing that he was dying because you didn’t like me to be alone.” She grimaced a little as though at a bad taste.

A splash of water from an overfull pail interrupted her. Footsteps sounded almost at the threshold of the door. “You must invent things,” she said. “What more is there? I must have forgotten﻿—”

===Chinese===
他凑近她，两手抓住她的胳膊，把她往自己跟前带。“听我说，”他说，“我有危险。我宁可杀了那个老太婆——管她是谁——也不愿在肖勒姆让人嚼舌头。我是个懦夫，你明白吗，杀了她，总比杀那个追来的人容易。这一回，你肯让我藏起来了吧？”他一松手，她便挣开身子。“总还有别的法子，”她说。忽然她飞快地讲起来：“你是我哥哥，明白吗？上礼拜你听说他快不行了，不放心我一个人，便赶了来。”说完她微微做了个怪相，像被什么怪口味到似的。

一只漫出桶沿的水桶泼出一串水声，打断了她。脚步声几乎已经响在门槛上。“你得跟着编，”她说，“还有什么？我准是漏了什么——”

===Original===
“What shall I call you? Your name?” Andrews whispered rapidly, as with a high squeak the latch of the door rose.

“Elizabeth,” she said, “Elizabeth.”

The door opened and it seemed incongruous after the panic that the footsteps had evoked to see only an old woman with a pail of water which went slip slop over the brim and splashed upon the floor. She was a little stout old woman who gave the impression of being very tightly pulled together by a great number of buttons that strayed from their normal positions and peeped out from interstices and side turnings in her voluminous clothes. She had small eyes and very faint, almost indistinguishable eyebrows. Her hair was some of it white and some of it grey and through it wandered stray strands of a very pale metallic gold which looked unnecessarily flippant on an old head. When she saw Andrews standing by the girl she put down the pail on the floor and preened her mouth to whistle until it appeared but one more addition to her collection of buttons. She did not actually whistle but hovered delicately upon the point, while her eyes, which changed from surprise to questioning and last to a somewhat sly amusement, seemed to whistle instead. Under her unembarrassed amused stare Andrews fidgeted and longed for his companion to speak.

At last the old woman, waiting no longer for an invitation, entered. Her eyes having taken in the pair of them were no longer interested. She placed the pail down on the stone floor and then with an old and very dirty rag began to scrub. She had cleaned but a small space when she found it necessary to pull aside the table on which the coffin lay, and this she did with a complete and to Andrews an amazing unconcern. Her eyes had taken in all they desired to see, but her thoughts remained amused. She suddenly chuckled and hastily beat the water in her pail and coughed to hide the sound.

===Chinese===
“我该怎么称呼你？你叫什么名字？”安德鲁斯飞快地低声问，门闩正“吱”地一声高高抬起。

“伊丽莎白，”她说，“伊丽莎白。”

门开了。方才那阵脚步声搅起的惊慌犹在，此刻进来的却只是一个提着水桶的老太婆，叫人觉得不甚相称；桶里的水漫过桶沿，噗嗤噗嗤泼洒在地板上。这是个矮墩墩的老太婆，浑身像被密密麻麻的一大把纽扣紧紧收拢在一起：那些纽扣东一颗西一颗，早离了本该在的位置，从宽大衣裳的夹缝里、岔道口上探头探脑。她眼睛细小，眉毛淡得几乎没有。头发半白半灰，其间穿插着几缕游丝般的淡金色发丝，闪着金属的光——长在一颗老脑袋上，未免轻佻得多余。她一见安德鲁斯站在姑娘身边，就把水桶往地板上一放，把嘴噘起来预备吹口哨，噘得那嘴也成了她纽扣收藏里的又一枚。口哨倒没真吹出来，只在那一点上精致地悬着；倒是她那双眼睛——由吃惊转为盘问，再转为几分狡黠的乐呵——仿佛替她把口哨吹了。在她这毫无顾忌、含着笑意的逼视下，安德鲁斯如坐针毡，只盼他的同伴快开口。

老太婆终于不等邀请了，自己走了进来。那双眼睛把两人扫了一遍，便再无兴趣。她把水桶放在石板地上，拿起一块又旧又脏的抹布擦起地来。才擦了巴掌大一块，她就觉得非把那张搁着棺材的桌子挪开不可，挪的时候神色自若，在安德鲁斯看来简直不可思议。她的眼睛早把她想看的都看够了，心里的念头却还乐着。她忽然噗嗤一笑，慌忙在桶里拍打水花，又咳了几声遮掩过去。

===Original===
The girl smiled towards Andrews and, with a small pout of the lips that said quite plainly, “now for it,” spoke. “This is my brother, Mrs. Butler,” she said.

The voice that came from the figure kneeling on the floor was startingly unexpected. It consorted, not with the white or the grey hairs, but with the too metallic yellow strands. It was soft, almost young, just avoided beauty. It was like a pretty sweet cake that had been soaked in port wine. It would have been lovely, if it had had the certainty of loveliness, but it was damped all through.

“Well now, I didn’t know that you had a brother, Miss Elizabeth,” it said.

===Chinese===
姑娘朝安德鲁斯一笑，嘴唇轻轻一撅，明明白白说的是“好戏开场”，然后开了口。“这是我哥哥，巴特勒夫人。”她说。

从跪在地板上的人影口里出来的嗓音，叫人大出意料。它与那白发灰发毫不相配，倒与那几缕金属光太重的黄发是一路的。嗓音轻柔，几乎称得上年轻，离美只差一步。像一块浸足了波特酒的香甜蛋糕；倘若它对自己的可爱有几分把握，原本是很动人的——可惜它浑身上下都是潮的。

“哎哟，这么说，我从前竟不知道你还有位哥哥呢，伊丽莎白小姐。”那嗓音说。

===Original===
“He came a week ago when he heard Mr. Jennings was dying,” the girl went on.

“And so a brother should.” The old woman wrung out her cloth into the pail and sat back unexpectedly on her heels. Her eyes were not soft like her voice but as sharp as they were small. Both Andrews and the girl became conscious of their stiff, unreal attitudes, standing a little apart from each other waiting for nothing. “You had all the looks in your family, Miss Elizabeth,” Mrs. Butler said. “Your brother doesn’t look very strong﻿—or perhaps he’s tired.” A giggle began to form like a soap bubble in either eye. It grew under almost visible constraint, until at last she let it loose to bound gaily round the room. Then she soaked her cloth again and began to scrub furiously as though she would daunt that spirit of rude flippancy. “And what’s your name, sir, if you don’t think me rude?”

===Chinese===
“他一礼拜前就来了，听说詹宁斯先生快不行了。”姑娘接着说。

“做哥哥的本来就该这样。”老太婆把抹布在桶里拧干，出其不意地往后一坐，跪坐在自己的脚跟上。她的眼睛可不像嗓音那样软，又小又尖。安德鲁斯和姑娘这才觉出两人那僵硬做作的姿态——彼此隔着一点距离站着，守着一场空等待。“你们一家的好模样全叫你一个人占了，伊丽莎白小姐，”巴特勒夫人说，“你哥哥瞧着不怎么结实——要么就是乏了。”两只眼睛里各自冒起一个咯咯的笑，像起了肥皂泡；那笑在几乎看得见的克制下越长越大，末了她一撒手，任它满屋子欢蹦乱跳。随后她又浸湿抹布，没命地擦起地板来，仿佛要把那股放肆轻佻的精灵慑服下去。“先生贵姓？别嫌我冒昧。”

===Original===
“Why, the same as my sister’s,” Andrews replied, trying to sound amused and at his ease.

“I meant your Christian name, sir?” she went on, making rapid progress across the floor.

“Oh, Francis, of course. Hasn’t my sister spoken of me?” In the space between a sentence and a sentence, he had had time to watch the sunlight mould the girl’s face, give lightness to its somewhat heavy lines, smooth its perplexity into peace. A dark Elizabeth, he thought, watching her hair, how strange. He began to enjoy himself, the burden of his fear had dropped away and left him in the middle of a childish game in which there was no harsh reality. “Elizabeth,” he said, “have you never spoken of me to Mrs. Butler? I take it very ill, I really do. And I away at sea imagining that you thought of me.”

“Why, are you a sailor, sir?” Mrs. Butler said, not troubling to raise her eyes from the arc of floor in which her small fat arms swung. “I shouldn’t have thought it.”

“But then I’m a bad sailor,” he continued, his eyes on the sunlight or that part of it that lay across Elizabeth’s face. He was determined to make her smile. “When I heard how﻿—he was dying, I left my ship. I thought my sister would want someone else besides you to protect her. You can’t imagine, Mrs. Butler, how often I’ve read of you under the stars.” He stopped. He had won his smile.

===Chinese===
“嗐，跟我妹妹一个姓，”安德鲁斯答道，竭力显得又风趣又自在。

“我问的是教名，先生？”她紧追不舍，抹布在地板上快速推进。

“哦，弗朗西斯，自然是弗朗西斯。我妹妹没跟你提起过我？”在一句话与一句话的空当里，他还有闲心看阳光在那姑娘脸上塑形，把她脸上偏重的线条调得轻快些，把眉间的惶惑熨成安宁。一个黑头发的伊丽莎白，他望着她的头发想，多奇怪。他开始自得其乐起来：恐惧的重负已经卸落，把他撂在一场孩子气的游戏中央，那里头没有半点严酷的现实。“伊丽莎白，”他说，“你就从没跟巴特勒夫人讲起过我？我可要见怪了，真的见怪。我还在海上漂着呢，一心当你心里惦记着我。”

“哟，先生原来是跑船的？”巴特勒夫人说，懒得把眼睛从她那小胖胳膊来回摆动的扇形地面上抬起来。“我可没瞧出来。”

“不过我是个不中用的水手，”他接着说，眼睛盯着那片阳光——或者说盯着落在伊丽莎白脸上的那一片。他打定主意要逗她一笑。“一听说他——快不行了，我就下了船。我想着妹妹身边除了你，总还得有个人照应。您想象不到，巴特勒夫人，我在星光底下读到过您多少回。”他停住了。他赢得了那一笑。

===Original===
And yet now that he had won his smile, he was ill at ease. It reminded him, perhaps, of all hopeless and unattainable things﻿—not desire then, for he was too weary for desire, but for civilisation. Civilisation meant for him the enjoyment of quiet﻿—gardens and unboisterous meals, music and the singing in Exeter Cathedral. These things were unattainable because of Carlyon. Of the others he had no fear. They could not, he felt, escape from their environment﻿—a rough, cursing, drinking life. He could escape from them in drawing rooms, but in the middle of however quiet a tea, however peaceful the lazy shadows of the fire, however soft the talk, the door might open, Carlyon enter.

Mrs. Butler cleaned on, her buttocks swaying rhythmically to the circular movement of her arms. He saw her suddenly as a hostile spy from reality, though it was not so that he would have phrased it. His fear was too sharp for abstractions. But unexpressed in conscious thought, he had felt of this house as of a cottage in a fairy-story. He had stumbled on it in a wood when blurred with sleep. It had given him shelter and a sense of mystery; it had not belonged to the world which he had known, the constant irritation and strain of the sea nor to the fear of the last few days. But Mrs. Butler had come from the town that morning. In crannies of her ears still lurked the sounds from which he had fled, the waves, fishwives’ voices, rattle of carts, “Mackerel, fresh mackerel,” gossip in the market, “Three of them escaped.”

Mrs. Butler had left the door open and through it he could see clearly in sunlight that which, when he came, had been obscured by weariness and night. He had thought of this cottage as alone in the middle of a wood. Now he could see that it stood at the edge of a mere coppice. Above the trees like a blister was the down over which he had come. “What’s that?” he said at a sound, unable to keep all sign of fear from his voice.

===Chinese===
然而赢得了这一笑，他反倒不自在了。那笑让他想起的，也许是一切无望而不可企及的东西——那不是欲念，欲念他早已疲惫得无从生起，而是一种对文明的思慕。文明之于他，意味着安然享受清静——花园，安安静静的家常饭，音乐，还有埃克塞特大教堂里的唱诗。这些东西可望不可即，都因为卡莱昂。其余那些人，他并不怕。他觉得他们逃不出自己的天地——那种粗野、咒骂、酗酒的生活。他躲进客厅就能躲开他们；可无论多清静的茶会，无论炉火的懒影多么宁帖，无论谈话多么轻柔，门都可能推开，卡莱昂走进来。

巴特勒夫人擦个不停，肥臀随着两臂的环形运动一摇一摆，颇有节律。他忽然把她看成一个从现实派来的敌探——他自然不会用这样的话来措辞；他的恐惧太锋利，容不下抽象。只是这话虽未在自觉的思绪里成形，他心里一直把这座房子当作童话里的小木屋：睡意昏沉之际，他在林中一头撞见了它。它给了他藏身之所和一种神秘感；它既不属于他所认识的那个世界——海上那种无休止的烦扰与紧绷——也不属于最近这几天的恐惧。可巴特勒夫人是当天早上从镇上下来的。她耳朵的缝隙里还潜伏着他逃离的那些声音：海浪，鱼市婆娘的吆喝，大车的轱辘声，“鲭鱼，新鲜的鲭鱼”，市场上的闲话，“三个人逃掉了”。

巴特勒夫人进来时没关门，透过门洞，在阳光下他看清了来时被疲惫和夜色遮住的一切。他原以为这座小屋孤零零地藏在林子深处；如今才看清，它不过坐落在小小一片灌木林的边上。树梢上头，像一只燎泡似的，鼓着他来时翻越的那片南唐斯丘陵。“那是什么响动？”他说，声音里到底没能藏住全部的惊慌。

===Original===
“Why, it’s only a cart,” the girl answered.

“A cart?” he cried and walked to a window. It was true. This cottage hidden, as he had thought, in a forest lay within a hundred yards of the high road. It was useless to tell himself that a high road was his safest place, that Carlyon, probably by now with a price upon his head, must equally fear the open. He was superstitious on the subject of Carlyon. He could not imagine Carlyon in hiding.

“A sailor?” said Mrs. Butler, her eyes fixed on the floor. “There’s sailors and sailors. There’s some as don’t like these gaugers, but I say as ’ow they be only doin’ their duty. They be paid for it same as me on this floor. And they get the worst of it most every time. Look at Tuesday.”

===Chinese===
“嗐，不过是一辆大车。”姑娘答道。

“大车？”他失声叫道，快步走到窗前。是真的。这座他以为藏在森林里的小屋，离大路不到一百码。跟自己说大路最安全、说卡莱昂——他这颗脑袋眼下多半已经悬了赏——必定同样害怕开阔地，都是白搭。在卡莱昂这件事上，他怀有一种迷信。他没法想象卡莱昂藏头露尾的模样。

“跑船的？”巴特勒夫人说，两眼仍盯着地板。“水手也有各式各样的水手哩。有人不喜欢这些缉私税吏，可依我说呀，人家不过是奉公办事，跟我趴在这块地板上一样，都是挣工钱的。况且每回吃亏的净是他们。瞧瞧礼拜二那回吧。”

===Original===
“What time’s this funeral?” Andrews asked, turning his back on Mrs. Butler with abrupt brutality. He was very conscious that behind his back she had raised an astonished head and was eyeing him with shrewd consideration. The girl he found had moved to the door and he followed her with a sense of relief, glad to leave behind, though only for a little, Mrs. Butler’s curiosity and her pretty, damp voice. “What time’s this funeral?” he repeated.

“They’ll be fetching him,” she said, “at eleven,” and her simple sentence cleared away the last illusion of isolation. Time was here in the cottage. Clocks ticked and hands went round as everywhere else in the world. He had a sense of time rushing past him, rushing like a Gadarene swine to destruction. Time squeaked at him as it passed at an increasing pace down a steep slope. Poets had told him over and over again that life was short. Now for the first time he knew it as a vital fact. He longed for peace and beauty, and the minutes were flying by, and he was still a fugitive, with mind muddled, obscured by fear of death.

===Chinese===
“这葬礼几点办？”安德鲁斯问道，突兀而近乎粗鲁地转过身去，把后背留给巴特勒夫人。他清清楚楚地觉出，背后的她惊讶地抬起头来，正用精明的眼光把他掂量。他发现姑娘已经走到门口，便跟了过去，心里一松——巴特勒夫人的好奇心也好，她那又甜美又发潮的嗓音也好，能撇下一会儿是一会儿。“这葬礼几点办？”他又问了一遍。

“他们来接他，”她说，“十一点钟。”她这句简简单单的话，扫除了与世隔绝的最后一点幻象。时间就在这小屋里。钟在嘀嗒，针在盘上转，同世上任何地方没有两样。他觉出时间正从身边飞驰而过，像格拉森的猪群（典出《圣经》，附魔的猪群冲崖投海）争先恐后地冲向毁灭。时间顺着陡坡越溜越快，一路尖啸着从他身旁擦过。诗人们翻来覆去地告诉他，人生苦短；直到此刻他才头一遭把它当成一条性命攸关的事实。他渴望安宁与美，分钟却在飞逝，而他依旧是个亡命者，脑子里一团糨糊，被对死亡的恐惧蒙蔽着。

===Original===
“Shall we be alone?” he asked, his voice a mixture of longing and apprehension.

“Alone,” she repeated in a low voice, so that her voice might not reach through the splashing of a damp cloth to Mrs. Butler’s ears. “No, we shan’t be alone. You don’t know these country people. I hate them,” she added with unexpected intensity. “This is a show to them. They’ll flock to it, but I shan’t feed them. They’ll expect to be fed. They haven’t been near me since he died, and I’d have welcomed anyone for a bit of company in the evening. They never came when he was alive.”

“What do you mean?” he raised his voice in unthinking fear. “A crowd of them?” He took hold of her wrist. “If you’ve planned this,” he said.

“Need you be a fool as well as a coward?” she answered in an offhand tired fashion. “Why should I plan anything? I’m not sufficiently interested in you.” She released her hand and moved out of doors. “I don’t know why I’ve helped you as much as I have,” she added with a small shrug.

===Chinese===
“到时候就咱们自己？”他问，声音里渴望与忧惧搅在一起。

“自己，”她低声重复了一遍，好让话音别穿过湿抹布的泼水声钻进巴特勒夫人的耳朵。“不，咱们清静不了。乡下人你怎么对付得了。我恨他们，”她添了一句，激烈得出乎意料。“在他们眼里这是一场戏。他们会成群结队地来看热闹，可我什么也不请他们吃。他们倒还指望吃一顿。他死了以后，没一个人走近过我；傍晚我原会欢迎任何人来作个伴。他活着的时候，他们可从来不登门。”

“你什么意思？”他不由自主，恐惧驱着抬高了嗓门。“会来一大群人？”他一把攥住她的手腕。“这要是你安排下的，”他说。

“你做了懦夫，还非得再做傻瓜不成？”她答道，有气无力，心不在焉。“我干嘛要安排什么？我对你还没多大兴趣。”她抽回手，走到门外去了。“我也不知道自己干嘛帮你帮到这个地步，”她微微一耸肩，又说。

===Original===
He followed still suspicious. He felt unreasonably grieved that this cottage was not the lonely woodland house, which he had imagined. “Don’t take credit for that,” he said. “I forced you to it.”

She did not look at him. Her hands were on her hips and she stared at the down over which he had come with a small wrinkle of perplexity. She seemed to be trying to puzzle out the reason behind her acts. “It was not fear,” she said, but it was not to him that she replied. “It would be a fool who’d be afraid of you,” she said and smiled as though at an amusing memory. “I suppose I was tired of being alone.”

===Chinese===
他跟上她，疑心未消。这座小屋竟不是他想象中那座林中孤屋，他没来由地为之怅然。“这功劳可别记在自己头上，”他说。“是我逼你的。”

她没有看他。两手叉着腰，望着他来时翻越的那道丘陵，眉心拧起一道小小的困惑的褶子。她像是想把自己这些举动背后的缘由琢磨个明白。“不是害怕，”她说，可她回答的并不是他。“怕你的才叫傻呢，”她说着笑了，像是想起一段好笑的往事。“我想，我不过是一个人待腻了。”

### III / 三

===Original===
“And though after my skin worms destroy this body, yet in my flesh shall I see God; whom I shall see for myself, and mine eyes shall behold, and not another.”

The priest was tall and thin and stooping and he suffered from a running cold. He snuffled between each phrase as he took long, loping strides through the graveyard. It was a raw day and he appeared anxious to get through with a dreary business. Between every phrase he snuffled and at the end of every sentence he gave a hasty furtive wipe at his nose with a corner of his surplice that blew out in the wind like a banner. He strode, not concealing his hatred of the cold, but those that followed after, a large straying band of intense villagers, walked as slowly as he allowed them, seemed almost to hold him back by the flapping end of his surplice. They refused to be cheated of a funeral. Their cheeks and noses were scarlet and their eyes sparkled like frost and peered avariciously after the wooden coffin.

It means nothing to them at all, the girl thought with acidity, sonorous words floating with strange lightness for their bulk over her head. They were here because a funeral was something to see, because, when rightly managed, it meant beer and cakes and because the long eddy of words that gathered together at regular intervals to rise and burst in a great ninth phrase (“Lord, let me know mine end and the number of my days: that I may be certified how long I have to live”) made them feel important. She would not give them beer and cakes, for she had been fond of the spirit that had inhabited the body carried before them. Yet because she had had no love for the body itself, which when she was small had beaten her and when she grew older had made strange crude gestures blindly towards her and repelled her, she felt unmoved. She was accustomed now to the absence of the cursing, unhappy, perplexed spirit. She had loved that with a quiet steady warmth. It had fed her and sheltered her and she was grateful, and when towards the end she had seen it putting up the best fight it was able against its own groping, sneering body, she had pitied it.

===Chinese===
“并且我这皮肉灭绝之后，我必在肉体之中得见神；我自己要见他，亲眼要看他，并不像外人。”

神父又高又瘦，驼着背，害着一场流个不停的伤风。他迈着大而缓慢的步子穿过墓园，每念一句就抽一下鼻子。天阴冷砭骨，他一副急于把这桩乏味差事打发掉的样子。每句与句之间他都抽一抽鼻子，每念完一句，就慌忙偷偷用白法衣的一角抹一下鼻子——那法衣在风里鼓荡着，像一面旗帜。他大步走着，毫不掩饰对寒冷的憎恶，可跟在后头的那一大群散散漫漫、心气十足的村民，走得跟他容许的一样慢，仿佛揪着那片猎猎翻飞的法衣下摆把他往回拽。他们不肯让人把一场葬礼从自己手里骗走。他们的脸颊和鼻头冻得猩红，眼睛亮得像霜，贪婪地盯住前面那口木棺。

这一切在他们全然没有任何意义，姑娘带着酸涩想道，那些洪亮的词句悬在她头顶，那么笨重的一大团，浮起来却出奇地轻。他们到这儿来，是因为葬礼是个可看的热闹，是因为操办得得体便意味着啤酒和点心，也因为那串长长打旋的词句每隔一定间歇便聚拢来、升起，在一记洪亮的第九句上迸裂（“主啊，求你叫我晓得我身之终，我的寿数几何，叫我知道我的生命不长”），让他们觉得自己也非同小可。她不会给他们啤酒和点心，因为她所钟爱的是那个灵，是它曾住在这具此刻被抬在他们前面的躯体里。可她对那躯体本身并没有感情——她年幼时它打过她，她大些了它又朝她做出些古怪粗鄙的姿态，盲盲目目地探过来，叫她厌恶——所以她心里波澜不惊。如今她已习惯于那个咒骂着的、不幸的、惶惑的灵的不在了。她曾以安静而恒久的温暖爱过那个灵。是它喂养她、庇护她，她心存感激；到了末了，她眼见它拼出全力抗击它自己那具摸索着的、讪笑着的躯体，她又怜悯过它。

===Original===
“For I am a stranger with thee; and a sojourner as all my fathers were. O spare me a little that I may recover my strength: before I go hence and be no more seen.”

Andrews stirred a little. They were the first words that had reached his consciousness since the fear of many persons had dumbed his heart. He was afraid when the villagers had arrived, the women to inspect the corpse and the men to look in vain for beer. Each new face had been a stab of anxiety and when unrecognised a faint relief, until the steady alternating currents of fear and comfort had lulled his mind asleep. He had been helped, turning his back on the chattering women, by the sight of the sea mist that poised itself for a moment on the top of the down over which he had come. Blown by a breeze behind, too faint to disperse it, it tottered for a moment drunkenly on the edge, and then fell in swathe after swathe into the valley. Its coming brought a sense of secrecy and of what he knew at heart was a false security. His unconsciousness held nothing but a dim irony and a perception of farce. He was the brother of the chief mourner, but the ceremony was to him only a solemn mummery. The man they put into the ground and for whom all these persons sang at intervals in a dreary whine was unknown to him and meant nothing more to him than the sudden sight of a bearded face and the dart of a falling golden star.

The girl—Elizabeth—his sister (it was hard to remember that she was his sister) had remained silent in the midst of a swiftly running current of voices. When the undertaker’s man had turned down the lid of the coffin there had been a little scurry of strange womenfolk to catch a last glimpse of the “deceased.” Then she had shown her one sign of feeling. She had turned to face them as though she would push them back and her mouth had twisted into an angry word which she did not speak. Then she gave a small gesture with her fingers addressed to herself. She stood aside and the undertaker’s man shut the coffin lid, casually as a man shuts a book. There was no air of finality about it, even when he drove in the nails. Andrews saw a little group of women whispering in a corner. They looked and whispered, and fear momentarily pierced his unconsciousness. He looked round him and imagined all faces turned towards him. The men disappointed of beer had nothing to do but talk and look curiously at the interior of the cottage, which they had never before entered. The women sniggered a little among themselves at the bareness and poked furtively at a chair here and a table there and made comments under their breath. Andrews thought that they were speaking of him. The men shuffled uneasily and stood massed together and fidgeted with their feet. They were annoyed with their wives for having brought them where there was no refreshment. Most of them had small farms and there was plenty of work they might have been doing. For want of other employment they looked cornerwise and carefully at the girl. They had seen her about many a time in the lanes but had been afraid to speak to her. There had been rumours—that she had been the dead man’s mistress, his natural child, a dozen contradictory tales, which united to put her outside the pale of “Good day,” comments on the weather or the crops, or even a nod of the head. Now death made her approachable and a little envied. They spoke of her slyly to each other in whispers, not so much to keep their comments from her as to keep them from their wives, comments on her appearance, on her potentialities as a bedfellow, on the fun she may have afforded to the man now dead. Andrews thought that they spoke of him.

===Chinese===
“因为我在你面前是客旅，是寄居的，像我列祖一般。求你宽容我，使我在去而不存之先，可以力量复原。”

安德鲁斯微微动了一下。自从对众人的恐惧把他的一颗心堵得喑哑，这是头一句抵达他意识的话。村民们到来时他很害怕：女人们来验看尸首，男人们来找啤酒却落了空。每一张新面孔都是扎来的一记焦灼，若是认不出是谁，才换来一丝轻微的宽解，直到恐惧与宽慰两股交替的水流稳稳把他摇进了昏睡。背过身不理那些叽叽喳喳的女人时，救了他的是海上来的雾：雾在他方才走下来的那座白垩丘顶上停驻了片刻。背后一阵微风推着它，风力又弱，驱不散它，它便醉醺醺地在崖沿上晃了一晃，随即一垄接一垄地倾泻进山谷。雾的到来带给他一种隐秘之感，以及一种他心底明白是虚假的安全。他的昏沉里不剩什么，只有一点暗淡的讥诮，一点对闹剧的知觉。他是丧主的哥哥，可这场仪式于他不过是一场庄严的哑剧。他们埋进土里的那个人，这些人不时用乏味的哀鸣为之歌唱的那个人，他并不认识，对他而言，那比起骤然瞥见的一张络腮胡子的脸、一颗金色流星坠落的斜光，多不出多少意味。

姑娘——伊丽莎白——他的妹妹（要记住她是他的妹妹真难），在一股奔涌不停的人声激流里一直沉默着。殡葬人放下棺盖时，陌生的女眷们一阵小小的急步骚动，抢着瞻仰“死者”最后一眼。那时她显出了她唯一的感情。她转身面向她们，像要把她们推回去，嘴扭成一个愤怒的字眼，却没有说出口。随后她朝自己做了个小小的手势。她站到一旁，殡葬人阖上棺盖，像人随手合上一本书。那动作里没有一点终结的意味，甚至在他把钉子钉进去的时候也没有。安德鲁斯看见角落里一小群女人在窃窃私语。她们看一眼、耳语几句，恐惧霎时刺穿了他的昏沉。他环顾四周，想象每张脸都转向了自己。啤酒落空了的男人们无事可做，只有闲扯，好奇地打量这间小屋的内部——他们从前从没进来过。女人们对着这屋子的空空荡荡彼此暗笑，这儿偷偷戳戳一把椅子，那儿戳戳一张桌子，压着嗓子品评。安德鲁斯以为她们在说他。男人们局促地蹭来蹭去，挤作一团，脚下烦躁不休。他们恼怪自己的婆娘把他们带到这么个没有酒饭的地方来。他们大多数有几亩小田，有的是活计本可去做。因为闲得没事，便从眼角里细细打量那姑娘。他们在巷子里见过她许多回，却从不敢跟她搭话。有过流言——说她是死者的情妇，是他的私生女——十几种互相矛盾的说法，汇在一处，把她逐出了“你好”、天气收成的寒暄、乃至点头之义的圈子。如今死亡使她变得可以接近，也叫人有些眼红。他们贼头贼脑地交头接耳议论她，倒不是怕她听见，是怕自己的婆娘听见——议论她的相貌，议论她做床伴的潜质，议论她或许给过这如今死了的男人多少快活。安德鲁斯以为他们在说他。

===Original===
With an effort he pulled his will erect. He saw himself standing on one side, an obvious stranger, uninterested and apart. He called “Elizabeth” with forced ease across the room. He had vague ideas of convincing them that he was her brother. She paid no attention, and he could think of no more to say. His will subsided slackly. (“For I am a stranger with thee; and a sojourner as all my fathers were.”)

Standing there in a misty graveyard beside the dark Elizabeth, Andrews felt his first flash of sympathy towards his father. Once his father had visited him at school. Andrews was in the gravel playground. It was in the interval between two lessons and he was hastily revising some Latin grammar. He had looked up and stared with amazement at the unexpected sight of his father, a tall, heavy man with a big beard clumsily dressed, crossing the gravel with the headmaster. The headmaster was small, quick and neat with birdlike motions. His father was shy, embarrassed, conscious suddenly of his own coarse bulk. He had said, “I was passing through and thought I’d come and see you.” He stopped, not knowing how to continue and stood shifting from one foot to the other. “Happy?” he asked. Andrews had the instinctive cruelty of a child. He remembered his father at home, domineering, brutal, a conscious master, not chary of his blows to either child or wife. “Very,” he said. His voice filled with artificial pleasure and he pronounced his words with artificial neatness. “We are doing Horace this term, father,” he said, “and Sophocles.” The headmaster beamed. His father murmured incoherently that he must go now, disappeared across the gravel, his heavy boots sounding self-consciously.

Andrews did not know then what kept his father away from home for short and frequent periods of blessed peace. He never knew the cause of that particular unfortunate visit. Perhaps he was on his way to the coast and a sudden realisation that his career must end sooner or later in death made him anxious to see his only idea of immortality. The voyage which followed must have reached its normal, successful end, for a few weeks later, when holidays fetched Andrews home, his father was there, dominant, easily aroused, as ready as ever with the whip, which he seemed to keep more for his family than for his hounds. A year later, while the child was at school and the father at sea, the mother died with the serene faithfulness of a completely broken will.

The shambling priest was reading the lesson in a meaningless drawl muffled by the mist and his increasing cold. The words meant no more to him than did the dead man. It was a mechanic ritual less conscious than the act of brushing teeth.

“I speak this to your shame but some men will say, how are the dead raised up? And with what body do they come? Thou fool, that which thou sowest is not quickened unless it die.”

===Chinese===
他费力地把自己的意志撑立起来。他看见自己站在一旁，一个明明白白的外人，漠然，疏隔。他隔着屋子喊了一声“伊丽莎白”，装出从容。他隐约想着要让这些人相信他是她哥哥。她没有理会，他也想不出再说什么。他的意志又软软地塌了下去。（“因为我在你面前是客旅，是寄居的，像我列祖一般。”）

站在雾蒙蒙的墓园里，站在黝黑的伊丽莎白身旁，安德鲁斯头一回对父亲闪过一丝同情。有一次父亲到学校去看过他。安德鲁斯正在碎石操场上，两堂课之间的歇息，他正匆匆温习几条拉丁文法。他抬起头，惊愕地盯住出乎意料出现在眼前的父亲——高大的、蓄着大胡子的笨拙男人，一身衣裳穿得不得法——正同校长一道走过碎石地。校长个子小，利落整洁，动作像鸟。父亲呢，怯生生的，窘迫，忽然意识到自己粗笨的块头。他说：“我路过这里，想着来看看你。”说完停住，不知怎么接下去，站在那儿两脚来回倒换。“快活吗？”他问。安德鲁斯怀着孩子天生的残忍。他记得父亲在家的样子：专横，粗暴，一个自觉的主子，对老婆孩子动起手来从不吝惜。“很快活。”他说。声音里灌满做作的高兴，吐字也做作地齐整。“这学期我们在读贺拉斯（Horace），父亲，”他说，“还有索福克勒斯（Sophocles）。”校长满面春风。他父亲含含糊糊咕哝说他这会儿得走了，从碎石地上消失了，沉重的靴子声听上去满是局促。

那时安德鲁斯并不知道，是什么让父亲隔三差五地短促离家，留下一段段蒙福的安宁。他始终不知道偏偏那一次倒霉的探访是为什么。也许他正动身去海边，忽然意识到这一行当迟早要终结于死亡，便急于看一眼他关于不朽的唯一念想。随后的那趟出海想必照常圆满收场，因为几个星期之后，假期把安德鲁斯接回家时，父亲在家里，专横，一点就着，鞭子照旧伺候着——那鞭子他养着，看样子更多是为家里人而不是为猎犬。一年之后，孩子在学校，父亲在海上，母亲死了，带着一颗被彻底压垮的意志的那种宁静的忠顺。

拖着脚步的神父用毫无意义的拖腔诵读经文，雾和他越来越重的伤风把声音捂得发闷。那些词句之于他，同那个死人也差不多少。这是一套机械的仪式，比刷牙还少几分自觉。

“我说这话，是要叫你们羞耻。但或有人要说，死人怎样复活呢？带着什么身体来呢？无知的人哪，你所种的若不死就不能生。”

===Original===
The coffin had been carried from the cottage in a farm cart. Elizabeth beside him he had walked into a wall of white that at every step melted before him and closed behind him. The villagers and their wives followed after, their footsteps sounding no louder than the drip of misty rain that fell from trees and bushes along their road. The silence was greater for the regular small tap tap of feet and drip drop of water. They could see the back of the cart which they followed but not the horse that drew it. Andrews looked behind him and saw a ghostly platoon. Faces and hands thrown forward in front of invisible bodies appeared and disappeared. He felt suddenly that all danger had been remitted until the funeral was over. Disembodied faces, hands that swam unattached in a white sea could not hurt him. He longed—not passionately, his mind was too asleep for passion—but with a small elusive ache that they would never reach the graveyard. This ache had crept into his sleep and also a sense of friendship with the girl who paced slowly beside him. He was asleep and longed a little that he might not awake. In his sleep one lay with him who would be gone when there was daylight in the brain.

They reached the burying place, and as the service went on fatigue grew and threatened to strip away his unconsciousness. He became aware that somewhere, as yet outside his mind but ready, opportunity given, to leap within, lay the fear to which he had grown accustomed. He held it at bay outside himself, but the struggle, as the minutes passed and the priest’s voice droned on, grew more intense.

They had taken the coffin to the edge of the grave, and the service must be drawing to an end. The priest’s voice grew rapid like the feet of a horse when its head is turned to home, faster and faster with the faintest trace of excitement at the thought of food and a rest from journeying: “O holy and merciful Saviour thou most worthy Judge eternal, suffer us not at our last hour for any pains of death to fall from thee.” They had lowered the coffin into the grave and began laboriously to shovel earth upon it. Spades slipped on the ground which was hard with cold. To Andrews the falling clods were a measurement of time, recording the vanishing moments of his peace. He would be happy to stand in the cold and the mist through eternity watching the shovelling spades. Fear was pressing in upon his mind. He could not keep it outside himself for long.

===Chinese===
棺材是靠一辆农家板车从小屋运来的。伊丽莎白走在他身边，他走进一堵白墙，每迈一步，那墙都在面前消融，又在身后合拢。村民和他们的婆娘跟在后面，脚步声不比一路从树木灌木上滴落的蒙蒙雨声更响。脚步细碎规律的嗒嗒和水珠的滴滴答答，反倒衬得四下愈静。他们看得见跟着的板车尾部，却看不见拉车的马。安德鲁斯回头一望，看见一队幽灵似的行列。一张张脸、一双手从看不见的躯干前伸出来，忽隐忽现。他忽然觉得，直到葬礼结束为止，一切危险都被赦免了。脱了躯壳的脸，在白茫茫大海里无着无落地游着的双手，伤不了他。他巴望——不是热切地巴望，他的心智睡得太沉，热切不起来——巴望他们永远走不到墓园。这一点隐隐的隐痛爬进了他的睡眠，同来的还有对缓缓走在身旁的姑娘的一种友情之感。他睡着了，还有点巴望自己就不再醒来。睡梦里有一个存在与他同卧，等脑子里天光一亮，那人就会不在。

他们到了下葬的地方，仪式进行着，疲惫越积越沉，眼看要把他的昏沉剥走。他觉察到，在某个地方——还在他心智之外，却整装待发，一有机会便要跃入——躺着他已经习惯了的那份恐惧。他把它拦挡在自身之外，可是随着一分钟一分钟过去，神父的嗓音单调地继续，这场搏斗愈来愈烈。

棺材已经移到墓穴边上，仪式想必快到尾声。神父的嗓音越来越快，像一匹调头回家的马的蹄子，越跑越急，只因为想到有饭吃、能歇脚，微微透着兴奋：“圣洁慈悲的救主啊，永恒的至公义的审判者，求你不要让我们在临终的时辰，因死亡的任何痛苦，从你坠落。”棺材已经放进墓穴，人们开始费力地往上面铲土。地冻得硬邦邦，铁锹直打滑。对安德鲁斯来说，落下的土块是时间的刻度，记录着他那点安宁一分一秒的消逝。他愿意永远站在这寒冷和雾里，望着那几把铲土的铁锹。恐惧正往他的心智里挤压。他撑不了多久，把它关在自己外面。

===Original===
Bundles of mist disintegrated. A low chatter of voices began replete with the blessing which had just been pronounced and moved towards the grave. Farmers stood in a ring and stared with interest at a hillock of earth judging its points. The women watched the chief mourner. By the rules of village life Elizabeth should now break down. Then after a brief struggle for the privilege one would put her arm round the girl and weep with her. Later they would be asked to accompany her back for refreshment. Their suspicions regarding Elizabeth’s birth and her moral character were confirmed when she abruptly turned her back on the grave.

She said to Andrews in a voice like frozen straw, “For God’s sake get rid of these people. I don’t want them. I don’t want them.” The mist opened a little, closed again and she was gone.

Andrews stood alone. He wanted to turn and run and put a wall of mist between him and that gathering of amazed eyes. Loneliness and fear were like the emptiness of hunger to his belly. If he took six steps away he would be lost to all the world in a blanket of white wool. He could find a childish comfort, bury his head beneath the bedclothes and fear no more the creaking of old furniture, deep in a darkness within a darkness. Why should any man be plagued as he had ever been plagued, with all the instincts—desires, fears, comforts—of a child and yet possess the wisdom of the man? In these moments of crisis he felt physically drawn in two—an agonising stretch of the nerves. One part of him said, “Hide yourself in this mist. You will see no one and nothing can hurt you. You will be comforted.” The other part said, “Fool! How they would talk.” He was the girl’s brother. He must act a little longer as her brother. That was the only safe way.

===Chinese===
一团团的雾散开了。一片低低的絮语响起来，满载着刚宣告过的祝福，朝坟墓挪动。农人们围成一圈，饶有兴致地盯住一个土堆，掂量它的种种好处。女人们盯住丧主。按村里生活的规矩，伊丽莎白此刻该垮下来了。然后经过一场对这份特权的短暂争夺，会有一个人搂住姑娘的肩膀，陪她一起哭。再往后，她们会被请去陪她回屋吃点东西。而她陡然转过身去背对坟墓，就把她们关于伊丽莎白出身和人品的猜疑坐实了。

她对安德鲁斯说话，嗓音像冻脆的干草：“看在上帝分上，把这些人打发走。我不要他们。我不要他们。”雾张开一条缝，又合上，她已经不见了。

安德鲁斯独自站着。他想转身就跑，跑出一道雾墙隔在自己和那一圈惊愕的眼睛之间。孤独和恐惧，就像肚子里饥饿的空虚。只要走开六步，他就会裹进一床白羊毛毯子里，全世界再也找不到他。他能找到一种孩子气的慰藉：把脑袋埋到被子底下，就再也不怕旧家具的吱嘎声，躲在黑暗里头的黑暗深处。为什么一个人偏要像他一向被折磨的那样受折磨，怀着一副孩子的全部天性——欲望、恐惧、慰藉——却又兼有成人的智慧？在这样的危机时刻，他觉得自己从肉体上被撕成两半——神经被拉扯得钻心地疼。他的一个部分说：“藏进雾里去。你谁也看不见，什么也伤不着你。你会得到抚慰。”另一个部分说：“蠢货！他们还不知要怎么嚼舌头。”他是姑娘的哥哥。他还得再把这哥哥当一会儿。这是唯一稳妥的路。

===Original===
He said to them—yet not to them but only to those amazed and offended eyes, “My sister’s upset. Forgive us if we don’t invite you back. She must be alone for a little. You will understand that it has been a great shock.” Very unconvincing and stiff his voice sounded to himself. He watched for any relaxation of the inquiry in the ring of eyes. Then he waited no longer but walked away. He stumbled as he went on a stone, which had fallen before its time from a gravedigger’s spade.

When he had walked a dozen yards he struck against an iron railing and the chill of the metal brought him part of the way towards consciousness. He felt his way along the rail gingerly with the tips of his fingers, finding relief in the slight pain he suffered from the stinging cold. When his feet, groping through an invisible gap, felt the broken ruts of the road beneath them, he waited. He had but to follow it half a mile to his left hand, so he calculated, to come upon the lights of the cottage. Yet there was no possible excuse to return. He should be thankful for the shelter of a night and the bare charity that had left him free. Bare charity enough, he thought, growing slowly aware of hunger. He had had no food for more than fifteen hours. There was little breeding left in him under the double influence of fear and hunger, but the small relic made him unwilling to thrust himself back as an unwanted guest. That she would accept him with an uninterested acquiescence deterred him. If she would only meet him again with resistance, he would be happy to seize shelter by force. He knew how easily he could work himself into a righteous anger and forget himself. It’s this damned Christianity, he thought, or else not enough of it. He would welcome her as an enemy, or as a friend who would pity him and understand his fear. It was her cold neutrality he hated.

With unexpected resolution he turned his back on the way he had come that morning and half ran as it were into an obscure future. The more he thought of the girl the more he hated her and pitied himself. If I had been a cat, he considered, she’d have given me something to eat. That he had not been offered food was the idea that now grated on his mind. The thought of her became so hateful, such an incarnation of inhuman indifference, that he nearly turned back to find her. He wanted to give her pain, beat her, make her cry out. She doesn’t know what it is to be alone and frightened, he thought. If I had been a cat… A tree brushed his face with a branch of wet twigs; even inanimate nature seemed to treat him with casual scorn. “I can’t be a coward, not altogether a coward,” he pleaded in a carefully hushed whisper. It needed courage to write that letter and go on living with them. And it was on the side of justice, he added, before his mind could murmur of jealousy.

===Chinese===
他对他们说——其实不是对他们，只对那些惊愕的、受了冒犯的眼睛：“我妹妹心烦意乱。我们就不请你们回去了，请多包涵。她得独自待一会儿。你们明白，这个打击太大了。”这声音在他自己听来生硬得很，毫无说服力。他觑着那一圈眼睛里的盘问有无松动的迹象。然后他不再等，转身走开。走的时候他在一块石头上绊了一下——那是掘墓人的铁锹早一步落下来的一块。

走出十几码，他撞上一道铁栏杆，金属的寒气把他往清醒里带了一程。他用指尖小心翼翼地沿栏杆摸索，刺骨的寒冷激起的轻微疼痛反倒让他好受些。等他的脚从一个看不见的缺口摸索出去，触到路面坑洼的车辙，他站住了。照他估算，顺着路朝左手走半英里，就能望见小屋的灯光。可是回去再没有任何借口。他该感激这一夜的庇护，感激那份留他自便的、赤裸裸的慈悲。够赤裸裸的了，他想，慢慢觉出饿来。他有十五个多钟头没吃东西了。恐惧和饥饿双管齐下，他身上所剩的教养无几，可就这么一点残余，也让他不愿把自己硬塞回去，做个不受欢迎的客人。她会以一种漠然的默许接纳他——正是这个叫他却步。要是她肯带着点抗拒来迎接他，他倒乐意凭强力占得一席庇护。他知道多么容易就能给自己鼓起一股义愤，忘乎所以。都是这该死的基督教害的，他想，要么就是基督教信得还不够。他宁愿她像个敌人那样迎他，或者像个朋友——会怜悯他、懂得他的恐惧。他恨的是她那种冷淡的中立。
