带着出乎意料的决绝，他背对早上来的那条路，几乎是跑着，扑进一个晦暗不明的未来。那姑娘他越想越恨，越想越自怜。要是我是一只猫，他想，她总该给我点吃的。没人让他吃上一口——此刻硌着他心的就是这个念头。她的形象变得可恨之极，成了不近人情的冷血的化身，他差点掉头回去找她。他想给她苦头，揍她，让她叫出声来。她根本不知道又孤单又害怕是什么滋味，他想。要是我是一只猫……一根树枝带着湿漉漉的细杈拂过他的脸；连无生的自然也随随便便地蔑视他。“我不能做懦夫，不能全然是懦夫，”他用小心压低的耳语为自己辩白。写那封信需要勇气，写完了还得继续同他们一处过活。何况那是站在正义一边的，他补了一句，抢在他心里那点因嫉妒而起的嘀咕之前。

===Original===
He became aware after a little of an uneasy feeling, which was not fear, nor shame, nor hunger. It would be dangerous to return, he said to himself. I can get clear away from the neighbourhood while this fog lasts. He walked on a little further, but hesitatingly. Carlyon’s quick, he thought. He’d search every shelter. I’m safer in this fog walking. When hunger forced itself again upon his notice he comforted himself illogically. After all there’s other shelter besides that cottage. He discovered that it was comforting to speak aloud. The small sound of his own voice was companionship in this white darkness and at the same time muffled by the fog, it was not loud enough to be overheard. He began to imagine fresh shelter; impelled by an empty stomach he returned but less convincingly to the thought of kindly old women. But there was something lacking in these dreams that had not been lacking the previous day. There was an ache in his mind as well as his belly, although he refused to take note of it. There was something very dissatisfying about the kindliest welcome which he imagined, but how could he recognise a fact, too ridiculous for expression, that he was homesick for the cottage in which he had spent a few uncomfortable hours? He fought hard against that realisation, and even quickened his step as though to remove himself out of the influence of a malign enchantment. In his struggle, for about the first time in the last three days, he forgot his danger and his fear. He did not even notice that he was walking uphill and that the fog was thinning very gradually in front of him. If he had had ears to hear, his own speech would have come to him with startling loudness in comparison with its previous imprisonment.

“A cat,” he said, “she’d have given a cat food,” but anger was disconcertingly confined to his voice. For as far as he knew the girl had had no food herself. He dwelt on the idea of a cat as constantly as he was able, but that image of inhumanity began rapidly to be scored over by fresh lines of thought, struggle as he would to preserve it intact. He remembered how she had led him to the dead man, awakening thus a brief feeling of intimacy between himself and her, and he remembered her words about the peace of God.

Andrews’s character was built of superficial dreams, sentimentality, cowardice, and yet he was constantly made aware beneath all these of an uncomfortable questioning critic. So now this other inhabitant of his body wondered whether he had not mistaken peace for inhumanity. Peace was not cowardly nor sentimental nor filled with illusion. Peace was a sanity which he did not believe that he had ever known. He remembered how once, becalmed at sea day after endless day, he had grown to loathe the water’s smooth unstirred surface as a symbol of a hatefully indifferent deity. And yet in the week of storm that followed he had longed to regain that quality which he began to regard as peace.

===Chinese===
过了一小会儿，他觉察到一种不安的感觉，不是恐惧，不是羞耻，也不是饿。回去是危险的，他对自己说。趁这场雾不散，我能远远离开这一带。他又朝前走了一截，却走得迟疑。卡莱昂耳朵尖，他想。他会搜遍每一处藏身的地方。我在雾里走着反倒安全。当饥饿又一次硬闯进他的注意，他不合逻辑地安慰自己：反正除了那间小屋，还有别的藏身处。他发现，说出声来倒叫人心安。在这片白色的黑暗里，自己那点细小的声音是个做伴的；同时又给雾捂着，不至于响得让人偷听了去。他开始想象新的藏身处；空肚子逼着，他又回到那些和善老太婆的念头上，只是不那么叫人信了。可这些梦里缺了一样东西，那样东西昨天还不曾缺。他心里也疼，不只肚子里，尽管他拒不理会。他想象里那最热忱的欢迎也有什么令人不能满足，可他怎么能认出那个事实呢——说出来都嫌可笑——他竟为一间只待过不舒服的几个钟头的小屋害起了乡愁？他拼命抗拒这个认识，甚至加快脚步，像要把自己从一个邪毒的魔咒的影响力里拔出来。在这场挣扎里，差不多是三天来的头一回，他忘了自己的危险和恐惧。他甚至没有留意自己正在上坡，也没留意雾在他前面正一点点变薄。倘若他有耳朵去听，自己的话声比起先前那副被囚的闷响，该响得吓他一跳了。

“一只猫，”他说，“一只猫她总会给吃的，”可恼怒偏偏只局限于话音里，叫他自己也不安。因为据他所知，姑娘自己也没有东西吃。他竭力把猫的念头不断抓在心上，可那幅不近人情的图像开始被一道道新的思绪迅速划划擦擦，随他怎样挣扎也保不全它的完整。他记起她怎样领他去见那个死人，就这样在他和她之间唤起过一阵短暂的亲近之感；他也记起她关于上帝的平安说的话。

安德鲁斯的性格由肤浅的梦想、感伤和怯懦筑成，然而在这一切底下，有一个令人不适的、爱诘问的批评者不断让他意识到它的存在。此刻，这另一个住在他身体里的住客便在追问：他是不是把平安错当成了不近人情。平安既不怯懦，也不感伤，也不满是幻觉。平安是一种清明的心智，他不信自己曾经领略过。他记起有一回，船在海上无风困住，一天又一天没有尽头，他渐渐憎恨起那平滑不动的水面，把它当作一个可恨地冷漠的神明的象征。而紧接着那一星期的风暴里，他又渴望重新赢得那品质——他开始把它称作平安。

===Original===
It was the sun shining on his eyes that woke him to the surroundings and an immediate knowledge of danger. He had been walking up hill and now emerged from the thickest fog as from a tunnel. It stood concrete at his back like a white wall. In front of him only faint floating wisps softened hedgerows, projecting boughs, the sun’s rays. It was not, however, the mere abstract fear of light which startled Andrews. A tall man, with dark hair uncovered by a hat, stood in the middle of the road. His back was turned and his hands were clasped behind him. Andrews could not mistake the light poise of the legs and shoulders that seemed to symbolise a spirit on tiptoe. He had been walking so fast up the hill that when he suddenly checked himself he nearly stumbled forward on his hands and knees. Although he had spent the last three days in almost continuous fear of Carlyon, now when the moment he had dreaded seemed to have arrived, his first instinct was not that of flight. It appeared incredible that he should so fear Carlyon, the man to whom he had turned continually for companionship in an alien and brutal life. He was only saved from stepping forward and touching Carlyon’s elbow by the sight of the man’s hands. Their clasp was tense, strained. They were the hands of a man holding himself as still as he was able in order to listen. Andrews half shifted a foot and the shoulders in front of him stiffened. He remembered a remark that Carlyon had once made to him, prompted by a sudden friendliness, “I’d know your step, Andrews, in a thousand.” He could see quite clearly the strange ugly face of Carlyon as it had looked at him then, shaded with an abstract tenderness. The face was a little swarthy, and very angular. A low brow belied the intelligence within. It would have been a crude, almost criminal, face if divorced from the quick but heavy body and the eyes which seemed brooding always on indefinable things, save when they lit with a kind of contempt at the body which housed them. The face had once been described as that of a chivalrous ape.

The hands like the hands of an ape would be strong. Andrews, moving as softly as he was able, took three steps backward and was swallowed in the mist. He waited listening with a racing heart; the sound of its beats he felt would drown any noise there might be. He could no longer see Carlyon and therefore he was certain that Carlyon could not see him. The anxiety that pecked at his nerve was the uncertainty whether or not Carlyon had recognised his tread. He waited, afraid to run, because in doing so he would be forced to turn his back.

No sound came, except a gentle, reiterated drip from a bough behind his right ear. He tried to persuade himself that Carlyon had heard nothing, and yet he could not banish the image of the tightly clenched hands. His mind changed tack and protested that even if Carlyon had heard and recognised his tread there was no cause for fear. Carlyon had, after all, no reason for supposing that he, Andrews, had been the cause of a certain disastrous fight. Carlyon was his friend. “My friend, my friend, my friend,” he repeated, trying to soothe the panic of his heart.

===Chinese===
是照在眼睛上的太阳把他唤醒，回到四周的景物，也立刻知道了危险。他一直在上坡，此刻从最浓的雾里钻了出来，像钻出一个隧道。雾在他背后立成一堵实体似的白墙。他面前只剩几缕飘浮的淡霭，把树篱、横出的枝干、太阳的光线都弄得柔和。然而，惊动安德鲁斯的并不是对光的那种单纯抽象的恐惧。一个高个子男人，深色头发，头上没戴帽子，站在路中央。他背朝着这边，双手在背后交握。安德鲁斯绝不会认错那双腿和肩的轻盈平衡，那仿佛踮着脚尖屏息的一个灵魂的写照。他上坡走得太急，猛地收住脚时，险些朝前扑倒，手膝着地。虽然过去三天他几乎一刻不停地惧怕卡莱昂，可当这被惧怕的一刻像是真到来了，他的头一个本能却不是逃。他会这样惧怕卡莱昂，说来难以置信——在那陌生而残忍的生活里，他一直不断地向他寻求陪伴。只凭看见那人的双手，他才免于跨出去碰一碰卡莱昂的手肘。那双手扣得紧紧的，绷着劲。那是一个极力稳住自己以便倾听的人的手。安德鲁斯半挪了一步脚，前面那双肩就僵住了。他记起卡莱昂有一回对他说过的一句话，是一阵突然涌起的亲善促成的：“安德鲁斯，你的脚步声，一千个人里我也认得出。”他清清楚楚看见当时卡莱昂望着他的那张奇怪的丑脸，蒙着一层抽象的温柔。那张脸略带黝黑，棱角极硬。低低的额头掩不住里面的聪慧。若不是配上那副敏捷却沉重的身板，配上那双仿佛总在冥想一些无可名状之事的眼睛——唯有当它们带着几分轻蔑亮起来、打量收容自己的这具肉身时才例外——那会是一张粗鲁的、近乎罪犯的脸。有人曾形容那张脸是一头侠义之猿的脸。

猿一样的手想必很有力气。安德鲁斯尽量放轻动作，朝后退了三步，让雾把自己吞了。他等着，心怦怦狂跳；他觉得那心跳的声音会盖过任何可能的响动。他看不见卡莱昂了，因此他确信卡莱昂也看不见他。啄着他神经的焦虑，是拿不准卡莱昂听没听出他的脚步。他等着，不敢跑，因为一跑他就不得不转过背去。

没有声音传来，只有右耳后头一根树枝上温和的、反复的滴水。他竭力说服自己卡莱昂什么也没听见，然而那双紧紧扣着的手的图像怎么也赶不走。他的心思换了航向，申辩说就算卡莱昂听见并认出了他的脚步，也没有什么可怕的。卡莱昂毕竟没有理由认定他安德鲁斯就是那场闯了大祸的火并的祸源。卡莱昂是他的朋友。“我的朋友，我的朋友，我的朋友，”他反复念着，想抚平心里的惊慌。

===Original===
Minutes must have passed before a sound broke the stillness. It was not a sound which Andrews had expected to hear. It was that of a low whistle, no louder than a man unconsciously might give in amazement. Andrews had counted six louder heart beats when the whistle was repeated. Then there was silence. Andrews very cautiously withdrew himself to the side of the road and a little further into the mist. His movements sounded terribly loud to his own ears. He bent forward and listened. A vague orange glow showed where the tunnel of mist came to an abrupt end. A few yards beyond stood the invisible Carlyon. Andrews did not believe that he had shifted so much as a foot.

Andrews leant a little further forward. He thought that he could hear a gentle whisper. He shivered. There was something uncanny in the thought of Carlyon with sad, apelike face standing very still with back turned and tense clenched hands, whistling and whispering to himself. For a moment Andrews wondered whether his friend (he found it impossible even in flight and fear to think of Carlyon other than as a friend) had been driven mad by the events of the last few days. He wanted to advance out of his tunnel and take Carlyon’s arm. He thought, as he had often thought before, how different everything would have been if Carlyon had been his father. Last night in the dark of the wood and far from Carlyon he had feared him. Now in more imminent danger he was torn between his fear, precipitate, unreasoning fear, and a friendship that was almost a grudging, soured love.

Andrews believed afterwards that in another moment he would have stepped out and greeted Carlyon, but as he stared into the orange glow, fear was given an opportunity to assert itself over friendship. A shadow for an instant striped vertically the glow and vanished again without a sound. Someone had entered the mist. Andrews cowered back against the hedge and listened. There was complete stillness. Andrews felt certain that somewhere within a few feet Carlyon also was listening, striving perhaps to catch those heartbeats which sounded so betrayingly loud. Then a stone was kicked and rumbled slowly a little way down the hill. A second shadow broke the glow and disappeared.

===Chinese===
想必过了好几分钟，才有声音打破寂静。那不是安德鲁斯料想会听见的声音。那是一声低低的口哨，不比一个人惊奇时不觉发出的更响。安德鲁斯数过六次更重的心跳，口哨又响了一遍。然后是寂静。安德鲁斯极其小心地退到路边，再往雾深处挪了挪。他的动静在自己耳朵里响得怕人。他俯身向前听。一线模糊的橙色光晕显出雾的隧道在哪里戛然而止。几码之外，站着那看不见的卡莱昂。安德鲁斯不相信他挪动过哪怕一步。

安德鲁斯又把身子探前了一点。他想自己能听见一阵轻柔的耳语。他打了个寒噤。想到卡莱昂，那张悲哀的、猿一般的脸，背着身，双手扣得死紧，站得纹丝不动，一边吹着口哨、对着自己耳语——这里面有种邪性的东西。一时间安德鲁斯疑心他的朋友（即使在逃亡和恐惧里，他也无法不把卡莱昂当朋友想）是不是被这几天的事逼疯了。他想走出自己的隧道，去挽住卡莱昂的胳膊。他像从前许多次想过的那样想：要是卡莱昂是他父亲，一切会多么不同。昨夜在黑树林里，离卡莱昂远远的，他怕他。此刻身处更迫近的危险，他在两种力量之间被撕扯着：一是恐惧，冒失的、不讲道理的恐惧；二是一种友情，那友情几乎是一份不情愿的、发了酸的爱。

安德鲁斯后来相信，再过一刻他就会跨出去招呼卡莱昂了，可正当他凝望那片橙色光晕，恐惧得到了一个机会，压过了友情。一道影子霎时把光晕竖着切了一道，又无声无息地消失了。有人进了雾。安德鲁斯缩身贴住树篱听着。四下彻底静止。他确信几英尺之内的什么地方，卡莱昂也在听，也许正竭力捕捉那些响得足以暴露行藏的心跳。随后一块石头被踢着了，咕噜噜顺着山坡往下滚了一小段。第二道影子切开光晕，又消失了。

===Original===
It was probably this second, more careless shadow, that Andrews next heard feeling along the hedge, with the noise of a small breeze through stubble. Progress was slow in a pathetic effort to be silent, pathetic with the pathos of a hippopotamus treading cautiously on dry twigs. The pathos, however, did not appeal to Andrews, who realised very clearly that in a few minutes he would inevitably be discovered. He could not fly without betraying himself, and his only hope was to step soundlessly into the middle of the road. But where was the first shadow, Carlyon? It needed a courage he was not accustomed to exercise to remove his back from the friendly firmness of the hedge and place himself defenceless in the road. He feared that if he moved he would come in contact with Carlyon. Only the slow pressure of necessity symbolised by the cautious crackling in the hedge creeping closer to him at every moment forced him at last to move.

The two paces which he took into the road seemed soundless even to himself, but he was not comforted. He felt completely exposed. Although he could see nothing, he felt, standing there ridiculously with slack, impotent arms that anyone could see him. He thought he could hear them coming towards him and had a wild longing to cry out to them, “Stop, stop, stop, please stop.” There was a game which he had played at school, where one boy, too often himself, stood with back turned counting ten, while the other boys advanced to touch his back. Andrews had perhaps forgotten, but he had never lost, the strain of waiting, hurriedly counting, for a hand to fall upon his back. So now he began to count in haste, “One, two, three, four, five, six, seven, eight, nine, ten,” as though there might be some remission for him at ten. He did not know why he counted and there was no remission.

He had a knife he remembered, in one pocket, but he could not remember which, and he did not dare to look. He was afraid even to raise a hand, lest it should make a sound in passing through the air. He let his arms hang limply at his side, like the arms of a doll empty of sawdust. After a considerable time the rustle in the hedge ceased. Somewhere behind a whispering began, too faint for him to catch a word. Then there was a rustle in the hedge on the opposite side of the road, more rapid, almost perfunctory. Then that too ceased and the whispering returned and hovered elusively in the mist. Sometimes he thought it came from his right side, sometimes from his left, at other times from behind him. It grew more rapid, seemed to beat desperately up and down, like a lost bird in a room. He began to think that he could distinguish words. Several times he imagined his own name “Andrews.” Hope stirred in his heart that Carlyon would give up the search and take his escape for granted. As though to confirm this hope the whisperers grew more and more careless. He could distinguish phrases. “Somewhere here,” and “I’d swear to his step.”

===Chinese===
安德鲁斯随后听见的，多半就是这第二道、更大意的影子在沿着树篱摸过来，带着一阵小风穿过麦茬的声响。进程很慢，那种想不出声的努力令人哀怜——那可悲，是一头河马在干树枝上小心落足的可悲。然而这份可悲并没打动安德鲁斯，他清清楚楚地明白，几分钟之内他必然会被发现。他不能不声不响地脱身，唯一的指望是悄没声地挪到大路中央。可第一道影子，卡莱昂，在哪儿？把背从树篱那友好的坚实上挪开、把自己毫无遮拦地放到路上，需要一种他不习惯运用的勇气。他怕自己一动就会撞上卡莱昂。只是树篱里那份小心的窸窣一刻比一刻更近，那必需之力的缓慢重压，到末了才逼得他动了。

他迈进路中的那两步，连他自己听来也悄无声息，可他并不因此安心。他觉得自己彻底暴露了。虽然什么也看不见，他觉得自己站在那儿荒唐可笑，两条胳膊松软无力，谁都能看见他。他觉得能听见他们正朝自己过来，心里发狂地想朝他们喊：“停，停，停下，请停下。”他上学时玩过一种游戏：一个男孩——多半又是他自己——背过身数到十，别的男孩偷偷上前去碰他的背。安德鲁斯也许忘了，却从没真正丢掉那种紧绷：一边急急地数着数，一边等一只手落在自己背上。此刻他又急急地数起来：“一，二，三，四，五，六，七，八，九，十，”仿佛数到十就能得着什么宽赦。他不知道自己为什么数，也没有什么宽赦到来。

他记得身上有把刀，在一个口袋里，可想不起是哪个，他也不敢去看。他连抬一抬手都怕，怕胳膊划过空气会出声。他让两臂软软地垂在身侧，像一具掏空了木屑的木偶的胳膊。过了好一阵，树篱里的窸窣停了。背后什么地方起了耳语，轻得他一个字也捉不住。接着路那边的树篱里又是一阵窸窣，更急，几乎敷衍了事。随后那阵也停了，耳语又回来，在雾里飘忽不定。有时他觉得在右边，有时在左边，又有时在背后。它越来越急，像在绝望地上下扑打，活像一只困在屋里的鸟。他开始觉得自己能分辨出字眼了。好几次他想象听见自己的名字“安德鲁斯”。心里搅起一线希望：卡莱昂会放弃搜寻，就当他已经逃掉。仿佛要印证这希望，耳语的人越来越不当心。他能分辨出语句了。“就在这一带”，还有“他的脚步声我可以赌咒”。

===Original===
After an interval Carlyon’s voice blew like a melancholy wind through the mist. “Andrews,” it said, “Andrews.” And then, “Why are you frightened? What’s the matter with you? It’s Carlyon, merely Carlyon.”

The fascination of the voice! It seemed to hold for Andrews everything which he so much desired—peace, friendship, the end of a useless struggle. He wanted to say, “Here I am, Carlyon,” and lie down there in the mist and sleep; and wake to find Carlyon sitting beside him talking of this and that with brooding kindliness, drowning the nauseating fatigue of danger, the acrid smell of smoke, the monotony of winds with the cool beauty of his voice. Above, the eternally reiterated clatter of feet on deck, the beat, beat, beat of flapping canvas, the curses, movement, scurry, unrest; below, Carlyon’s apelike face transfigured with peace—

> Ye have been fresh and green,
> Ye have been filled with flowers,
> And ye the walks have been,
> Where maids have spent their hours.

“Andrews, Andrews,” with a soft melancholy. “I must not, I must not,” he said to himself, sobbing hysterically and yet with an effort retaining silence, though the effort was a tearing pain in throat and chest. “That’s over.” Over forever friendship, poetry, silence at the heart of noise; remained fear and a continual flight. And he had intended to win peace.

Carlyon, he realised, had not spoken now for many moments. He was surrounded again by silence save for the drip, drip, drip of the laden bough. Space that had closed in on him during the sound of the voice calling spread away again on every side. He was alone in a wilderness of white mist hopelessly barren of companionship. He waited listening for a little while longer and then stumbled back into the mist the way that he had come. He thought that Carlyon had been deceived or had given up the search. It did not occur to him that Carlyon might wait quietly and listen to find the direction which he took. Andrews ran crookedly along invisible ruts with a slow strange lightening of the heart.

===Chinese===
隔了一阵，卡莱昂的声音穿过雾吹过来，像一阵忧郁的风。“安德鲁斯，”那声音说，“安德鲁斯。”然后是：“你怕什么？你怎么了？是卡莱昂，不过是卡莱昂。”

那声音的魔力！它仿佛替安德鲁斯盛着一切他渴求至深的东西——平安，友谊，一场无用挣扎的终结。他想说：“我在这儿，卡莱昂，”然后就地躺进雾里睡去；醒来时看见卡莱昂坐在身边，带着沉思的慈和说着这个那个，用他那嗓音清凉的美，把危险的令人作呕的疲惫、烟的刺鼻气味、风的单调统统淹没。上面，是甲板上永不止歇的脚步杂沓，帆布翻卷着，噼啪，噼啪，噼啪，咒骂，走动，奔忙，不宁；下面，是卡莱昂那张猿一般的脸，被平安照得变了模样——

> 你们曾鲜翠青青，
> 你们曾繁花满枝，
> 你们曾是那小径，
> 少女们曾在那里消磨时辰。

“安德鲁斯，安德鲁斯，”带着一声轻柔的忧郁。“我不能，我不能，”他对自己说，歇斯底里地啜泣着，却又竭力保持不出声，尽管这份竭力是喉咙和胸腔里一阵撕裂般的疼。“那都过去了。”永远过去了：友谊，诗，喧嚣中心的寂静；剩下的只是恐惧和无休止的逃亡。而他本来是一心要赢得平安的。

他意识到，卡莱昂已经有好一阵没再说话了。寂静重新围拢了他，只剩那根沉沉的树枝滴答、滴答、滴答。方才那呼唤的声音响起时向他收紧的空间，此刻又向四面八方散开。他独自待在白雾的荒野里，荒瘠得长不出一丝同伴之谊。他又听了片刻，然后跌跌撞撞退回雾里，循着来的方向走去。他以为卡莱昂受了骗，或者已经放弃搜寻。他全没想到，卡莱昂也许会静静地等着听，好辨明他去的方向。安德鲁斯歪歪斜斜地沿着看不见的车辙跑，心口带着一种缓慢而奇异的轻。

### IV / 四

===Original===
He became aware of the cottage again by the red glow of a hidden flame, which penetrated a little way into the white blanket of mist with a promise of warmth and calm companionship and food. Fear had not dispelled his hunger, it had but overlaid it with a more fierce emotion. Now with the slow return of peace he remembered what his belly desired. He was not angry nor frightened now, only a little ill at ease. He advanced cautiously, with one arm of his spirit raised to ward off a blow.

Through the window he peered into a room deprived of daylight. A large fire burnt with a kind of subdued ferocity and its red rays, instead of bearing light, spilt blacker pools of darkness in the room. Only in a small semicircle before it was a space cleared, and the dark pushed back from there formed a more sombre and concentrated wall on the further side. Squatting on the floor in the cleared space Elizabeth knitted with a metallic flash—flash of needles like sparks from a gaseous coal.

Her figure started so distinctly from the shadows, distorted though it was by the glass, that Andrews did not realise that his own face was veiled. He tapped with fingers which he intended to sound gentle and reassuring. She looked up and remained staring at him with a mixture of fear, perplexity and doubt, and let the knitting fall upon her lap. He smiled but was unaware that she could not see his smile, or glimpsed at most a vague grimace from almost invisible lips. He tapped again and saw her lift whatever it was she had been knitting to her breast and tightly press it to her. How slim, he thought, as she rose and stood (a dark Elizabeth, he wondered again) where the flicker of the flames played up and down her body like the dazed, groping fingers of a lover. Her hand pressed so hard on her breast that it appeared to be reaching for the heart to hold it and still its beats. Only then did Andrews realise that she could not see him clearly, and that she was afraid. But at the moment when he prepared to reassure her, the small quiver of fear left her lips and she passed from the zone of the firelight and advanced to the window through the shadows.

===Chinese===
一簇藏在屋内的火苗透出的红光，让他重新意识到了那座小屋；红光微微渗进白色的雾毯，允诺着温暖、安宁的陪伴和食物。恐惧并没有驱散饥饿，只是用一种更猛烈的情绪把它盖住了。此刻随着平静缓缓归位，他记起了肚子想要什么。现在他不怒也不怕，只是有点儿局促。他小心地向前挪近，仿佛灵魂举起一只手臂，要挡开一记打击。

透过窗玻璃，他窥视着一间被夺去白昼的屋子。一大炉火燃着，带着一种压低了的凶猛；它的红光并不带来光明，反倒在屋里泼洒出一洼洼更黑的黑暗。唯有火前一小块半圆的地方是空的，被推开的黑暗在那一边积成一堵更阴沉、更浓稠的墙。伊丽莎白蹲在那片空地的地板上织着东西，织针闪着金属的微光——那闪光像煤气火里迸出的火星。

她的身影尽管隔着玻璃有些变形，却从阴影里清晰地凸现出来，以致安德鲁斯（Andrews）没有意识到，自己那张脸是罩在暗处的。他用手指叩窗，本想让那声音显得温柔而令人安心。她抬起头，怔怔地望着他，眼里混着恐惧、困惑和疑虑，手里的活计落到了膝上。他笑了，却不知道她看不见他的笑，至多瞥见几乎隐没的唇形扭出一个模糊的怪相。他又叩了叩，看见她把手里的织物举到胸前，紧紧按住。多苗条啊，他想；她起身立定（又是一个暗影里的伊丽莎白，他再次纳闷），站在火光闪动之处，那光在她身上上下游走，像恋人恍恍惚惚、摸索着的手指。她的手在胸口压得那样紧，仿佛要伸进去抓住心脏，按住它的跳动。直到这时安德鲁斯才明白，她看不清他，她在害怕。可就在他准备安慰她的一瞬，那点畏惧的微颤离开了她的唇边，她走出火光的范围，穿过阴影朝窗前走来。

===Original===
He heard her fingers feeling not very certainly for the catch. Then the window swung open and he stepped away. “Is it really you back?” she whispered, and he could not tell from her voice whether she was afraid or glad.

“Yes, yes,” he said, “it’s me.”

She said: “Oh, you,” in a flat, disappointed tone. “What do you want?” He became afraid that she would again shut the window, leaving him in the cold, deprived of the tossing fire.

===Chinese===
他听见她的手指没有太大把握地摸索着插销。随后窗户荡开了，他退开一步。“真是你回来了？”她低声问，从声音里他分不出她是怕还是喜。

“是的，是的，”他说，“是我。”

“哦，是你啊。”她说，声调平板，透着失望。“你要干什么？”他害怕她会重新关上窗，把他丢在寒冷里，夺走那炉跳动的火。

===Original===
“Won’t you let me in?” he asked. “You needn’t be afraid,” and when she laughed ironically, he began to speak rapidly. “I did all that you told me,” he said. “I got rid of all those wretched villagers.”

“Was it necessary to come here to tell me that?” she asked.

“I want shelter,” he said with despairing simplicity. He heard her leave the window and unbolt the door. “Come in then if you must,” she called to him.

===Chinese===
“你不放我进去吗？”他问。“你用不着害怕。”她讥诮地一笑，他便急促地说下去：“你吩咐的我都办了，”他说。“那些讨厌的村民，我全打发走了。”

“非得跑到这儿来说这个吗？”她问。

“我想要个避身的地方。”他说得直白，透着绝望。他听见她离开窗口，拔开了门闩。“那就进来吧，非进来不可的话。”她朝他喊道。

===Original===
He came and moved at once to the fire, his momentary sentiment drowned in the mere desire to be warm, to drink heat with every pore of his body. He felt that he could with small encouragement have lifted the burning coals and pressed them to his breast. He twisted his figure into odd distorted shapes, so that every part of him might receive a blessing from gracefully gesticulating hands of flame.

“Have you any food?” he asked. With the cold acquiescence which he had feared she went and fetched a loaf of bread, and would have placed it on the table had he not stretched out his hands for it. Still crouching over the fire he broke off portions with his fingers. Only when his hunger was partly satisfied something uneasily stirring in his mind made him apologise.

“I haven’t had food for fifteen hours,” he said. “I was hungry and cold out there. It’s good of you …”

She came into the circle of firelight. “There’s no reason why I should shut you out,” she said. “I’ve been alone. You are better than no one, even you.”

===Chinese===
他进来后径直凑到火边，方才那一瞬的感伤早被淹没了，淹在单纯想暖和过来的渴望里——他要用身体的每一个毛孔去啜饮热气。他觉得只要有人小小鼓励一句，自己简直能把烧红的煤块捧起来贴在胸口。他把身体扭成种种古怪的姿势，好让每一处都能从火苗那优雅招摇的手掌里领受一份祝福。

“你有吃的吗？”他问。她带着他担心的那种冷淡的默许，走去取来一条面包；要不是他伸出双手去接，她本要把它放在桌上的。他仍旧蹲伏在火前，用手指掰下一块块来。直到饥饿稍稍平息，心里有什么东西不安地骚动起来，他才道了歉。

“我十五个钟头没吃东西了，”他说。“在外头又饿又冷。你真是好心……”

她走进火光的圈子里。“我没有理由把你关在外头，”她说。“我一直是一个人。有个人总比没有人强，哪怕是你。”

===Original===
Warmed by the fire, hunger quenched by bread, he began to grow jocular.

“You oughtn’t to find any difficulty in getting company,” he laughed. “And who was it you expected to find outside the window?”

“We’ve buried him,” she said. “I don’t suppose that he’ll return.”

Andrews looked up in astonishment at a pale, set face, touched with a reluctant grief. “You don’t mean,” he asked in awestruck astonishment, “that you thought …”

“Why shouldn’t I think that?” she asked, not with indignation but with candid questioning. “He’s only a few days dead.”

===Chinese===
火烤暖了身体，面包压住了饥饿，他开始打趣起来。

“你找伴儿不该有什么难处，”他笑道。“那你方才在窗外，当以为会看见谁？”

“我们把他埋了，”她说。“我想他不会再回来了。”

安德鲁斯惊愕地抬头，触到一张苍白而绷紧的脸，罩着一层不情愿的哀伤。“你不是当真，”他震骇地惊问，“以为他……”

“我为什么不能这么想？”她问，并非愤慨，而是坦率的质询。“他死了才几天。”

===Original===
“But they don’t rise again,” Andrews said in the kind of solemn whisper which he had used as a boy in the school chapel.

“Their spirits do,” she answered, and her white, still face continued to question him.

“Do you believe in all that?” he asked, not in mockery, but in a curiosity tinctured with longing.

“Of course; you can read it in the Bible.”

===Chinese===
“可是死人不会再起来，”安德鲁斯用一种庄重的低语说，那语气还是他小时候在学校礼拜堂里用过的。

“他们的灵魂会，”她答道，她那苍白而安静的脸继续在质询他。

“这些你全信？”他问，并非嘲弄，而是一种掺着渴望的好奇。

“当然；圣经上写着。”

===Original===
“Then,” he hesitated a moment, “if men are not quite dead, when we bury them, we can still hurt them, make them suffer, revenge ourselves.”

“You must be bad,” she said fearfully, “to think of that. But don’t forget that they can hurt us, too.”

She came up to the fire and stood beside him, and he shifted a little under the clear, courageous gaze of her eyes. “I’m not afraid of you now,” she said, “because you are just a person I know, but when you came last night you were a stranger and I was afraid. But then I thought to myself that he,” and she pointed to the table as though the coffin still lay there, “would not let me be harmed. He was a bad man, but he wanted me, and he’d never let anyone else get me.”

“I never meant any harm to you,” Andrews muttered, and then added with a convulsive pleading, “It was only fear that made me come. You other people never seem to understand fear. You expect everyone to be brave like yourself. It’s not a man’s fault whether he’s brave or cowardly. It’s all in the way he’s born. My father and mother made me. I didn’t make myself.”

===Chinese===
“那么，”他迟疑了一下，“如果人被埋下去并没有死透，我们还能伤害他们，让他们受苦，向他们报仇。”

“会想这种事，你心一定坏，”她惶恐地说。“可是别忘了，他们也能伤我们。”

她走到火边，站在他身旁；在她那双眼睛坦率而勇敢的逼视下，他稍稍不安地挪了挪。“现在我倒不怕你了，”她说，“因为你不过是个我认识的人；可你昨夜来的时候，你是个陌生人，我怕。后来我对自己说，他，”她指了指桌子，仿佛棺材还停在那儿，“不会让人伤了我的。他是个坏人，可他要我，他绝不会让别的任何人把我抢走。”

“我从没想伤害你，”安德鲁斯嘟哝着，随即痉挛似的央求道：“是恐惧才叫我来的。你们这些人好像从来不懂恐惧。你们指望人人都像你自己一样勇敢。勇敢还是怯懦，不是一个人的过错。那全看他是怎么生的。我父母生了我，我没法自己造一个我。”

===Original===
“I never blamed you,” she protested. “But you always seem to leave out God.”

“Oh, that,” he said. “That’s all on a par with your spirits. I don’t believe in that stuff. Though I’d like to believe in the spirits, that we could still hurt a man who is dead,” he added with a mixture of passion and wistfulness.

“You can’t if they are in heaven,” she commented.

“There’s no danger of that with the man I hate,” Andrews laughed angrily. “It’s curious, isn’t it, how one can hate the dead. It makes one almost believe your stuff. If they are transparent like the air, perhaps we breathe them in.” He screwed up his mouth as though at a bad taste.

===Chinese===
“我从来没有怪你，”她申辩道。“可你好像总是把上帝漏掉。”

“哦，那个，”他说。“那跟你那些灵魂是一路货。那种东西我不信。不过灵魂我倒愿意信——愿意信我们还能伤害一个死了的人，”他补上一句，激情和怅惘混在一起。

“他们若在天堂，你就伤不着了，”她评道。

“我恨的那个人，绝没有上天堂的危险，”安德鲁斯怒气冲冲地笑道。“说来奇怪，不是吗，人竟能恨一个死人。这倒叫人几乎要信你那一套了。要是他们像空气一样透明，说不定我们一呼吸就把他们吸了进去。”他撇起嘴，像尝到了什么坏味道。

===Original===
She watched him curiously. “Tell me,” she said, “where have you been since we buried him?”

He began to speak with resentful anger. “I told you it was only fear that drove me to you last night, didn’t I? Well, I don’t want to trouble you any more.”

“And fear brought you back again?”

“Yes—at least, not entirely.” Looking down at her dark hair, pale face and calm eyes seemed to infuriate him. “You women,” he said, “you are all the same. You are always on your guard against us. Always imagine that we are out to get you. You don’t know what a man wants.”

“What do you want?” she asked and added with a practicality which increased his meaningless anger, “Food? I have some more bread in a cupboard.”

===Chinese===
她好奇地打量着他。“告诉我，”她说，“我们把他埋了以后，你去了哪儿？”

他开口时带着怨怒。“我跟你说过，昨夜只是恐惧把我赶到你这儿来的，对不对？好吧，我不想再打搅你了。”

“又是恐惧把你带回来的？”

“是——至少不全是。”他低头看她的深色头发、苍白的脸和沉静的眼睛，看着看着似乎倒被激怒了。“你们女人，”他说，“全是一路货。总是提防着我们。总以为我们存心要算计你们。你们根本不懂男人要什么。”

“你要什么？”她问，随即带着一种务实劲儿补了一句，那务实劲儿更是给他的无名火添了柴：“吃的吗？碗柜里还有面包。”

===Original===
He made a despairing motion with his hand, which she interpreted as a refusal. “We get tired of our own kind,” he said, “the coarseness, hairiness—you don’t understand. Sometimes I’ve paid street women simply to talk to them, but they are like the rest of you. They don’t understand that I don’t want their bodies.”

“You’ve taught us what to think,” she interrupted with a faint bitterness breaking the peace of her mind.

He took no notice of what she said. “I’ll tell you,” he said, “a reason why I came back. You can laugh at me. I was homesick for here.”

He turned his back on her. “I’m not making love to you. It wasn’t you. It was just the place. I slept here and I hadn’t slept before for three days.” He waited with shoulders a little hunched for her laughter.

===Chinese===
他绝望地摆了摆手，她当作是回绝。“我们对自己那一类人也会厌倦，”他说，“那粗鲁，那多毛——你不懂。有时候我付钱给街上的女人，只为跟她们说说话，可她们跟你见过的其余女人没什么两样。她们不明白，我不要她们的身体。”

“我们该怎么想，还不是你们教出来的，”她打断他，一丝淡淡的苦涩打破了她心境的平和。

她说的话他没有理会。“我告诉你，”他说，“我回来的一个缘故。你可以笑我。我想这儿想得像害乡愁一样。”

他转过身去背对着她。“我不是在向你调情。不是为了你。就为这地方。我在这儿睡了一觉，在这之前我三天没合眼。”他微微耸着肩，等她发笑。

===Original===
She did not laugh and after a little he turned. She had been gazing at his back. “Aren’t you amused?” he asked ironically. His relations with her seemed necessarily compounded of suspicion. When he first came he had been suspicious of her acts and now he was suspicious of her thoughts.

“I was wondering,” she said, “whom you were frightened of and why I like you.” Her eyes wandered down his body from face to feet and stayed at his right heel. “You’ve worn your stockings out,” she said simply, but the way in which she turned the words on her tongue till they came out with a rounded sweetness gave to their simplicity a hidden significance.

“They are not of silk,” he said, still seeking for disguised mockery.

She held out a hand which she had kept pressed to her side. “Here is a stocking,” she said; “see if it will fit you.”

He took it from her as cautiously as if it had been a strange reptile and turned it over and over. He saw that it had been newly darned and remembered how he had seen her from the window working in the firelit space.

===Chinese===
她没有笑。过了一小会儿，他转过身来。她一直在凝视他的后背。“你不觉得好笑吗？”他话里带刺。他与她之间的关系，似乎必然是拿猜疑调成的。他初来时猜疑她的举动，如今猜疑她的心思。

“我是在纳闷，”她说，“你在怕谁，还有我为什么喜欢你。”她的目光顺着他的身子从脸一直溜到脚，停在他的右脚跟上。“你的长袜穿破了，”她平平淡淡地说，可她把这句话在舌尖上翻来覆去，直到吐出来时带着一种圆润的甜，平淡里便藏下了一点隐秘的意味。

“又不是丝的，”他说，仍旧在搜寻影射的讥讽。

她伸出那只一直按在身侧的手。“这儿有一只长袜，”她说；“看看合不合你的脚。”

他从她手里接过它，小心翼翼像接过一条陌生的爬虫，翻来覆去地看。他看见织补的针脚是新的，记起他方才从窗外望见她在火光照亮的那块地方做活。

===Original===
“You were mending this,” he said, “when I came to the window.” She made no answer and he examined it again. “A man’s stocking,” he commented.

“It was his,” she replied.

He laughed. “Do your spirits wear stockings?” he asked.

She clenched and unclenched her hands, as one nervously wrought up by another’s stupidity. “I had to do something,” she murmured rapidly as though her breath had been nearly exhausted by a too long and fatiguing race. “I couldn’t just sit.” She turned her back on him and walked to the window and leant her forehead against it, as though seeking coolness or perhaps support.

===Chinese===
“我来窗前的时候，你补的就是它。”他说。她没有作声，他又细看了一遍。“男人的长袜。”他评道。

“是他的。”她答道。

他笑了。“你们的灵魂也穿长袜么？”他问。

她双手攥紧又松开，像被别人的迟钝搅得神经紧张。“我总得做点什么，”她急促地低语，仿佛一口气被一场太长太累的奔跑耗尽了。“我不能干坐着。”她转过身背对他，走到窗前，把额头抵在窗玻璃上，像在寻求凉意，或许是在寻求支撑。

===Original===
Andrews turned and turned the stocking in his hand. Once at the window Elizabeth’s figure was motionless. He could not even catch the sound of her breathing. A gap of shadow separated them, and the flickering of the flames made useless but persistent attempts to cross it. He was shamed by the patient obstinacy of their compassion, and was temporarily rapt from his own fear, hatred and self-abasement, touched for a lightning instant with a disinterested longing for self-sacrifice. He would not cross that bridge of shadows, for he feared that if he touched her he would lose the sense of something unapproachably beautiful, and his own momentary chivalry would vanish before the coward, the bully and the lustful sentimentalist to whom he was accustomed. For that instant his second criticising self was silent; indeed he was that self.

===Chinese===
安德鲁斯把长袜在手里翻过来又翻过去。伊丽莎白立在窗前，身影一动不动。他连她的呼吸声都听不见。一道阴影的深谷把他们隔开，火光摇曳，徒然而执拗地一次又一次想跨过去。火光那份怜悯的耐心与固执使他羞愧；他一时从自己的恐惧、仇恨与自贱中被摄走，在电光石火的一瞬，被一种无私的、渴望自我牺牲的心情触动。他不肯跨过那座阴影之桥，因为他怕一旦碰着她，就会失去那不可企及的美感，他一时兴起的骑士风度，就会在他熟惯的那几个角色——懦夫、恶霸、好色的感伤主义者——面前烟消云散。那一瞬，他那第二个专事苛评的自我沉默了；说真的，他就是那个自我。

===Original===
He was on the point of making some stumbling gesture of contrition, when the coward in him leaped up and closed his mouth. Be careful, it cautioned him. You are a fugitive; you must not tie yourself. Even as he surrendered to that prompting he regretted the surrender. He knew that for a few seconds he had been happy, with the same happiness, but a stronger, as he had gained momentarily in the past from music, from Carlyon’s voice, from a sudden sense of companionship with other men.

The mist which had been white was turning grey. The real dark was approaching, but it made no apparent difference to the room. Andrews, feeling the comfortable warmth of the fire behind him, wondered how Carlyon was faring in a colder and surely more alien world. And yet was it more alien? Carlyon had the friendship and the trust of his two fellow fugitives. He was not alone. The old self-pity began to crawl back into Andrews’s heart, as he watched the girl’s motionless back.

===Chinese===
他正要做个结结巴巴的悔罪姿态，他身上的懦夫一跃而起，捂住了他的嘴。小心，那懦夫告诫他。你是个逃犯；不可把自己拴住。他一面听从这怂恿，一面已为这听从后悔。他知道这几秒钟里他是幸福的，与过去他从音乐里、从卡莱昂（Carlyon）的声音里、从与旁人骤然同心的一刻里暂时得到的幸福，是同一种幸福，只是更强烈。

原先是白色的雾正在转灰。真正的黑暗临近了，可屋子里看不出什么变化。安德鲁斯感受着背后炉火的暖意，心里琢磨卡莱昂在一个更寒冷、而且肯定更疏异的世界里情形怎样。然而那世界当真更疏异吗？卡莱昂有两个亡命同伙的友情和信任。他不是独自一人。他望着姑娘一动不动的背影，旧日的自怜又开始爬回安德鲁斯的心头。

===Original===
“Can we light some candles,” he asked, “and make this room more cheerful?”

“There are two candlesticks on the table,” she said, keeping her forehead pressed to the window, “and two on the dresser. You can light them if you like.”

Andrews made a spill from a playbill, which he found in his pocket, and lit it at the fire. Then he passed, from candle to candle making little aspiring peaks of flame pierce the shadows. Slowly they rose higher and small haloes formed round their summits, a powdery glow like motes in sunlight. Cloaked from all draughts by the surrounding mist they burnt straight upwards, tapering to a point as fine as a needle. The shadows were driven back into the corners of the room where they crouched darkly like sulking dogs rebuked.

===Chinese===
“我们点几支蜡烛好不好？”他问，“让这屋子快活一点。”

“桌上有两座烛台，”她说，额头仍抵在窗上，“碗柜上还有两座。你愿意点就点吧。”

安德鲁斯从口袋里翻出一张戏单，搓成一根纸捻，在火上点着了。随后他一支一支点过去，让一小簇一小簇向上攀的火苗刺进阴影。火苗慢慢升高，顶上结成小小的光晕，一片粉雾似的微光，像阳光里的浮尘。四面的雾替它们挡住了一切穿堂风，火苗笔直向上烧，尖细得收成一枚针尖。阴影被赶回屋角，黑沉沉地蜷伏在那里，像挨了训斥、赌着气的狗。

===Original===
When Andrews had lit the last candle he turned and saw that Elizabeth was watching him. Joy and grief were both moods able to pass lightly across her face without disturbing the permanent thoughtfulness of her eyes, which seemed to regard life with a gaze devoid of emotion. The candles now tipped her face with gaiety. She made no reference to her short surrender to grief, but clapped her hands, so that he stared at her amazed by this rapid change of mood.

“I like this,” she said, “we’ll have tea. I’m glad to have someone to talk to—even you,” and she moved to the dresser and began to take out plates, cups, a loaf of bread, some butter, a kettle which she filled and put upon the fire. With proud and reverent fingers she drew a caddy from the dresser, handling it as reverently as a gold casket.

“I haven’t had tea,” he said slowly, “like this since I left home … I’ve wanted it though.” He hesitated. “It’s queer that you should be treating me like this, like a friend.”

===Chinese===
点起最后一支蜡烛时，安德鲁斯转过身来，看见伊丽莎白正望着他。喜与哀都能轻盈地掠过她的脸，搅不动她眼中那份长驻的沉思；那目光似乎以毫无情绪的注视打量着人生。此刻烛光给她的脸点染上欢快。她对自己方才短暂的悲哀只字不提，只拍起手来，倒叫他瞪着她，惊异于她心绪转得这样快。

“我喜欢这样，”她说，“我们喝茶吧。有人说话我真高兴——哪怕是你。”她走到碗柜前，开始往外拿碟子、茶杯、一条面包、一点黄油，又灌满水壶坐上火。她用自豪而虔敬的手指从碗柜里捧出一只茶叶罐，捧弄得像捧一只金匣。

“自从离家，”他慢慢地说，“我就没这样喝过茶……可我一直想望。”他迟疑了一下。“真怪，你会这样款待我，像个朋友。”

===Original===
Having pulled the only two chairs which the room held up to the fire, she regarded him with sombre amusement. “Am I treating you like a friend?” she asked. “I can’t tell. I’ve never had one.”

He had a sudden wish to tell her everything, from what he was fleeing and for what cause, but caution and a feeling of peace restrained him. He wished to forget it himself and cling only to this growing sense of intimacy, of two minds moving side by side, and watch the firelight gleam downwards into the dark amber of the tea.

“It’s strange,” he said, “how often I’ve longed for a tea like this. In a rough, hurrying sort of life with men, one longs sometimes for refinement—and tea seems to me a symbol of that—peace, security, women, idle talk—and the night outside.”

===Chinese===
她把屋里仅有的两把椅子拖到火前，带着一种阴郁的趣味打量他。“我这是把你当朋友待吗？”她问。“我说不上。我从没有过朋友。”

他忽然想把一切都说给她听：自己在逃什么，为何而逃；可是谨慎和一种安宁之感把他拦住了。他自己也想忘掉那一切，只攀住这渐渐生长的亲密感，攀住两颗心并肩同行的感觉，望着火光向下闪进茶的深琥珀色里。

“真奇怪，”他说，“多少回我盼着这样喝一杯茶。在那种跟男人们一道粗鲁匆忙的生活里，人有时渴望一点精致——在我看来茶就是它的象征——和平，安稳，女人，闲谈——还有外面的夜。”

===Original===
“A loaf of bread,” she said, “no jam, no cakes.”

“That’s nothing,” he brooded over the thick china cup, which he held awkwardly with an unaccustomed hand.

“Why are you here?” she asked. “You don’t belong. You should be a student, I think. You look like a man who daydreams.”

“Doesn’t even a student need courage?” he questioned bitterly. “And I’m not a dreamer. I hate dreams.”

“Is there anything you care for or want?” She watched him as though he were a new and curious animal.

===Chinese===
“一条面包，”她说，“没有果酱，没有点心。”

“那不算什么。”他俯身对着那厚壁的瓷杯出神，用一只不惯此物的手笨拙地握着它。

“你为什么会落到这里？”她问。“你不属于这一行。我看你本该去念书。你这人看上去像个白日梦做个没完的。”

“难道念书的就不需要勇气？”他苦涩地质问。“何况我不是做梦的人。我恨梦。”

“可有一样东西是你在乎的、想要的？”她望着他，像望一头新奇的野兽。

===Original===
“To be null and void,” he said without hesitation.

“Dead?”

The sound of the word seemed to draw his eyes to the window, which stared now on complete darkness.

“No, no,” he said, “not that.” He gave a small shiver and spoke again. “When music plays, one does not see or think; one hardly hears. A bowl—and the music is poured in until there is no ‘I,’ I *am* the music.”

“But why, why,” she asked, “did you ever come to live like this?” and with a small gesture of her hand she seemed to enclose his fear, his misery, his fugitive body and mind.

“My father did it before me,” he replied.

===Chinese===
“归于无效，化为乌有。”他毫不犹豫。

“死？”

这个字的声响似乎把他的眼睛牵向窗口；窗子此刻正瞪着一片纯粹的黑。

“不，不，”他说，“不是那个。”他轻轻打了个寒噤，又说下去。“音乐奏起来的时候，人看不见，也不想；连听也几乎听不见。一只碗——音乐注进来，直到没有‘我’，我*就是*音乐。”

“可是为什么，为什么，”她问，“你偏要过这样的日子？”她手做了个小小的动作，仿佛把他的恐惧、他的悲惨、他逃亡的身子与心神一并拢在了里面。

“我父亲在我之前就这样，”他答道。

===Original===
“Was that all?” she asked.

“No, I was fascinated,” he said. “There’s a man I know with a voice as near to music as any voice I’ve ever heard,” he hesitated and then looked up at her, “except for yours.”

She paid no attention to the compliment, but frowning a little at the fire nipped her lip between small sharp teeth.

“Can’t he help you now that you are in trouble?” she asked. “Can’t you go to him?”

===Chinese===
“就这些？”她问。

“不，还有，是那种生活迷住了我，”他说。“我认识一个人，他的嗓音，就我所听过的嗓音而论，再没有比它更接近音乐的了，”他顿了顿，抬头望着她，“除了你的。”

她对这句恭维全不在意，只对着火微微蹙眉，用细小尖利的牙齿轻咬嘴唇。

“如今你有难，他不能帮你吗？”她问。“你不能去找他？”

===Original===
He stared at her in amazement. He had forgotten that she was ignorant of his story and of his flight from Carlyon, and because he had forgotten, her remark came to him with the force of a wise suggestion. “Andrews, Andrews,” an echo of a soft melancholy voice reached him. “Why are you frightened? It’s Carlyon, merely Carlyon.” The voice was tipped always with the cool, pure poetry which it loved. Why, indeed, should he not go to Carlyon and confess the wrong he had done and explain? That voice could not help but understand. He would go as the woman who had sinned to Christ, and the comparison seemed to him to carry no blasphemy, so strong was the impulse to rise and go to the door and go out into the night.

===Chinese===
他惊愕地瞪着她。他忘了她并不知道他的来历，不知道他从卡莱昂那里逃了出来；正因为忘了，她这句话落在他身上，便带着一个明智建议的全部力量。“安德鲁斯，安德鲁斯，”一个柔和而忧郁的声音的回响追上了他。“你怕什么？那是卡莱昂，不过是卡莱昂。”那声音的尖端，从来缀着它所爱的那种清凉、纯净的诗。是啊，他为什么不能去找卡莱昂，认下自己做下的事，解释个明白？那声音不可能不懂。他要去，像那个犯了罪的女人去见基督；这比拟在他心里并不带丝毫亵渎——起身、走出门、走进黑夜的冲动是这样强烈。

===Original===
“Is it of him you are frightened?” she asked, watching the changes in his face. He had thought her voice also near to music, and now he sat still, watching with a strange disinterestedness the two musics come in conflict for the mastery of his movements. One was subtle, a thing of suggestions and of memories; the other, plain, clear-cut, ringing. One spoke of a dreamy escape from reality; the other was reality, deliberately sane. If he stayed sooner or later he must face his fear; if he went he left calmness, clarity, instinctive wisdom for a vague and uncertain refuge. How would Carlyon greet his confession? Carlyon was a romantic with his face in the clouds, who hated any who gave him contact with a grubby earth. Andrews remembered suddenly, his mind still drifting between the two differing musics, another Carlyon, a Carlyon who had shot one of his own men in the back, because on a cargo-running night the man had raped a young girl. No trouble followed, for the man had been a coward and unpopular in a crew of men, who with all their faults and villainies, had the one virtue of courage. Andrews remembered Carlyon’s face, as he stepped back from the dark bundle where it lay on a beach silvered by the moon. The thoughtful eyes which peered from the apelike skull had been suffused with disgust and a kind of disillusionment. They had re-embarked with the utmost speed, lest the shot should have aroused the revenue men, but Carlyon was the last to enter the boat. He came with evident reluctance like a man who had left a lover on land, and he had indeed left a lover, whom he did not see again for many weeks, a dear and romantic illusion of adventure.

===Chinese===
“你怕的就是他？”她问，望着他脸上神色的变幻。他原也觉得她的嗓音近于音乐，此刻他静坐着，以一种奇特的超然，看两股音乐交战，争夺支配他行动的权柄。一股幽微，是暗示与记忆之物；另一股平实、清朗、铮然作响。一股说着一场逃离现实的迷梦；另一股就是现实，刻意地清醒。留下，他迟早要直面他的恐惧；走，他便是丢下平静、清晰、直觉的智慧，去换一个缥缈不定的藏身处。卡莱昂会怎样接待他的忏悔？卡莱昂是个把脸埋进云里的浪漫派，恨一切让他沾到龌龊尘土的人。安德鲁斯的心神还在两股不同的音乐之间漂流，忽然记起另一个卡莱昂——一个曾从背后枪杀自己手下的卡莱昂，因为在一个走货的夜晚，那人强暴了一个年轻姑娘。事后没有起任何风波，因为那人是个懦夫，在一船人里不得人心；那一船人虽然有种种过失和恶行，却有唯一的美德：勇气。安德鲁斯记得卡莱昂的脸，那时他从沙滩上那捆黑黢黢的东西旁退开一步；沙滩在月光下泛着银白。从那猿猴似的头颅里向外窥视的、若有所思的眼睛，浸满了厌恶和一种幻灭。他们以最快的速度重新上船，唯恐枪声惊动了缉私官员；但卡莱昂是最后一个进船的。他上船时分明怀着不情愿，像个把情人留在岸上的人；而他也的确留下了一个情人，此后许多星期不曾再见——一个关于冒险的亲爱的、浪漫的幻象。

===Original===
“Andrews, Andrews,” the voice had lost its charm. That music was spell-less, for Andrews remembered now that it was with the same soft melancholy regret that Carlyon had spoken to the offending smuggler. Pointing out to the sea he had said: “Look here. Can you tell me what that is?” and the man had turned his back to scan a waste of small ridges, which formed, advanced, fell and receded, and continued so to form, advance, fall and recede, as his eyes glazed in death.

“I can’t go to him,” he said aloud.

===Chinese===
“安德鲁斯，安德鲁斯，”那声音已经失去了魅力。那音乐失了魔咒，因为安德鲁斯此刻记起，卡莱昂当初对那个犯了事的走私者说话，用的正是同一种柔和的忧郁与惋惜。他指向大海说：“看那儿。你能告诉我那是什么？”那人便转过背去，望着一望无际的小小浪脊：它们涌起、推进、塌落、退去，又继续涌起、推进、塌落、退去，直到他的眼睛在死亡里蒙上翳障。

“我不能去找他。”他说出了声。

===Original===
“But if he came to you? …” she asked, as though she intended to make up a quarrel between two schoolboys on their dignity.

“No, no,” he said, and suddenly rose with a poignant, stabbing sense of fear. “What’s that?” he whispered. Elizabeth leant forward in her chair listening. “You are imagining things,” she said.

With unexpected brutality he struck her hand, as it lay on the table, with his fist, so that she caught her breath with pain. “Can’t you whisper?” he asked. “Do you want to tell the whole world there’s someone here? There, didn’t you hear that?” And this time she thought that she could hear a very faint stir of gravel no louder than a rustle of leaves. She nodded her head slowly. “There’s someone moving on the path,” she murmured. The hand which he had struck stiffened into a small and resolute fist.

“For God’s sake,” Andrews muttered, staring round him. She jerked a finger at the door which led to the shed where he had slept the previous night. He half ran to it on tiptoe, and as he looked back, he saw that she had again taken up the stocking which he had dropped unused upon the floor. The red glow of the fire struck upwards and tinged with colour her serene, pale face. Then he closed the door and stood in the dark of the shed, giving occasional rapid shivers like a man in a fever.

===Chinese===
“可要是他来找你呢？……”她问，口气像有意给两个端着架子闹别扭的小学生讲和。

“不，不，”他说，猛地站起身，一种尖锐刺骨的恐惧攫住了他。“那是什么声音？”他低语。伊丽莎白在椅里探身细听。“你疑神疑鬼，”她说。

他猝然显出蛮横，一拳砸在她搁在桌上的手上，砸得她痛得倒抽一口气。“你就不能小点声？”他问。“你要向全世界嚷嚷这儿有人吗？听，方才那声音你没听见？”这一回她觉得自己当真能听见一声极轻的砂砾响动，不比树叶的簌簌声更响。她缓缓点头。“小路上有人在走，”她低声说。挨打的那只手攥紧了，成了一只小小的、决绝的拳头。

“看在上帝分上，”安德鲁斯低语着，环顾四周。她朝那扇通向棚屋的门猛地一指——昨夜他就睡在那棚屋里。他踮着脚尖半跑过去；回头看时，他看见她已经重新拿起那只长袜——那只他没有用过、丢在地板上的袜子。火的红光向上投射，给她安详苍白的脸染上颜色。随后他掩上门，站在棚屋的黑暗里，一阵阵急促地打寒噤，像个发着高热的人。

===Original===
The next sound he heard was Carlyon’s voice. Its suddenness pierced him. He had expected at least to have been given warning, and time to brace his knees and heart, if by no more than a knock or the click of a lifting latch.

It penetrated to him through keyhole and crack, kindly and reassuring. “Forgive me,” it said. “I’m completely lost in this fog.”

Countering the deceptive music with its own clear tone, Elizabeth’s voice struck against Carlyon’s like sword against sword. “Why didn’t you knock?” it said.

===Chinese===
他听见的下一个声音，是卡莱昂的嗓音。它的猝然把他刺透了。他原以为至少会得到一点预警，有时间绷紧膝头和心，哪怕只是一声敲门，或者门闩抬起的一声轻响。

那声音穿过钥匙孔和门缝渗到他这里，和善而令人安心。“原谅我，”它说，“这雾把我完全弄迷了。”

伊丽莎白的声音用它自身清亮的调门抵挡那惑人的音乐，与卡莱昂的声音铮然相击，如剑对剑。“你怎么不敲门？”它说。

===Original===
Had she realised, Andrews wondered, listening intently in the dark, that this was the man he feared. He searched a frightened mind in vain for some way of warning her. He could imagine Carlyon’s apelike face gazing at her with a disarming frankness. “One can’t be too careful around here,” he said. His voice sounded a little nearer as though he had come over to the fire. “You are not alone?” he asked.

Andrews put his hand to his throat. Something had betrayed him. Perhaps as he stood like a blind man in the dark she was giving away his hiding place soundlessly with a wink, a lift of the eyebrow. He had a momentary impulse to fling open the door and rush at Carlyon. It would at least be man against man with no odds, he thought, until the unsleeping inner critic taunted him: “You are not a man.” At least a coward can have cunning, he protested, and kneeling down on the floor, he put his eye to the keyhole. It was a moment before he could find the position of the speakers. Elizabeth was sitting in her chair, hand thrust in the stocking, calmly looking for holes. She is overacting her calmness, he thought fearfully. Carlyon stood over her watching her with an apparent mixture of reverence and regret. He made a small motion towards the two cups, which stood with brazen effrontery upon the table.

===Chinese===
安德鲁斯在黑暗里凝神细听，心里嘀咕：她是否已意识到，这就是他害怕的那个人。他在一颗吓慌了的心里徒然搜寻提醒她的法子。他能想见卡莱昂那张猿猴脸，正带着令人解除戒备的坦率望着她。“这一带不能不多加小心，”他说。他的声音听上去近了些，仿佛已走到火边。“屋里没有别人？”他问。

安德鲁斯把手按上喉咙。有什么东西出卖了他。也许正当他像个盲人似的立在黑暗里，她正使着眼色、挑着眉梢，无声地泄露他的藏身之处。他一时冲动，想推门直冲出去，扑向卡莱昂。至少那是人与人对抗，谁也不占便宜，他想；直到那不眠的内心批评者讥诮他：“你不是个男人。”懦夫至少还剩得起诡诈，他抗辩道，于是跪在地板上，把眼睛凑上钥匙孔。过了一会儿他才找到说话人的方位。伊丽莎白坐在椅里，一只手插在长袜中，从容地找着破洞。她的镇定演得太过了，他惶恐地想。卡莱昂俯身立在她跟前望着她，神色分明是敬意与惋惜的混合。他朝桌上那两只杯子轻轻一指——它们正厚颜无耻地在桌上摆着。

===Original===
She finished her search of the stocking and laid it on her lap. “I am alone,” she said. “My brother has just gone out. He is not far,” she added. “I can easily call to him, if you don’t go.”

Carlyon smiled. “You must not be afraid of me,” he said. “Perhaps I know your brother. Is he a little over the middle height, slightly built, dark, with frightened obstinate eyes?”

“That’s not my brother,” Elizabeth said. “He is short and squat—and very strong.”

“Then I am not looking for your brother.” He picked up one of the cups. “He must have been here very lately,” he said. “The tea is hot. And he left in a hurry with his tea unfinished. Curious that we did not meet.” He gazed round the room with no attempt to hide his curiosity.

===Chinese===
她搜完长袜，把它搁在膝上。“就我一个人，”她说。“我哥哥刚出去。他走不远，”她补了一句。“你若不走，我一叫就能叫应他。”

卡莱昂笑了。“你不必怕我，”他说。“说不定我认得你哥哥。他是不是比中等个儿略高些，瘦瘦的，黑黑的，一双眼睛又惊惶又固执？”

“那不是我哥哥，”伊丽莎白说。“他又矮又壮——力气很大。”

“那我要找的就不是你哥哥。”他拿起一只杯子。“他必定刚来过不久，”他说，“茶还烫着。而且他走得慌忙，茶都没喝完。奇怪，我们竟没有碰上。”他环视屋子，毫不掩饰自己的好奇。

===Original===
“That is my cup you have,” Elizabeth said. “Will you allow me to finish it?”

Andrews kneeling by the keyhole put up his hand to ease his collar as Elizabeth’s lips touched the cup and drained what he had left. A strange loving cup, he thought bitterly, but his bitterness vanished before a wave of humility which for one moment even cleared his mind of its consciousness of fear. He had been kneeling to gain a view of the room beyond, but now in heart he knelt to her. She is a saint, he thought. The charity and courage with which she hid him from his enemy he had taken for granted; but to his muddled unstraight mind the act of drinking from the same cup came with a surprising nobility. It touched him where he was most open to impression; it struck straight at his own awareness of cowardice. Kneeling in the dark not only of the room but of his spirit he imagined that with unhesitating intimacy she had touched his lips and defiled her own.

“I didn’t meet your brother,” Carlyon repeated, still with a touch of regretful tenderness.

===Chinese===
“你拿的是我的杯子，”伊丽莎白说。“容我把它喝完行吗？”

安德鲁斯跪在钥匙孔旁，抬手松了松衣领；这时伊丽莎白的唇触到杯沿，把他剩下的茶一饮而尽。一只奇异的共饮之杯，他辛酸地想；可这辛酸随即退避于一阵谦卑的浪潮之前，那浪潮有一瞬甚至涤净了他的心，使它意识不到恐惧。他跪下原是为了窥看那边的屋子，此刻在心地里，他是在向她下跪。她是个圣徒，他想。她掩护他躲开仇敌的那份仁慈与勇气，他一直视作理所当然；可对他那混乱而不正直的心智来说，同杯共饮之举带着一种惊人的高贵直逼过来。它击中了他最易受触动的地方；它直直打在他自己那份怯懦的意识上。跪在黑暗里——不只是屋子的黑暗，还有他灵魂的黑暗——他想象她曾毫无迟疑地、亲昵地触到他的唇，玷污了她自己的。

“我没有碰上你哥哥，”卡莱昂又说了一遍，语气里仍带着一点惋惜的温柔。

===Original===
“There is another door,” she said without hesitation. Carlyon turned, and to Andrews watching through the keyhole their eyes seemed to meet. His humility and trust vanished as quickly as they had arisen. Carlyon made a step towards the door. She’s betrayed me, Andrews thought, and with fumbling panic-stricken fingers he sought for his knife. Yet he did not dare to open it, even when he had found it, lest the click should make itself heard through the closed door. Carlyon seemed to be staring straight at him. It was incredible that he could not see the eye which watched him through the keyhole, yet he hesitated, nonplussed perhaps as Andrews had been by the girl’s courage, thinking she must have help somewhere, that there must be a trap laid. Then she spoke again carelessly and without hurry, leaning forward to warm her hands at the fire. “It’s no use going there,” she said. “He locked the door as he went out.”

===Chinese===
“还有一扇门，”她毫不迟疑地说。卡莱昂转过身；在钥匙孔外窥看的安德鲁斯看来，两人的目光似乎碰了个正着。他的谦卑与信任，来得快去得也快。卡莱昂朝那扇门跨了一步。她出卖了我，安德鲁斯想，惊慌失措的手指笨拙地摸索他的刀。可是即便摸到了，他也不敢把刀打开，怕那一声轻响穿过关着的门被人听见。卡莱昂似乎正直勾勾地瞪着他。他竟看不见钥匙孔里那只窥视的眼睛，真是不可思议；可他踌躇了，也许像安德鲁斯一样被那姑娘的勇气弄懵了，心想她必有帮手在什么地方，必是设下了圈套。这时她又开口了，随意从容，探身向火暖着手。“去那边也没用，”她说，“他出去时把门锁上了。”

===Original===
For the man in the dark there was a moment of suspense, while Carlyon hesitated. He had only to try the door for all to be discovered. Finally, he refrained. In part perhaps it was because he feared a trap, but his chief reason must have been that embarrassing streak of chivalry which would not allow him to show openly his doubt of a woman’s word. He turned away and stood in the middle of the room in almost pathetic perplexity. If he had known beforehand that there was a woman to be dealt with, he would have sent one of his companions to the cottage in his place, the small, cunning cockney Harry, or the elephantine Joe.

She regarded him with faint amusement from his receding forehead and deep sunk eyes to the strange contrast of his small lightly poised feet. “You are very muddy,” she commented and cast a pathetic glance towards the floor, still fresh and clean from Mrs. Butler’s scrubbing.

===Chinese===
对黑暗中的那个人来说，这是一刻悬心，卡莱昂迟疑着。他只消去试一试那扇门，一切便全败露。终于他忍住了。一部分也许因为他怕中圈套，但主要的缘故，想必是他骨子里那一段令人难堪的骑士气，不容许他把对一个女人的话的怀疑公开表露出来。他转过身去，站在屋子中央，那狼狈的困窘几乎叫人心酸。倘若事先知道要对付的是个女人，他本会派一个同伙替他来这小屋——那个狡猾的小个子伦敦佬哈里（cockney Harry），或是大象般的乔（Joe）。

她带着淡淡的兴味打量他，从他后削的额头、深陷的眼睛，一直看到他那一双小巧而轻稳站立的脚，那双脚与他的身形成了奇怪的对照。“你这一身泥，”她评论道，朝地板投去怜惜的一眼——那地板经巴特勒夫人（Mrs. Butler）擦洗，还清爽干净着。

===Original===
“I am sorry,” he said, “very sorry. The fact is …”

“Don’t trouble to invent a lie,” she murmured abstractedly, her attention seeming to wander to the glowing heart of the fire. “You are looking for someone. Anybody can tell that. Unless you are flying from someone like the other man.”

“The other man?” he leant a little forward with excitement, and Andrews once again prepared himself for betrayal. The act of drinking from his cup, which had filled him with such humility, seemed now to underline what he considered the vileness of her treachery.

“The man you described,” she said, “the frightened, obstinate man.”

“He’s here?” Andrews could hardly catch Carlyon’s whisper. Carlyon’s right hand had hidden itself in an inner pocket.

===Chinese===
“对不起，”他说，“很对不起。事实是……”

“不必费神编瞎话，”她心不在焉地喃喃道，注意力似乎飘去了火那发亮的中心。“你在找一个人。谁都看得出来。除非你也是在躲什么人，像先前那个男人。”

“先前那个男人？”他微微前倾，来了劲头；安德鲁斯再一次做好准备，等她出卖自己。方才那同杯共饮之举曾使他那样谦卑，此刻回想起来，倒像是给他认定的她的背叛之卑劣，重重画了一道着重线。

“就是你形容的那个人，”她说，“那个惊惶的、固执的人。”

“他在这儿？”卡莱昂的低语轻得让安德鲁斯几乎捉不住。卡莱昂的右手已经藏进了内袋。

===Original===
“He slept here last night,” she said.

“And now?”

“He went with the morning, north, I think, but I don’t know.”

“Yes, that’s true,” Carlyon murmured. “He nearly ran into me, but escaped again in this wretched fog. He may return here then.”

===Chinese===
“他昨夜睡在这儿，”她说。

“现在呢？”

“他天一亮就走了，往北去了，我想，可我说不准。”

“对，这不错，”卡莱昂低声道。“他差点撞上我，又在这下作的雾里逃掉了。这么说他还可能回到这儿来。”

===Original===
She laughed. “I don’t think so,” and pointed to the corner where the unloaded gun stood. “Fear,” she added, “and shame.”

“And your brother?” he asked with a sudden, quick remnant of suspicion.

“He was not here last night, but I warned your friend that he would be here tonight. Shall I warn you?” she added.

“I am not afraid,” Carlyon answered, “nor ashamed.”

She looked again at his muddy clothes. “But you too are flying,” she asked, “from something?”

===Chinese===
她笑了。“我看不会，”她指着屋角那支没装弹药的枪说。“恐惧，”她补上一句，“还有羞耻。”

“那你哥哥呢？”他问，残存的疑心倏地一闪。

“他昨夜不在这儿，可我警告过你的朋友，说他今夜会在这儿。要我也警告你一声吗？”她补了一句。

“我不害怕，”卡莱昂答道，“也不羞耻。”

她又看了看他那一身泥。“可你也在逃，”她问，“也在躲着什么？”

===Original===
“From the law,” Carlyon replied with unhesitating frankness, “not from my friends—or from myself,” he added with brooding thoughtfulness.

“Why all this fuss?” she asked, her eyes, kindled in the red reflected glow of the fire, gazing up at him with passionate sincerity and condemning, in an equal judgment, his mud, his flight, his search.

He watched her with fascination and with a kind of difficulty, as though trying to cling with his eyes to some bright object obscurely shining at the bottom of a dark and deep well. “He’s a sort of Judas,” he said softly and reluctantly.

“He didn’t seem to be a man with money,” she said. “Are you certain?”

“No. But if I could meet him, I should know in a moment. He hasn’t the courage to hide anything.” He shivered slightly as a cold draught insinuated itself under the door.

===Chinese===
“躲法律，”卡莱昂回答，坦然毫不犹豫，“不是躲朋友——也不是躲我自己，”他补上这一句，沉思郁结。

“何必这样兴师动众？”她问。她的眼睛在火光红色的反照里点亮，仰望着他，热烈而诚恳，以同等的裁判，判下他的泥、他的逃亡、他的搜寻。

他望着她，着迷，又带着一种吃力，仿佛要用眼睛攀住一口又黑又深的井底隐约发亮的一件东西。“他是个犹大一流的角色，”他轻声说，说得不情愿。

“他看上去不像个有钱的人，”她说。“你拿得准吗？”

“拿不准。可只要让我碰上他，我立刻就能知道。他没有胆子把任何事藏在心里。”一阵冷风悄悄从门底下钻进来，他微微一颤。

===Original===
“You are cold,” Elizabeth said. “Come to the fire.” He looked at her for a moment as though amazed at her friendliness and then advanced to the fire and let the heat and flame stain his hands a red gold. “Why can’t you leave him alone?” she asked. “Is he worth the trouble and risk?”

Out of their deep sockets Carlyon’s eyes peered cautiously, as though he wondered how far it was possible to make this serene stranger understand. “I knew him very well,” he said hesitatingly. “We were friends. He must have known me well. Now I hate him. I’m certain that this is hate.”

Her voice touched him like a slow warm flame. “Tell me,” she said.

He looked at her again with that impression of amazement slowly welling up from a dark, deeply hidden source. “You have a lovely voice,” he said. “It is just as though you were ready to play music to any stranger. You know who I am?” he asked.

“One of the Gentlemen,” she said, and waited.

===Chinese===
“你冷了，”伊丽莎白说。“到火边来。”他看了她片刻，仿佛惊异于她的友善，随后走到火前，任热气与火光把他的手染成赤金色。“你们为什么不能放过他？”她问。“他值得这些周折和风险吗？”

卡莱昂的眼睛从深深的眼窝里谨慎地窥视，仿佛在掂量，能让这位安详的陌生人明白到什么地步。“我跟他很熟，”他迟疑地说。“我们原是朋友。他也一定很熟我。如今我恨他。我拿得准，这是恨。”

她的声音触到他，像一朵缓慢而温热的火苗。“说给我听，”她说。

他再度望着她，那份惊异又从一处幽暗深藏的源头里慢慢涌上来。“你的声音很美，”他说。“简直像你随时预备给随便哪个陌生人奏乐。你知道我是谁吗？”他问。

“那班『绅士』（the Gentlemen）里的一位，”她说，等着下文。

===Original===
“So was the man who was here last night. We were friends. I told him things I would not tell anyone now—what things I loved and why. And after three years with us he betrayed us to the law.”

“Are you sure of that?”

“Someone must have done,” he said. “Six men are in gaol on a charge of murder. There was a fight and a gauger was shot, poor devil. Four of us escaped; the two men who are with me, and Andrews, who has done his best to avoid us. And when did he escape? Before we were surprised. I’m certain of that. Why is he afraid of meeting me? I know he is.” His eyes, having taken a sad, suspicious gaze at the world, seemed to hide themselves yet deeper in his skull. “You will not understand,” he said, “how he has spoilt everything. It was a rough life, but there seemed something fine in it—adventure, courage, high stakes. Now we are a lot of gaol-birds, murderers. Doesn’t it seem mean to you,” he cried suddenly, “that a man should be shot dead over a case of spirits? What a dull, dirty game it makes it all appear.”

===Chinese===
“昨夜来的那个人也是。我们原是朋友。我告诉过他一些如今我对谁也不会讲的事——我爱些什么，为什么爱。跟我们混了三年，他把我出卖给了法律。”

“你拿得准是这个缘故？”

“总得有人干的，”他说。“六个人下了狱，罪名是谋杀。动了一场手，一个缉私税吏吃了枪子，可怜虫。我们四个逃了出来：跟我在一起的那两个，还有安德鲁斯——他用尽力气躲开我们。而且他是什么时候溜的？在我们被突袭堵住之前。这一点我拿得准。他为什么怕见我？我知道他怕。”他的眼睛朝这世界投了忧伤而疑忌的一瞥，随即仿佛往头颅深处躲得更深。“你不会明白，”他说，“他把一切毁成什么样子了。那本是一种粗粝的营生，可里面像有点高贵的东西——冒险，胆气，孤注一掷。如今我们成了一窝牢囚、杀人犯。一个人为了一箱烈酒给打死在地，你不觉得下作吗，”他忽然喊道，“这下整场把戏显得多么乏味，多么龌龊。”

===Original===
She looked at him with pity but not with sympathy. “It must have been that all along,” she said.

He shrugged his shoulders. “Yes, but I didn’t know,” he said. “Should I thank him for my enlightenment?”

She smiled at the tendrils of the fire uncurling themselves and folding again in bud. “Is a man’s death and your dream broken worth all this fuss?” she demanded with voice raised a little as though she would carry her protest against man’s stupidity beyond the room and out into the shrouding mist and night.

===Chinese===
她带着怜悯看他，却谈不上同情。“那营生打根上就是这样，”她说。

他耸了耸肩。“是，可我从前不知道，”他说。“我的这番觉悟，该谢谢他吗？”

她对着火微笑，火焰的卷须舒展开来，又蜷回成花苞。“一个人的死，加上你破灭的梦，值得这样大动干戈吗？”她提高了一点嗓音责问，仿佛要把她对人之愚蠢的抗辩带出这间屋子，带进外面包裹一切的雾与夜里去。

===Original===
“You are so sane,” he said sadly. “You women are all so sane. A dream is often all there is to a man. I think that you are lovely, good and full of pity, but that is only a dream. You know all about yourself, how you are greedy for this and that, afraid of insects, full of disgusting physical needs. You’ll never find a man who will love you for anything but a bare, unfilled-in outline of yourself. A man will even forget his own details when he can, until he appears an epic hero, and it needs his woman to see that he’s a fool. Only a woman can love a real person.”

“You may be right,” she said, “though I don’t understand most of it. I once knew a man, though, who so forgot his own details as you call them, that he believed himself a coward and nothing else.”

“That’s less common,” Carlyon answered. “Women generally show us up to ourselves and we hate them for it. I suppose that man would love the woman who showed him up.”

She suddenly dropped her seriousness and laughed. “Poor man,” she mocked, “and you hate this friend of yours because he’s shown you up. What a fool you are to waste your time on such a hate.”

===Chinese===
“你太清醒了，”他忧伤地说。“你们女人全都太清醒。一个男人身上，常常只剩下一个梦。我想你可爱、善良、满怀怜悯，可那不过是个梦。你对自己知道得一清二楚：你贪这个恋那个，怕虫子，浑身是叫人恶心的生理需要。你永远找不到一个男人，会爱你身上别的什么；他爱的至多是你那副光秃秃的、没填细节的轮廓。男人只要能忘，连自己的细节也一并忘掉，直到俨然一位史诗英雄；要他的女人来看明白，他其实是个傻瓜。只有女人能爱一个真实的人。”

“也许你对，”她说，“尽管大部分我听不懂。不过我从前认得一个人，他把自己那些你所谓的细节忘得太干净，以至于相信自己除了是个懦夫，什么也不是。”

“那倒少见些，”卡莱昂答道。“女人一般把我们自己的原形揭给我们看，我们为此恨她们。我想那个男人该会爱那个揭他的女人。”

她忽然收起严肃，笑出了声。“可怜的人，”她揶揄道，“你恨你这位朋友，就因为他揭了你的底。为这样一场恨糟蹋光阴，你可真是个傻瓜。”

===Original===
He made a small motion with his hands towards the fire, as though he wished to seize its light and heat, and bear them to his brain. “Yes,” he said. “I hate him,” and then waited, with his eyes peeping, as it were beseechingly, from his low skull in a longing to be convinced of his own futility and of his own hate.

“But what, after all, could you do if you met him?” she protested.

“I should make sure that I was right,” he answered, “and then I should kill him.”

“And what would be the use of that?” she asked.

===Chinese===
他朝火的方向轻轻伸出两手，仿佛想抓住它的光和热，捧到自己的脑子里去。“是的，”他说。“我恨他。”随后他等着，眼睛从低矮的头颅里觑望出来，近乎乞怜，巴望有人来说服他：他是无用的，他的恨也是无用的。

“可你当真碰上了他，又能怎么样呢？”她抗声道。

“我要先确证自己没有恨错，”他答道，“然后我就杀了他。”

“那又有什么用？”她问。

===Original===
He edged a little away from her and threw back his head, as though he were protecting something infinitely dear. “There would be no use,” he said, “no use, but I have a mission.”

He saw her lift eyes full of a pleading friendship. “You are in danger of something worse than the law,” she said.

He looked at her with suspicion. “Why all these arguments?” he asked. “Did you like the man?” He eyed her with regret and disgust as he would have done a lovely picture soiled with ordure. “Did you get fond of him in a night?”

“No,” she said simply. “But I have lived with hate since I was a child. Why don’t you escape from the country? If you stay you’ll only injure yourself or else something you never intended to harm. That’s always the way.”

===Chinese===
他从她身边悄悄挪开一点，仰起头，仿佛在护卫什么无限珍爱的东西。“是没有用，”他说，“没有用，可我负有一桩使命。”

他看见她抬起眼睛，盛满恳求的友情。“你有陷入比法律更糟的境地的危险，”她说。

他疑心地望她。“何必这样争来争去？”他问。“你喜欢那个人？”他带着遗憾和厌恶端详她，像端详一幅被污物涂脏的可爱的画。“你一夜之间就跟他有了情分？”

“没有，”她平平地说。“可我从孩子时候起就跟恨住在一起。你为什么不逃出国去？留下，你毁的只会是你自己，不然就是一样你从未想伤害的东西。向来是这样。”

===Original===
He took no notice of her words, but watched her face with curiosity and fascination. “If I could take you with me,” he murmured, “I should have with me peace and charity. Have you noticed,” he said softly, his eyes peering like a dog’s between the bars of a cage, “how in the middle of a storm there’s always a moment of silence?” He half raised his arms as though he were about to protest at the necessity which drove him back into the storm, and then let them drop in a kind of tired despair.

“You are free,” she whispered, her eyes watching him not through bars but through the gold mist which the flames of the fire shed, “you are not bound.”

He shrugged his shoulders and said with a resentful carelessness. “Oh, there’s no peace for me.” He turned on his heel decisively, but he had taken only three steps to the door, when he came back.

===Chinese===
她的话他没有理会，只带着好奇和着迷看她的脸。“要是能带你一起走，”他喃喃道，“我身边就有了和平与仁慈。你可曾留意，”他轻声说，眼睛像狗一样从笼栏间向外窥望，“暴风雨的当中，总有一刻是寂静的？”他半抬起双臂，像要抗议那把他赶回暴风雨里去的必然，随后又把手垂下，带着一种疲惫的绝望。

“你是自由的，”她低语，眼睛望着他，不是隔着栏杆，而是隔着火焰洒下的金色雾霭，“你不受束缚。”

他耸耸肩，愤懑而满不在乎地说：“哦，我这样的人没有太平日子。”他断然转身就走，可朝门走了才三步，又折了回来。

===Original===
He did not look at her but said with a touch of embarrassment:

“You say he went north?”

“Yes,” she said.

“Of course. I know that,” he commented. “We nearly met.” He shifted a little on his feet. “I don’t know your name,” he continued. “I don’t want you to come to any harm. If he should come back, you mustn’t shelter him or warn him.”

===Chinese===
他不看她，带着一点窘迫说：

“你说他往北去了？”

“是，”她说。

“当然。这我知道，”他评论道。“我们差点碰上。”他在脚上微微换了换重心。“我还不知道你的名字，”他接着说。“我不愿你受到任何伤害。他要是回来，你不能窝藏他，也不能给他报信。”

===Original===
“Is that a command?” she asked with gentle mockery.

“Yes,” he said, and then added in stumbling haste, “but I will beg you, too. You cannot be mixed up in this. You don’t belong to our world, noise, hate. Stay with peace.”

“Are the two so separate?” she asked.

He listened with his head a little on one side and eyes half-closed, like a man in the presence of a faint music. Then he covered his eyes for a moment with his hand. “You muddle me,” he said.

===Chinese===
“这是命令？”她问，带着温柔的揶揄。

“是，”他说，随即又磕磕绊绊地急忙补上：“可我也求求你。这件事你不能掺和。你不属于我们的世界——喧嚣，仇恨。守着你的和平吧。”

“这两样就那样隔得开吗？”她问。

他偏着头，半闭着眼听，像一个人面对一缕微弱的音乐。随后他用手在眼睛上遮了一霎。“你把我搅糊涂了，”他说。

===Original===
“Are they so separate?” she repeated.

“Let them remain separate,” he said vehemently and bitterly, “you can’t come to us, and it’s too easy for us to come to you.”

“Where are you going?” she asked.

“To look for him,” he answered. “I’ll find him. I know him too well to lose him.”

“And he knows you,” she added.

===Chinese===
“它们就那样隔得开吗？”她又说了一遍。

“让它们分开吧，”他说得激切而辛酸，“你到不了我们那边，我们到你们那边却太容易。”

“你上哪儿去？”她问。

“去找他，”他答。“我会找到他的。我太熟他了，丢不了他。”

“他也熟你，”她说。

===Original===
Carlyon came nearer to her again. “Was he laughing at me the whole time,” he asked, “while we were friends? He’s a coward and cowards are cunning. I told him all the things I liked. I read him things, shared what I loved with him. I can only make him forget what I told him by killing him,” he added with an incongruous pathos.

Elizabeth said: “Were they as secret as that?”

He backed away from her suspiciously, as though he feared that she too had designs on his most intimate thoughts. “I’ve warned you,” he said abruptly. “I won’t bother you any longer. You had better not tell your brother that I have been. I don’t wish any harm to him either.” He turned and walked very quickly to the door, as though he were afraid that some word might delay him further. When he opened the door a cold draught filled the room with smoke and mist. He shivered a little. As he closed it he shut away from himself the sight of Elizabeth’s face, its serenity troubled by a faint and obscure pity.

===Chinese===
卡莱昂又朝她走近了些。“我们做朋友的时候，”他问，“他是不是从头到尾都在笑我？他是个懦夫，而懦夫诡计多端。我把我喜欢的种种都告诉了他。我念东西给他听，把我所爱的与他分享。只有杀了他，才能叫他忘掉我告诉过他的那些话，”他补上这一句，带着一种不相称的哀婉。

伊丽莎白说：“告诉他的那些话，就那么机密吗？”

他疑心地从她跟前退开，仿佛怕她对他的最隐秘的心思也存着企图。“我警告过你了，”他突兀地说。“不再打搅你了。你最好别告诉你哥哥我来过。我也不愿他有任何不测。”他转过身，快步走向门口，仿佛怕哪一句话会再把他绊住。他开门时，一股冷风卷着烟和雾灌满屋子。他微微打了个寒噤。掩门的时候，他把伊丽莎白那张脸也关在了外面——那张安详的脸，正为一丝隐约而暧昧的怜悯所扰动。

### V / 五

===Original===
Andrews put the closed knife back into his pocket. The dark which had been cold to him grew warm with friendliness. He was overwhelmed with an immense gratitude, so that he was unwilling to open the door and remind Elizabeth of his presence. She was as unapproachable to him in this mood as a picture, as holy as a vision. He remembered his first entrance to the cottage and his last sight of her before he sank exhausted, the pale resolute face set between two yellow flames.

Quietly as though he were in the presence of a mystery he turned the handle of the door and remained on the threshold unresolved and diffident. She was standing by the table cleaning the cups and plates which they had left.

“Is that you?” she said, without looking up. “Put these in the cupboard,” and when he had obeyed her she returned to the fire and bending down to poke the coals murmured with a half-amused asperity, “A couple of fools.”

Andrews shifted from one foot to another. He found, faced by this devastating matter of factness, an inability to utter his thanks. He plucked nervously at a button and at last burst out in a tone almost of resentment, “I’m grateful.”

===Chinese===
安德鲁斯（Andrews）把合上的小刀放回衣袋。先前于他冷冰冰的黑暗，此刻透出友爱的暖意。他被一股巨大的感激压倒了，竟不愿推门进去，提醒伊丽莎白（Elizabeth）他还在门外。她此刻于他高不可攀，像一幅画，圣洁得像一个幻象。他记起自己头一回走进这小屋的情形，记起力竭倒下之前望她的最后一眼：那张苍白而坚毅的脸，嵌在两簇黄色的火苗之间。

他悄没声儿地转动门把手，仿佛置身于一件神秘的事物跟前，随即迟疑不决、自惭形秽地留在了门槛上。她正站在桌边，擦拭他们留下的杯盘。

“是你吗？”她说，头也不抬。“把这些收进碗柜去。”等他照办了，她又回到炉火边，弯下腰去拨煤块，半带取笑半带尖刻地咕哝了一句：“一对傻瓜。”

安德鲁斯把重心从这只脚挪到那只脚。面对这份叫人招架不住的实事求是，他发觉自己道不出一句道谢的话来。他神经质地扯弄着一颗纽扣，末了几乎带着怨气冲口而出：“我感激你。”

===Original===
“But what’s it all about?” she asked spreading out her hands in a gesture of humorous perplexity. “I hate mysteries,” she added, herself mysteriously brooding behind dark eyes flecked only on the surface by amusement.

“Didn’t you hear what he said?” Andrews replied and muttered in so low a tone that Elizabeth was forced to lean forward to catch his words, “A sort of Judas.”

“Do you expect me to believe all that he said,” she stared at Andrews in wide-eyed, innocent astonishment. “He’s your enemy.”

“Would you believe what I said?” he asked with angry foreknowledge of the answer.

“Of course,” she said. “Tell me.”

===Chinese===
“可这一切究竟是怎么回事？”她摊开两手，做了个诙谐的困惑姿势。“我恨闷葫芦，”她补上一句，自己却在黑眼睛后面神秘地沉思着，眼睛只表面上沾了一点戏谑的光。

“他说的那些话你没听见吗？”安德鲁斯答道，跟着低声咕哝了一句，低得伊丽莎白不得不探过身来才听得清：“有点像个犹大（Judas）。”

“你指望我把他说的一切都信了？”她瞪着安德鲁斯，眼睛睁得圆圆的，一脸天真的惊愕。“他是你的仇人。”

“那我说的，你就会信？”他问，恼怒地早料到了回答。

“当然，”她说。“讲给我听。”

===Original===
He watched her in amazement. All his sentimental melodramatic instincts rose up in him to take advantage of the occasion. O, the blessed relief, he thought, to stumble forward, go down on my knees to her, weep, say “I am tired out. A hunted man pursued by worse than death.” He could hear his own voice break on the phrase. But as he was about to obey those instincts, that other hard, critical self spoke with unexpected distinctness. “You fool, she’ll see through that. Haven’t you enough gratitude to speak the truth?” But then, he protested, I lose all chance of being comforted. But when he looked at her, the critic won. He stood where he was with hands clasped behind his back and head a little bent, but eyes staring intently, angrily for the first sign of contempt.

“It’s all true,” he said.

“Tell me,” she repeated.

“It’s not a story which would interest you,” he protested, in a vain hope of avoiding further humiliation.

She sat down and leaning her chin on her hand watched him with a friendly amusement.

===Chinese===
他惊愕地望着她。他一身感伤的、情节剧式的本能全都涌了上来，要抓住这个机会大做文章。哦，天赐的解脱啊，他想，踉跄着扑上前去，跪倒在她的脚边，哭，说“我累垮了。一个被追捕的人，被比死亡更可怕的东西追捕着。”他能听见自己的嗓音在这句台词上碎裂。可就在他正要听命于那些本能的时候，另一个冷硬的、爱挑剔的自我却开了口，清晰得出乎意料。“你这傻瓜，那一套她一眼就看穿。你就没有一点感激，肯说句实话？”可那样一来，他分辩道，我就再没有指望得到安慰了。然而他一望她，批评者便赢了。他站在原地，两手背在身后，头微微低着，一双眼睛却紧张地、怒冲冲地盯住她，等头一丝轻蔑的迹象。

“句句是真，”他说。

“讲吧，”她又说一遍。

“我的故事引不起你的兴趣，”他推托着，枉想借此躲开更多的羞辱。

她坐下来，下巴支在手上，带着友善的兴味望着他。

===Original===
“You must earn your night’s lodging with the story,” she said. “Come here.”

“No,” he clung as a desperate resort to a position in which he could at least look physically down upon her. “If I must speak, I’ll speak here.”

He twisted a button round and round till it dangled loosely on its cotton stalk. He did not know how to begin. He shut his eyes and plunged into a rapid current of words.

“We were running spirits from France,” he said, “and I betrayed them. That’s all there is to it. I wrote to the Customs officer at Shoreham and gave a date and an hour and a place. When we landed the gaugers were waiting for us. There was a fight, but I slipped away. It seems that a gauger was killed.” He opened his eyes and gazed at her angrily. “Don’t dare to despise me,” he said. “You don’t know why I did it, my thoughts, feelings. I’m a coward, I know, and none of you can understand a coward. You are all so brave and quiet, peaceful.”

She took no notice of his angry outburst, but watched him thoughtfully. “I wonder why you did it?” she answered.

He shook his head and answered in a kind of deep hopefulness, “You wouldn’t understand.”

===Chinese===
“你得拿故事来挣你这一夜的宿处，”她说。“到这儿来。”

“不，”他死守原地，这是绝境中抓来的一着：这个位置至少能让他从身量上居高临下地看她。“如果非讲不可，我就在这儿讲。”

他把一颗纽扣捻过来捻过去，直捻得它只剩几根棉线，松松地荡着。他不知道怎样开口。他闭上眼睛，一头扎进湍急的话流里去。

“我们当时在跑一批从法国来的烈酒，”他说，“我出卖了他们。就这么回事。我写了信给肖勒姆（Shoreham）缉私局（the Customs）的官员，给了日期、钟点和地点。我们一上岸，缉私税吏（gaugers）已经候着我们了。打了一场，我溜脱了。听说有一个税吏送了命。”他睁开眼睛，怒冲冲地瞪着她。“不许你看不起我，”他说。“你不知道我为什么干这事，不知道我的心思，我的感受。我知道自己是个懦夫，可你们谁也不明白一个懦夫。你们全都那么勇敢，那么沉静，那么心安。”

她并不理会他这一通怒气，只若有所思地望着他。“我倒想不通，你为什么要干呢？”她答道。

他摇了摇头，答话里带着一种深深的冀望：“你不会懂的。”

===Original===
“But why,” Elizabeth asked, “did you ever start smuggling? You are not made for that work.”

“My father was a smuggler,” Andrews said. “A common, bullying smuggler, but damnably clever. He saved money on it and sent me to school. What was the use of having me taught Greek, if I was to spend my life like this?” and his hand in its vague comprehensive gesture included the bare room, the cold night, his muddy clothes and fear. He came a little nearer to the fire.

“I will tell you why he sent me to school,” he said, leaning forward as though to impart a confidence. “It was so that he could brag about it. He was proud of his success. He was never caught and they never had any evidence against him. His crew worshipped him. I tell you he’s become a legend on this coast. I’ve never dared to say these things about him to anyone but you. And all the time I was at sea, I could see how they wondered that such a mountain could bring forth such a mouse.”

“Why do you hate your father so?” Elizabeth asked. “Is it because of this?” and with her hand she imitated the comprehensive gesture which he had made a minute before.

“Oh, no,” he said, “no.” He watched her with a despairing intentness and a hopeless longing for some sign of comprehension. He pleaded with her not as an advocate to a jury but as a prisoner already condemned to his judge. “You can’t understand,” he said, “what life was like with these men. I could do nothing which was not weighed up with my father and found wanting. They kept on telling me of his courage, of what he would have done, what a hero he was. And I knew all the time things they didn’t know, how he had beaten my mother, of his conceit, his ignorance, his beastly bullying ways. They gave me up in the end,” he said smiling without any gaiety. “I was of no account. They were kind to me, charitably, because that man was my father.”

===Chinese===
“可你当初，”伊丽莎白问，“怎么干起走私这一行的？你不是做那事的料。”

“我父亲是个走私的，”安德鲁斯说。“一个粗俗的、恃强凌弱的走私贩子，可精明得要命。他靠这个攒下钱，送我上了学。既然我注定要这样过一辈子，教我希腊文又有什么用？”他的手做了个含糊而包罗一切的手势，把空荡荡的屋子、寒冷的夜、他一身泥污的衣裳连同恐惧都划拉了进去。他朝炉火挪近了一点。

“我告诉你他为什么送我上学，”他说着朝前倾过身来，像要吐露一件心腹话。“为的是他能拿这个吹嘘。他为自己的成功得意。他从没被抓获过，他们手里也从来没有过能定他罪的证据。他的船员们把他奉若神明。我告诉你，他在这一带海岸已经成了传奇。这些话除了你，我对谁也不敢说。而我在海上那么些年，一直看得出来他们心里纳闷：这样一座大山，怎么就生得出这样一只耗子。”

“你为什么这样恨你父亲？”伊丽莎白问。“是不是因为这个？”她用手模仿了他方才那个包罗一切的手势。

“哦，不是，”他说，“不是。”他定定地望着她，那凝视近乎绝望，怀着一种无望的渴求，盼着一点能够领会的迹象。他向她陈情，不像辩护人向陪审团，倒像一个已经定罪的囚犯向他的法官。“你不会明白，”他说，“同这班人过的是什么日子。我做什么，都要拿我父亲来掂量一遍，然后被认定不如他。他们没完没了地对我讲他的胆量，讲换了他会怎样做，讲他是条好汉。而我从头到尾知道他们不知道的事：他怎样打我母亲，他的自负，他的愚昧，他那副畜生似的霸道嘴脸。到头来他们对我死了心，”他说着笑了，笑里没有半点欢意。“我算不得数。他们待我和气，那是行善式的和气，因为那个人是我父亲。”

===Original===
“But why, why,” she asked, “did you ever mix yourself up with them?”

“That was Carlyon,” he said softly, wondering whether the twisted feeling at his heart when he uttered the name was love or hate. It was at any rate something bitter and irrevocable.

“The man who was here?”

“Yes,” Andrews said. “My father was killed at sea and they dropped the body overboard, so that even when he was dead, the law had no evidence against him. I was at school. My mother died a couple of years before. I think he broke her heart, if there’s such a thing as a broken heart. He broke her body anyway.” Andrews’s face grew white as though from the blinding heat of an inner fire. “I loved my mother,” he said. “She was a quiet pale woman who loved flowers. We used to go for walks together in the holidays and collect them from the hedges and ditches. Then we would press them and put them in an album. Once my father was at home—he had been drinking, I think—and he found us. We were so busy that we didn’t hear him when he called. He came and tore the leaves out of the album and scrumpled them in his fists, great unwieldy fists. He was unwieldy altogether, large, clumsy, bearded, but with a quick cunning brain and small eyes.”

“Why did your mother marry him?” Elizabeth asked.

“They eloped,” Andrews said. “My mother was incurably romantic.”

“And when your father died?”

===Chinese===
“可是为什么，为什么，”她问，“你当初偏要把自己搅和他们中间去？”

“那是卡莱昂（Carlyon），”他轻声说，心里纳闷：说出这个名字时心口那一阵拧痛，究竟是爱还是恨。至少，那是一种又苦涩、又无可挽回的东西。

“就是到这儿来的那个人？”

“是的，”安德鲁斯说。“我父亲死在海上，他们把尸首抛下了船，这样他死了，法律照样拿不到他的罪证。我当时在上学。我母亲早两三年就去世了。我想是他碎了她的心——倘若世上真有碎了的心这回事。反正他毁掉了她的身子。”安德鲁斯的脸白了下来，仿佛叫一股内在的火烧得眩目。“我爱我母亲，”他说。“她是个安安静静、脸色苍白的妇人，爱花草。假里我们常一块儿出去走走，从树篱边、沟渠沿上采花。采回来压平了，夹进一本标本册里。有一回父亲在家——我想他是喝了酒——撞见了我们。我们正忙得入神，他喊我们都没听见。他走过来，把标本册里的花叶一页页撕下来，攥在那两只拳头里揉烂——那样两只笨重难使的大拳头。他整个人也是那样笨重：高大，拙夯，满脸胡须，脑子里却转得又快又狡，一双眼睛小小的。”

“你母亲当初为什么嫁他？”伊丽莎白问。

“他们是私奔的，”安德鲁斯说。“我母亲浪漫得没救。”

“那么你父亲死的时候呢？”

===Original===
“That was more than three years ago,” Andrews continued in a tone as tired as though he were speaking of three centuries. “I was finishing school, and Carlyon brought the news. I was glad. You see it appeared to me to mean the end of fear. My father used to beat me unmercifully, because he said it would put courage into me. I think he was a little mad towards the end. My mother’s death frightened him, for he was superstitious. When I heard that he was dead, I thought it was the beginning of a life of peace.”

“And why not?” Elizabeth asked. “Why this?”

He bent his head sullenly. “I was alone,” he said. “I wasn’t sure what to do. Carlyon asked me to come back with him and I went.” He raised his head and said fiercely, “Can’t you understand? You’ve seen the man for yourself? There’s something about him… I was a boy,” he added as though he was an old man discussing a far distant past. “Perhaps I was romantic like my mother. God knows I ought to be cured of that now. He was brave, adventurous and yet he loved music and the things which I loved, colours, scents, all that part of me which I could not speak of at school or to my father. I went with Carlyon. What a fool I was. How could I be such a fool?”

She screwed up her mouth as though at a wry taste. “Yes, but the betrayal?” she said.

He drew himself up, moving a little away. “I don’t expect anyone to understand that,” he said. He gave a momentary impression of great dignity, which he spoilt by an immediate capitulation. “You can’t realise the life I came to,” he said. “There were storms and I was seasick. There were periods of nightlong waiting off the coast for signals which did not come and I could not help showing my nerves. And there was no hope of any change, of any peace at last except death. My father had left his boat and every penny he had saved to Carlyon. That was why Carlyon came to me in Devon. He was curious to see the neglected son, and then I suppose he took pity on me. I believe he liked me,” Andrews added slowly and regretfully with another painful twist at the heart.

===Chinese===
“那是三年多以前的事了，”安德鲁斯接着说，语调疲惫得仿佛在讲三个世纪前的旧事。“我学业将满，是卡莱昂来报的丧。我心里高兴。你明白，在我看，那似乎意味着恐惧到头了。我父亲从前下手无情地揍我，说是好往我身子里打进胆量。我想他到末了有点疯了。我母亲的死吓着了他，因为他迷信。听说他死了，我以为从此可以开始过太平日子了。”

“那怎么没成呢？”伊丽莎白问。“怎么偏偏弄成现在这样？”

他闷闷地垂下头。“我孤身一人，”他说。“我不知道该怎么办。卡莱昂叫我跟他回去，我就去了。”他抬起头，狠狠地说：“难道你不明白？那个人你亲眼见过的？他身上有一样东西……那时候我是个孩子，”他补上一句，口气像个老人在讲一段隔了半世的往事。“也许我像我母亲，也浪漫。上帝知道，如今这毛病我本该治好了。他有胆量，爱冒险，却懂得音乐，懂得我所爱的那些东西——颜色，气味，我身上那一部分没法对学校、也没法对父亲说出口的东西。我就跟了卡莱昂。我多傻啊。我怎么会傻成那样？”

她撇了撇嘴，仿佛尝到一股怪味。“是啊，可那桩出卖呢？”她说。

他挺直了身子，朝旁边挪开了一点。“这个我不指望有谁明白，”他说。那一瞬他显得极有尊严，可紧接着就立刻投降，亲手把这点尊严糟蹋了。“你想不出我过的是一种什么日子，”他说。“遇上风暴我就晕船。有整夜停在近海等信号、信号偏偏不来的时候，我那点紧张怎么也藏不住。而且没有任何改变的指望，除了死，到头也休想有安宁。我父亲把他的船和攒下的每一文钱都留给了卡莱昂。卡莱昂就为这个到德文郡（Devon）来找我。他好奇，想看看那个受冷落的儿子；后来，我想，他就可怜起我来了。我相信他是喜欢我的，”安德鲁斯慢慢地说，语含怅恨，心口又是一阵拧痛。

===Original===
“I thought my father was dead,” he continued, “but I soon found that he had followed me on board. The first member of the crew I met as I was hauled, pushed behind and pulled in front, on to the boat, was Joe, a fat, big, clumsy, stupid creature, a prize bull of a man. ‘You’ll soon get your sea legs, sir,’ he said to me, ‘if you are your father’s son.’ They worshipped my father, all except a little wizened half-witted youth called Tims, whom my father had made his personal servant. My father, I suppose, had bullied him.
