And unless I did something it would have gone on for always and always. And I wanted to show those men that I was someone to be considered, that I had the power to smash all their plans.”

“And to hang them?”

“I never thought of that. I swear it. How could I tell they’d fight?”

===Chinese===

“不许发表演说，”爱德华·帕金爵士用他那又高又倨傲的嗓音叱道。“问你什么就答什么。”

“因为我有一个我恨的父亲，人家总拿他摆在我面前作榜样。这叫我发疯。而且我是个懦夫。这你们全知道。”安德鲁斯抓住证人席的边沿，身子前倾，声音里带着怒气，脸涨得通红，又羞又愧。“我怕挨伤害，我恨那片海，恨那喧闹，恨那危险。要不是我动手做了点什么，这日子就会无休无止地继续下去，永远永远。我要叫那些人知道，我是个不容小看的人，我有本事把他们全部的算计砸个稀烂。”

“还叫他们上绞架呢？”

“那个我从来没想到。我发誓。我怎么知道他们会开枪抵抗？”

===Original===

“And your friend, the man Carlyon? Did you do nothing to warn him?”

“It was a case of him or me.”

A bearded man called Hake in the second row of the prisoners sprang to his feet and shook his fist at Andrews. “It’s him or you still,” he cried. “He’ll get you for this.” A warder pulled him down.

The Court was growing unbearably stuffy. The judge and the ladies in the gallery were fluttering scented handkerchiefs. Andrews’s forehead was hot and sticky with sweat. He wiped it with the palm of his hand. He felt as though he had been standing for hours exposed to the gaze of the Court. His lips were dry and he longed for water. Give me strength to go through with this, he implored silently—not of God but of the image which he carried in his heart and behind which he tried to hide the faces that watched him.

===Chinese===

“那么你的朋友，卡莱昂那个人呢？你竟毫无一点示警之举？”

“那是不容两全的事：不是他，就是我。”

被告第二排一个名叫黑克的大胡子猛地跳起来，朝安德鲁斯挥舞拳头。“到这会儿还是他跟你没完！”他吼道。“他会要你的命。”一个看守把他拽了下去。

法庭里闷得叫人受不住。法官和旁听席上的女士们都在扑扇香手帕。安德鲁斯的额头汗津津地发烫。他用手掌抹了抹。他觉得自己仿佛已经站了几个钟头，赤裸裸地晾在满法庭的目光底下。嘴唇干裂，他渴望着水。给我力量撑到底，他默然哀告——不是向上帝，而是向他揣在心口的那个形象哀告；他想躲在那形象背后，挡住那些逼视着他的脸。

===Original===

“Where is your father?” Mr. Braddock asked.

“In hell I hope,” Andrews answered, and a burst of laughter from the gallery came like a breath of cool spring wind to a tropic night. No relief of cool winds was allowed in a court of justice. Laughter was suppressed by the usher’s cries.

“Do you mean that he is dead?”

“Yes.”

===Chinese===

“你父亲在哪里？”布拉多克先生问。

“但愿是在地狱里，”安德鲁斯回答，旁听席上爆出的一阵大笑，像一股春天的凉风拂过热带的夜。可是法庭之上不许有凉风解人。笑声叫庭吏的断喝压了下去。

“你是说你父亲已经死了？”

“是的。”

===Original===

“And it was jealousy of a dead man which impelled you to betray comrades of three years’ standing?”

“Yes.”

“Do you expect the jury to understand that?”

“No.” Andrews’s voice drooped wearily. He felt a sudden longing to explain to this red-faced counsel who plagued him so with questions that he had not slept all night. “I don’t expect anyone to understand,” he said. In his heart he added—save Elizabeth—and Carlyon.

===Chinese===

“于是，是对一个死人的妒忌，驱使你出卖了三年的老弟兄？”

“是的。”

“你指望陪审团明白这个？”

“不指望。”安德鲁斯的声音疲惫地低垂下去。他忽然很想对这张用问话折磨他不休的红脸说明白：他一夜没有合眼。“我不指望谁明白，”他说。心里却添了一句——除了伊丽莎白——还有卡莱昂。

===Original===

“Do you expect the jury to believe it?”

“It is true.”

The red face came at him again with the persistence of an insect.

“I suggest to you that your whole story is untrue?”

===Chinese===

“你指望陪审团信这个？”

“这是真话。”

那张红脸又逼上来，纠缠不休，像一只虫子。

“我要向你指出：你这套说法整个儿是假的？”

===Original===

Andrews shook his head, but he could not shake off that voice which came at him again and again and again.

“That you never laid any information?”

“I did.”

“That you are telling this story to save yourself from the dock?”

“No.”

===Chinese===

安德鲁斯摇了摇头，却甩不掉那个声音，它一遍又一遍又一遍地逼上来。

“说你根本没有递过什么密报？”

“我递了。”

“说你编这套故事，是为了叫自己躲开被告席？”

“不是。”

===Original===

“That you never landed with a cargo on the night of February 10?”

“I did, I tell you.”

“That you were with a woman, a notorious woman?”

“No. It’s untrue.”

===Chinese===

“说二月十日夜里你根本没有随货登岸？”

“我登了，我告诉你。”

“说你当时正同一个女人在一起——一个声名狼藉的女人？”

“没有。这是污蔑。”

===Original===

Andrews’s weariness grew on him. He held the sides of the witness box as a support. I could sleep now, he said to himself.

“Will you stand there on your oath and tell the jury that you have not been keeping company with a loose woman?”

“No, I refused,” he said wearily. He could not understand how this red bladder with the bullying voice was so well aware of his movements.

“What do you mean you refused?”

“I was in the Sussex Pad at Shoreham when the girl came up to me. But I wouldn’t have her. Carlyon came in to drink and I was afraid that he’d see me. So I said ‘No.’ I said ‘No. I won’t sleep with you. Not tonight.’ And I slipped out. And I don’t know whether Carlyon saw me or not. I was afraid and I ran for miles, for miles up over the downs.”

“That is no doubt another woman. There’s no need to tell the jury of all the women with whom you have consorted.” Mr. Braddock sniggered and the jury tittered. Sir Edward Parkin allowed himself a faint smile as he watched the young women in the public gallery.

===Chinese===

疲惫一阵阵漫过安德鲁斯。他扶住证人席两侧，权当支撑。这会儿我就能睡着，他对自己说。

“你敢站在那儿、指着你发的誓，告诉陪审团你从来没有同放荡女人厮混吗？”

“没有，我回绝了，”他倦怠地说。他弄不明白，这只声音恫人的红尿泡，怎么对他的行踪知道得一清二楚。

“你说你回绝了，什么意思？”

“我在肖勒姆的「萨塞克斯帕德」（the Sussex Pad）酒馆里，那姑娘凑上前来。可我不要她。卡莱昂进来喝酒，我怕叫他瞧见。所以我说了‘不行’。我说：‘不行。我不跟你睡。今夜不。’我就溜了出去。卡莱昂看见我没有，我不知道。我怕，一口气跑了好几英里，好几英里，翻过了白垩丘。”

“那毫无疑问是另一个女人。用不着向陪审团细数你厮混过的所有女人。”布拉多克先生嗤嗤一笑，陪审团也跟着窃笑。爱德华·帕金爵士望着旁听席上的年轻女子们，也不禁莞尔。

===Original===

The faces in front of Andrews, the solicitors at the table, the usher, the now soundly sleeping Clerk of Arraigns, the bearded prisoners in the dock, the spectators in the gallery, the twelve hostile cow-like jurymen, were becoming rapidly an indistinct blur, one large composite face of many eyes and mouths. Only Mr. Braddock’s face, red and angry, protruded very distinctly out of this mass, as he leant forward to shoot out his questions, which seemed to Andrews absurd and meaningless.

“Do you still persist in saying that you landed with the prisoners on the night of February 10?”

“But it’s true, I tell you.” Andrews clenched his fists and longed to beat back that red aggressive face into the grey mists which surrounded it. Then I could sleep, he thought, and his mind dwelt with longing on the cool white sheets and warm clean blankets which had been wasted on his restless mind and body the night before.

“Carry your mind back two days. Were you not in the company of a notoriously loose woman?”

“No. I don’t understand. I haven’t been with a woman like that for weeks. Can’t you take my answer and have done.” Staring at the face of Mr. Braddock, as it darted back and forth, Andrews was surprised to see it apparently disintegrate under his eyes. It softened and collapsed and reformed itself into a kind of tigerish amiability.

===Chinese===

安德鲁斯眼前那些面孔——律师席上的诉状律师、庭吏、此刻睡得正酣的提审书记官、被告席上蓄须的囚犯、旁听席上的看客、十二张怀有敌意的木然牛脸——正在迅速地模糊成一片，汇成一张由无数眼睛和嘴巴拼成的大脸。只有布拉多克先生那张红彤彤的怒容，从这一大团里极其分明地凸出来；他探着身子发射他的问题，那些问题在安德鲁斯听来荒唐而无意义。

“你还咬定说，二月十日夜里你是同这些被告一道登的岸？”

“可这是真的，我告诉你。”安德鲁斯攥紧拳头，恨不能一拳把那张气势汹汹的红脸打退到包围着它的灰色雾霭里去。那样我就能睡了，他想；他的心思饥渴地流连着前一夜白白糟蹋掉的清凉白床单和干净暖被子——糟蹋在他那具不得安宁的身子和脑袋上。

“把你的心神退回两天之前。你不是正同一个声名狼藉的放荡女人厮混么？”

“没有。我不明白。我都几个星期没沾过那种女人了。你就不能把我的回答收下算数。”安德鲁斯瞪着布拉多克先生那张蹿来蹿去的脸，吃惊地看见它显然在自己眼前瓦解了：软化，坍塌，重新聚拢，变成一种老虎式的和蔼。

===Original===

“I don’t want to tire you. This must be a very trying experience for you.” Mr. Braddock paused, and even in his weariness Andrews smiled, remembering the weaver Bottom—“I can roar you as softly as any sucking dove.”

“I think we are talking at cross-purposes. I am sure that you don’t wish to hinder the course of justice. Only tell the jury where you were staying two nights ago.”

“At a cottage out Hassocks way.”

“Not all by yourself, surely?” The red face creased itself into a sneer, the coarse mouth with two great gravestone teeth sniggered out loud, seeming to give a lead and a cue to laughter from gallery and jury. The usher, grinning himself, called perfunctorily for silence.

===Chinese===

“我不想累着你。这对你准是一段够受的滋味。”布拉多克先生顿了顿，安德鲁斯纵然疲惫，也笑了，想起织工波顿（Bottom）——“我可以吼给你听，温柔得像随便哪一只吃奶的乳鸽。”

“我看咱们是岔在两头、话不投机了。我确信你不愿意阻挠司法的进行。只消告诉陪审团，前天夜里你宿在哪里。”

“在哈索克斯（Hassocks）那头的一座小屋里。”

“总不会是孤零零一个人吧？”那张红脸皱起一道讥诮，那张粗鄙的嘴、那两颗墓碑似的大门牙，嗤地笑出了声，仿佛给旁听席和陪审团的哄笑递了眼色、提了词。庭吏自己也咧着嘴，例行公事地吆喝了一声肃静。

===Original===

“What do you mean?” The laughter confused Andrews. It was like a mist between himself and any clear thought.

“Answer the question,” Mr. Braddock snapped at him. “It was plain enough. Were you alone?”

“No. Why? I was with—”

“With whom?”

He hesitated. He did not know her name, he realised.

===Chinese===

“你这是什么意思？”笑声搅乱了安德鲁斯。它像一片雾，横在他和任何清晰的念头之间。

“回答问题，”布拉多克先生厉声说。“问题够明白的了。你是一个人吗？”

“不是。怎么了？我是同——”

“同谁？”

他迟疑了。他这才发觉，他并不知道她的姓名。

===Original===

“A woman?”

The word woman seemed too general and too coarse a name to describe the banner under which he now fought. A woman? He had known many women, and Elizabeth was not like one of them. She was something more remote and infinitely more desirable.

“No,” he said, and then seeing Mr. Braddock’s great mouth open for another question, he grew dismayed—“at least …” he said and stood confused, hopelessly barren of words.

“Don’t jest with us. It must have been either a woman, a man or a child. Which was it?”

“A woman,” and before he could add some qualifying phrase he was struck by a wave of laughter from every corner of the Court. He came out of it, as though half drowned, red, gasping, blind to everything but the face of his questioner, which was already darting forward for another question.

===Chinese===

“一个女人？”

“女人”这个词，用来称呼他此刻赖以作战的那面旗帜，显得太笼统、太粗鄙了。一个女人？他认识的女人多了，伊丽莎白同她们哪一个都不像。她要更遥远些，也更可渴望到无以复加。

“不是，”他说；随即瞥见布拉多克先生那张大嘴又要张开问下一句，他慌了神——“至少……”他说，站在那里，乱作一团，可怜地想不出一个词。

“不许同法庭耍笑。那必定是个女人、一个男人，或者一个孩子。究竟是哪个？”

“一个女人，”话音未落，他还没来得及添上一点补救的字眼，一阵哄笑就从法庭的每个角落劈头盖脸打来。他从浪里挣出来，像半溺的人，满脸通红，气喘吁吁，除了一张逼问者的脸，什么都看不见了——那张脸已经又蹿上前去，准备问下一句了。

===Original===

“What is her name?”

“Elizabeth,” he murmured indistinctly, but loud enough for Mr. Braddock to hear. He gave it to the Court with the air of a jester. “Elizabeth. And what is the young woman’s surname?”

“I don’t know.”

“What was that the witness said?” Sir Edward Parkin tapped the sheet of paper in front of him with his pen.

“He doesn’t know her surname, my lord,” Mr. Braddock replied with a grin. Sir Edward Parkin smiled, and as though his smile gave an awaited sanction, laughter again swept the Court.

===Chinese===

“她叫什么名字？”

“伊丽莎白，”他含混地咕哝，却响得布拉多克先生听得见。他把这名字报给法庭时，摆出一副小丑的架势。“伊丽莎白。那么这位年轻女子的姓呢？”

“我不知道。”

“证人说什么？”爱德华·帕金爵士用钢笔敲着他面前那页纸。

“他说他不知道她的姓，大人，”布拉多克先生咧嘴答道。爱德华·帕金爵士微微一笑，仿佛这一笑颁下了等候多时的恩准，笑声又扫过整个法庭。

===Original===

“My lord,” Mr. Braddock continued, when silence had been restored, “the witness’s ignorance is not as astounding as it may seem. Opinion on the point differs a great deal among her neighbours.”

Andrews leant forward and banged the edge of the box with his clenched fist. “What are you insinuating?” he said.

“Be quiet,” Sir Edward Parkin turned on him, fingers poised in the act of taking snuff. He turned and smiled ingratiatingly at Mr. Braddock. The case was proving more amusing than he had foreseen.

“Well, my lord, I shall bring a witness to show that the girl is the daughter, probably illegitimate, of a woman called Garnet. The woman is dead and no one knows whether she ever had a husband. They had a lodger staying with them and he took over the farm when the woman died. It is a common idea in the countryside that the girl was not only the daughter of this man, but also his mistress.”

===Chinese===

“大人，”肃静恢复之后，布拉多克先生接着说，“证人的这番无知，其实并不像表面上那样骇人听闻。在这一层上，她街坊四邻的看法就大相径庭。”

安德鲁斯探身向前，攥紧的拳头砸在证人席边沿上。“你影射什么？”他说。

“安静，”爱德华·帕金爵士朝他喝道，手指头还悬在取鼻烟的姿势上。他转过脸，向布拉多克先生讨好地一笑。这案子比他预想的有趣得多。

“是，大人，我将传一名证人，证明那姑娘是一个唤作加尼特（Garnet）的女人的女儿，多半还是私生。那女人已经死了，没人知道她到底有过丈夫没有。她们家里原先住着一位房客，女人死后农场便归了他。乡下人普遍的说法是：那姑娘不光是这男人的女儿，还是他的相好。”

===Original===

“Where is the man?”

“He is dead, my lord.”

“Do you propose to call the girl as a witness?”

“No, my lord, the information has only this moment come into my hands, and in any case the girl would not be a witness in whom the jury could place any credence. The whole story is a very sordid one.”

===Chinese===

“那男人如今何在？”

“他也死了，大人。”

“你打算传那姑娘出庭作证吗？”

“不，大人，这消息方才才到我手里；再说，那姑娘也绝不是陪审团肯于置信的证人。这段故事整个儿是桩很不堪的丑事。”

===Original===

“My God, do you know what’s beautiful?” Andrews cried.

“If you cannot keep silent,” Sir Edward Parkin said, “I shall commit you for contempt of court.”

“My lord,” Andrews appealed, and hesitated, trying to shake off the mist of weariness that clung round his brain and clogged his words.

“Is there something you want to say?”

===Chinese===

“我的上帝，你可知道什么叫美？”安德鲁斯喊道。

“你若不能保持安静，”爱德华·帕金爵士说，“我就以藐视法庭罪收押你。”

“大人，”安德鲁斯哀告道，随即迟疑了，竭力想抖落缠在脑际的那层疲惫之雾——是它堵住了他的话。

“你有什么话要说吗？”

===Original===

Andrews lifted a hand to his forehead. He must find words in the mist which shrouded him, words to express the gold which suffused it from the light of candles lit in a far place behind the brain.

“Say what you want to say or be silent.”

“My lord, it’s not sordid,” he muttered very low. It seemed hopeless to find words until he had slept.

“Mr. Braddock, the witness says that it is not sordid.” The laughter beat upon Andrews’s head, till it felt physically bruised as though by hail.

Mr. Braddock felt himself riding to victory upon a gale of laughter.

===Chinese===

安德鲁斯抬手按住额头。他必须在裹住他的那片雾里找到字眼，找到能说出那片金的字眼——那金光，是从脑子后方远处点亮的烛焰透进雾里来的。

“有话就说，不然就安静。”

“大人，那不是丑事，”他低低地咕哝。不先睡一觉，要找到字眼似乎全无指望。

“布拉多克先生，证人说那不是丑事。”笑声劈头砸在安德鲁斯的脑袋上，砸得他真真切切地生疼，像遭了一阵冰雹。

布拉多克先生只觉得自己是乘着一阵笑声的狂风，一路凯旋。

===Original===

“Take your mind back to two mornings ago. We will leave out the night,” he added with a snigger. “Do you remember a woman coming to the cottage?”

“Yes.”

“Is it true that your friend without a surname, Elizabeth, told the woman that you were her brother?”

“Yes.”

“Why?”

“I can’t remember.”

===Chinese===

“把你的心神退回前天早晨。夜里那段咱们略过不提，”他加了一句，嗤嗤地笑。“你可记得有个女人来过那小屋？”

“记得。”

“你那位没有姓的朋友伊丽莎白，对那女人说你是她哥哥——可有此事？”

“有。”

“为什么？”

“我记不得了。”

===Original===

“Did she say that you’d been staying with her for a week?”

“I think so. I can’t remember anything. I’m tired.”

“That is all I want to ask you.”

Can I at last sit down and sleep? Andrews wondered incredulously. His doubt was justified. Sir Henry Merriman rose.

“Did you stay at the cottage for a week?”

“No. Two nights. That was all.”

===Chinese===

“她可曾说你已经在她那里住了一个星期？”

“好像是。我什么都记不得。我累。”

“我要问的就是这些。”

这下我总算可以坐下睡觉了么？安德鲁斯不敢相信地想。他的怀疑是有道理的。亨利·梅里曼爵士起立。

“你在那小屋里住了一个星期吗？”

“没有。两个晚上，仅此而已。”

===Original===

“Think hard. Can’t you remember why she told those lies. They were to help you?”

“Of course. She’d never lie for herself. It was because I was afraid that the woman would talk in the town. I was afraid of Carlyon.”

“Why were you afraid?”

“He knew that I’d betrayed him. He was after me. He came to the cottage while I was there. But she hid me. She fooled him. She was brave like a saint. She drank out of my cup. How can he say there was anything sordid? It’s all lies they tell about her. If I wasn’t so tired I could tell you all.”

===Chinese===

“使劲想想。你想不起她为什么要说那些谎？那是为了帮你？”

“当然是为帮我。她自己是从不撒谎的。是因为我怕那女人到镇上去嚼舌头。我怕卡莱昂。”

“你为什么怕他？”

“他知道我出卖了他。他在追我。我待在小屋里的时候，他来过。可她把我藏了起来。她把他哄过去了。她勇敢得像一位圣徒。她用我的杯子喝过水。他怎么说得出有什么不堪？他们讲她的全是谎话。我要不是累成这样，满可以一样一样都讲给你们听。”

===Original===

“Why did she do all this for you? Were you her lover?”

“No. It was just charity. I’ve never touched her, I swear it.”

“Thank you. That is all.” Andrews stood where he was, unbelieving that the end had at last come, that he had done what Elizabeth had urged him to do, that all was over now and he could sleep. He felt a hand pull at his sleeve. He stumbled down the steps to the floor of the court, still under the influence of the guiding hand, which now pulled him gently and insistently towards the door.

===Chinese===

“她为你做这一切，图什么？你是她的情人吗？”

“不是。那不过是慈悲。我从没碰过她一个指头，我发誓。”

“多谢。问完了。”安德鲁斯站在原地，不敢相信终局终于来了，不敢相信他已做成了伊丽莎白催他做的事，一切都完了，他可以睡了。他觉出一只手扯他的衣袖。他跌跌撞撞走下台阶，来到法庭的地面，那只引路的手始终没有放开，此刻正温和而执拗地把他往门口拉。

===Original===

As he passed the dock a voice called to him, “Andrews.” He stopped and looked up. It took him a moment to focus his eyes. Then he saw that it was Tims. “Let me out, Andrews,” he implored.

There was a hostile murmur from the gallery and Andrews flushed. Anger, unreasoning and undirected, against himself, against his father, against this boy who held him for one moment from his sleep, tossed back an answer. “You fool, I’ve put you there.” Then he was outside the Court.

“I want to sleep,” he said. “Can I go?”

He found that he was speaking to an officer. “Not outside I shouldn’t,” the man said. “There’s a crowd there. You ain’t too popular. Better wait till the case is over. They’ll look after you then.”

===Chinese===

经过被告席时，有个声音唤他：“安德鲁斯。”他停步抬头。眼睛过了一会儿才聚上焦。他看清了，那是蒂姆斯。“放我出去，安德鲁斯，”他哀求道。

旁听席上一片敌意的嘘声，安德鲁斯涨红了脸。一股怒气——没来由、没目标的怒气，冲着自己，冲着父亲，冲着这个把他从睡眠边上拽住片刻的少年——把一句回答掷了回去。“你这傻瓜，是我把你弄进去的。”随即他已到了法庭之外。

“我要睡觉，”他说。“我能走了吗？”

他发觉自己是在同一名警官说话。“出去么，我看先别，”那人说。“外头人多着呢。你的人缘可不怎么样。顶好等案子审完。到那时自会有人照应你。”

===Original===

“Anywhere—a chair.” He put his hand against the wall to support himself.

“There’s the witness’s room.”

“I can’t go back there. They won’t give me any peace. Isn’t there anywhere?”

The officer softened a little. “Here,” he said, “you’d better sit here.” He pointed at a bench against the wall. “It’s against orders,” he added grudgingly, but already Andrews had sank down on it and had let sleep come, instantaneous, dreamless sleep, that carried for one instant only a confusion of faces, bearded angry faces, sniggering red faces, one pale face, a gold mist and then nothing at all.

===Chinese===

“随便哪儿——一把椅子就行。”他用手撑住墙，支住自己。

“有证人室。”

“那屋里我回不去。他们不会给我安宁的。就再没有别处了？”

那警官心软了些。“这儿，”他说，“你顶好就坐这儿。”他指着靠墙的一条长凳。“这是违了规矩的，”他又勉强添了一句；可是安德鲁斯已经瘫坐下去，听凭睡眠降临——瞬息之间、无梦的睡眠，那里面只装了一瞬间的纷乱面孔：一张张蓄着怒气的大胡子脸，一张张嗤笑的红脸，一张苍白的脸，一片金色的雾，随后便什么都没有了。

===Original===

“That is the case for the Crown.” Sir Henry Merriman’s voice, filtering through the big double doors of the court, came too softly to disturb Andrews, where he slept. To him in a state of content, of unknowing, without dreams, weeks might have passed and not hours. The voice was a clear whisper. That was all. And he had not wakened, when, a long time previously, the Court had risen for luncheon. The whispers of the witnesses had then ceased to sound in the corridor. There had been silence, a shuffle of persons rising to their feet and then, as the doors of the Court swung open, loud voices and a roar of conversation which burst like a bomb. Andrews slept on, slept on through the heavy reluctant return of feet, weighed down by a good meal eaten, slept on as the doors closed and the whispers of the witnesses began again.

The officer in the corridor leant his ear against the door and listened, avid for any excitement to conquer boredom. He cast an eye towards Andrews in the hope of conversation, but Andrews slept. The prisoners inside were making their defence; so much the officer could gather from the broken sentences that reached him. Each man’s defence had been written out for him by his solicitor, and it was read in a toneless stumbling voice. Through the glass front of the door the officer could see the prisoners. The trial was reaching its final stages and so was the light. The Court was veiled depressingly in grey, not yet sufficiently dark to justify the lighting of the candles. The prisoners, in spite of their confidence in the jury, felt the gloom and were a little touched by fear. Each as he read from the sheet of paper in front of him felt the constraining presence of a dead man rise to refute his arguments. A man had been killed. A hundred alibis could not turn that fact into a falsehood. As though by mutual consent, bent on the sacrifice of an unwanted Jonah, they edged a little away from the half-witted youth, until he sat in a small cleared space, which in that crowded Court took on the dimensions of a desert.

Each man’s defence was a little subtly changed. This man at the supposed time of the affray had been drinking with a friend, this man had been in bed with his wife. All would bring witnesses to prove their stories and only the perorations were similar, “So help me God I am innocent.”

===Chinese===

“控方的案件至此审毕。”亨利·梅里曼爵士的声音滤过法庭高大的双扇门，落到安德鲁斯睡的地方，轻得惊不动他。在他那种餍足的、无所知的、无梦的状态里，过去的是几个星期，而不仅仅是几个钟头，也未可知。那声音只是一声清晰的耳语，如此而已。而更久以前，法庭退庭用午餐的时候，他也没有醒。证人交头接耳的语声，那时曾在走廊里消停下去：一片寂静，一阵纷纷起立的窸窣，随后，法庭的两扇门一荡开，高声笑语便像炸弹一样炸裂开来。安德鲁斯照睡不误，睡过了那顿饱饭压沉了的、脚步迟迟的归来，睡过了两扇门的重新合拢和证人们低语的再度响起。

走廊里的警官把耳朵贴在门上，贪婪地捕捉任何一点可以驱赶无聊的热闹。他朝安德鲁斯瞟了一眼，指望攀谈几句，可是安德鲁斯睡着。里面囚犯们正在各自答辩；这一点，警官从断续飘到他耳边的句子上听得出来。每个人的答辩词都是诉状律师预先替他写好的，用一种平板而磕绊的声音宣读。隔着门上的玻璃，警官看得见那些囚犯。审讯进到了末段，天光也是。法庭郁郁地罩在灰色里，可天还没有黑到可以点烛的程度。囚犯们尽管对陪审团满怀信心，也感到了这股阴沉，被恐惧轻轻碰了一下。每个人对着面前的纸页宣读时，都觉出一个死人的身影逼上来，要驳倒他的论点。人是被杀死了。一百个不在场证明也不能把这个事实变成假话。仿佛出于默契，存心要牺牲一头碍事的约拿，他们一个个从那痴傻的少年身边往外挪了挪，直到他独自坐在一小块清空的场地里——在那拥挤的法庭上，那一小块地方竟有了沙漠的尺度。

每个人的答辩词都做了些微妙的不同。这一个，出事的时辰正同朋友喝酒；那一个，正同老婆在床上。个个都要传证人来证明自己的说法，只有结尾千篇一律：“上帝助我，我是清白的。”

===Original===

Four times the stumbling, mechanical stories were repeated to set the officer yawning, and then there was a change. It was the turn of Hake, the large black-bearded man who had threatened Andrews from the dock. When he rose the candles were being lighted in Court and his shadow swung across the ceiling in the manner of a gigantic bird. His voice boomed into the corridor like struck metal deeply toned.

“My lord, the gentlemen of the jury have a responsibility on them today the like of which will never come their way again. Whose word are they going to take? Those gaugers, afraid of losing their jobs the whole lot of them, ours—men they’ve drunk with—that sneak’s, that Andrews with his loose woman, or ours? If they hang us and the truth comes out who’ll speak for their souls in the day of Judgment? Who’ll defend their bodies here?”

“Prisoner,” a high petulant voice, “are you threatening the jury? The jury have nothing to do with the punishment. They have only to decide whether you are innocent or guilty.”

“I only warn them …”

===Chinese===

那磕磕绊绊、机械刻板的调子重复了四遍，听得警官直打哈欠，随后起了变化。轮到黑克了——就是从被告席上吓唬过安德鲁斯的大黑胡子。他起立时，法庭里正点起蜡烛，他的影子荡过天花板，像一只巨鸟。他的嗓音轰进走廊，像敲响的金属，声音沉宏。

“大人，陪审团的先生们今天肩上担的干系，往后一辈子再不会有第二回。他们要信谁的话？信那些税吏的？怕丢饭碗，一个个全怕——信俺们的？他们可是一块儿喝过酒的人——信那个密探的？那个安德鲁斯，带着他那个浪女人的？还是信俺们的？他们若绞了俺们，真相大白的那天，末日审判的日子，谁替他们的灵魂说话？在这里，谁来护他们的皮肉？”

“囚犯，”一个尖利的、乖戾的声音说，“你这是在威胁陪审团吗？惩处的事与陪审团无干。他们只须裁断你有罪还是无辜。”

“俺不过是警告他们……”

===Original===

“The jury will be protected in the performance of their duty. Threats do not strengthen your case.”

“Are you going to hang us?”

“I am anxious to be fair, but unless you proceed with your defence, you must sit down.”

“My defence is the same as these others. I wasn’t there. I’ll prove it with witnesses as these will. But a man’s been killed, you’ll say, you can’t get over that. Well, I’ll tell you who killed him. He did,” and his finger pierced across and emphasised the desert which surrounded Tims. Tims leapt to his feet. “You don’t mean it,” he said, “you are lying. Tell them you are lying.” He sank down again on his chair and covering his face with his hands began to cry with a peculiar moaning sound like a sick animal’s. Mingled with the booming voice it made a peculiar orchestral effect in the corridor.

===Chinese===

“陪审团履职，自会受保护。威胁替你的案子帮不了忙。”

“你们要绞死俺们？”

“我一心想做到公正，可你若不继续答辩，就必须坐下。”

“俺的答辩跟他们几个一样。俺没在场。俺也要传证人来证明，跟他们一样。可你们会说：人死了，这一条抹不掉。好，俺告诉你们是谁杀的。是他干的，”他的手指横里一戳，点着包围蒂姆斯的那片沙漠。蒂姆斯一蹦而起。“你不是当真的，”他说，“你撒谎。跟他们说你是撒谎。”他重新瘫坐进椅子，双手捂住脸，哭起来，带着一种特别的呻吟，像病兽的呜咽。那呻吟混进轰鸣的嗓音里，在走廊里造成一种古怪的交响效果。

===Original===

“I’ve heard him, I tell you, talking about it. He’s a half-witted loon, you can see that for yourself, more fit for the asylum than for the gallows. He used to tell me many a time what he intended to do to Rexall. Rexall used to tease him in the street. You’ve heard a gauger say so himself, but there’s more evidence than that to it. I wouldn’t expect you to take a gauger’s word. But listen here—you are honest men and will bring us in innocent.”

“You are not addressing the jury, you are addressing the Court.”

“I’m sorry, my lord, what I mean to say,” he leant forward over the edge of the dock towards the jury, “the jury will want to know what’s to happen to that Judas and his woman. Let them leave it to us, I say, let them leave it to us.”

Before Sir Edward Parkin could speak he sat down. The officer stole a glance at Andrews. He slept on.

===Chinese===

“俺听见过他，俺告诉你们，亲口念叨这事。他是个痴傻的疯子，你们自己看得出，进疯人院比上绞架更合适。他从前多少回对俺说，他要拿雷克索尔怎样怎样。雷克索尔常在街上逗弄他。税吏里头有人自己都这么说过，可证据还不止这一条。俺也不指望你们单信一个税吏的话。可你们听着——你们是诚实人，会把俺们断成无罪的。”

“你不是在对陪审团说话，你是在对法庭说话。”

“对不住，大人，俺的意思是，”他从被告席边沿探出身子，朝陪审团那边凑，“陪审团会想知道，那个犹大和他的女人往后怎么发落。叫他们把这交给俺们办，俺说，交给俺们办。”

没等爱德华·帕金爵士开口，他坐下了。警官偷偷瞟了安德鲁斯一眼。他照睡不误。

===Original===

The Court seemed peculiarly silent when that booming voice was still. They were waiting for the last prisoner to make his defence, but he remained seated, his face covered by his hands which shook spasmodically in time with his moans.

“Richard Tims, this is the time that it becomes your duty to make your defence.”

He made no reply, no sign even that he had heard the judge’s voice.

“Mr. Braddock, you represent the prisoner, do you not?”

“I, my lord?” Mr. Braddock rose, sweeping his gown round him, as though to escape pollution. “This prisoner? No, my lord. I represent the other prisoners.”

“No one ever seems capable of making out the lists correctly. You are put down for all the prisoners, Mr. Braddock.”

===Chinese===

那轰鸣的嗓音一停，法庭显得出奇地静。大家在等最后一名囚犯答辩，他却坐着不动，脸埋在手里，两只手一阵阵痉挛地抖，应着他呻吟的节拍。

“理查德·蒂姆斯（Richard Tims），此刻轮到你履行答辩之责了。”

他不作回答，连一丝听见了法官声音的表示也没有。

“布拉多克先生，这名囚犯由你代理，是不是？”

“我吗，大人？”布拉多克先生起立，把法袍朝身上一拢，仿佛要躲开什么脏东西。“这名囚犯？不，大人。我代理的是其他被告。”

“看来这名单从来没人能造对。所有被告名下写的都是你，布拉多克先生。”

===Original===

“I was never so instructed, my lord.”

“Which of you represents this prisoner?”

There was no reply.

“Has this prisoner had no legal advice?” Sir Edward Parkin protested with a faint note of annoyance.

“If he had wished, my lord, he could have had counsel.”

“This is very trying. The case has gone on long enough as it is. I don’t want any delay. The Assizes is a very full one.”

===Chinese===

“从没有人这样嘱咐过我，大人。”

“你们哪一位代理这名囚犯？”

没有人应声。

“这囚犯竟没有人给他出过法律主意？”爱德华·帕金爵士带着一丝恼意诘问道。

“他若有意，大人，本可以延请律师。”

“这实在叫人为难。案子已经拖得够久了。我不容再耽搁。这一季巡回审的案子本来就多。”

===Original===

“My lord,” an elderly little man with blinking eyes rose to his feet, “I will represent the prisoner if you so wish it.”

“Thank you, Mr. Petty. Will you explain to the prisoner that he must make his defence?”

Mr. Petty stepped delicately to the edge of the Court and holding a handkerchief to his nose spoke to the boy.

“It’s no use, my lord, the prisoner is not in a fit state to make his defence.”

“The jury will take it that he merely asserts his innocence. Mr. Braddock will you call your witnesses?” Sir Edward Parkin leant back and dabbed his fingers furiously in his snuffbox. He was annoyed. The case had been held up for at least two minutes. His breakfast had been a bad one, his luncheon worse, and he was hungry. The trial showed no sign of reaching an end, but his hunger, far from leading to an adjournment, only confirmed his obstinacy. He would sit till midnight if necessary, but he would finish the trial.

One after another men, women and children filed into the witness box and committed mechanical perjury. This woman was in bed with that man at the time of the murder, this man was toasting another in whisky, a child had heard its father undressing upstairs. Sir Henry Merriman shrugged his shoulders at Mr. Farne. “They have us,” he seemed to say. “That man Andrews,” Mr. Farne whispered, “was worse than useless.” Only occasionally did they trouble to cross-examine. The witnesses had been too well primed in their stories. Mr. Petty, having magnanimously undertaken the task of representing the half-wit, closed his eyes and went to sleep.

===Chinese===

“大人，”一个上了年纪的瘦小男人眨巴着眼睛站起身，“大人若恩准，卑职愿代这囚犯辩护。”

“多谢，佩蒂先生（Mr. Petty）。请你向这囚犯说明，他必须答辩。”

佩蒂先生斯斯文文踱到法庭边沿，用手帕掩着鼻子，同那少年说话。

“没有用，大人，囚犯这情形没法答辩。”

“陪审团将认定他仅是申明自己无辜。布拉多克先生，请传你的证人。”爱德华·帕金爵士朝椅背一靠，手指头在鼻烟盒里狠狠地捻。他恼了。案子至少被耽搁了两分钟。他的早餐本就不佳，午餐更糟，他饿着。审讯毫无收尾的迹象，可他的饥饿非但没有引他考虑休庭，反倒坐实了他的固执。必要的话，坐到半夜他也在所不惜，但他要把这案子审完。

男男女女，还有孩子，一个接一个鱼贯走进证人席，机械地作着伪证。谋杀发生的时辰，这个女人正同那个男人在床上；这个人正用威士忌敬另一个人；一个孩子听见父亲在楼上脱衣裳。亨利·梅里曼爵士朝法恩先生耸了耸肩。“咱们输给他们了，”那神气仿佛说。“那个安德鲁斯，”法恩先生耳语道，“比没用还糟。”他们只偶尔费神盘问两句。证人早把词儿背得太熟了。佩蒂先生宽宏大量地揽下了替痴儿辩护的差事，这时闭上眼睛睡着了。

===Original===

Mrs. Butler scrambled up the steps of the witness box and allowed her ample breasts to flow over the edge. Yes, she had seen Andrews at a certain woman’s cottage two days previously. Yes, there had been every indication that he had slept in the place. The woman had told her that Andrews had been there for a week. Yes, the woman was a notoriously loose liver. All the neighbourhood knew it.

“What the neighbourhood says is not evidence.”

“No, my lord, but what my eyes have seen is evidence.”

Sir Henry Merriman’s voice stabbed itself into the corridor, sharp and clear as an icicle. “Did you hear this woman call the man Andrews her brother?”

“Yes.”

===Chinese===

巴特勒夫人（Mrs. Butler）手脚并用爬上证人席的台阶，任她那对丰硕的乳房从席沿漫出来。是的，两天前她曾在某个女人的小屋见过安德鲁斯。是的，种种迹象表明他睡在那屋里。那女人告诉过她，安德鲁斯在那里住了一个星期。是的，那女人出了名的浪。左近一带谁不知道。

“街坊怎么说，不算是证据。”

“是不算，大人，可俺这双眼亲眼见的，就算是证据了。”

亨利·梅里曼爵士的声音刺进走廊来，又尖又亮，像一柄冰锥。“你可听见这女人管安德鲁斯叫她哥哥？”

“听见了。”

===Original===

“Was that true?”

“No, of course it weren’t true. They didn’t take me in, I can tell you.” Her hand unerringly sought the thin strands of gold in her hair and she stroked them lovingly. “I know what it is to love,” she said in her sweet, damp voice. “I could tell the love light in ’is eyes.”

“What does the woman mean?”

“She means, my lord,” Mr. Braddock explained with unction, “that the man Andrews appeared to be in love with the woman.”

“How on earth could she tell that?”

===Chinese===

“可是真话？”

“不是，自然不是真话。他们可哄不过俺，俺告诉你。”她的手不差分毫地摸到头发里那几缕金黄，爱抚地捻着。“俺知道恋爱是什么滋味，”她用甜腻而潮润的嗓音说。“他眼睛里那点恋爱的光，俺一瞧就看出来哩。”

“这女人什么意思？”

“她的意思是，大人，”布拉多克先生油滑地解释，“安德鲁斯这个人，看上去同那女人坠了情网。”

“这她到底是怎么看出来的？”

===Original===

“A woman’s intuition, m’ lord.” Mrs. Butler’s hand stroked one capacious breast. “And I can tell you something else, m’ lord. Only one bed had been slept in.”

“If the woman lied with regard to her relationship with Andrews, have you any reason for believing her other statement that he had been with her for a week? I suggest that he had arrived only the night before.”

“Well, I don’t know anything, sir. But ’e must ’ave made quick time with ’er, mustn’t ’e?” Mrs. Butler leered ingratiatingly at Sir Edward Parkin. “Men are very shy, my lord. I’ve known many in my time, my lord, and I speak with conviction.”

Sir Edward Parkin turned away his face, screwed up a little as though he suffered from nausea. “Have you finished with this good woman, Sir Henry?”

“Yes, my lord.”

===Chinese===

“女人的直觉呀，大人。”巴特勒夫人的手抚着一只宽宽的胸脯。“俺还能告诉大人一桩事哩，大人。床上睡过人的，只有一张。”

“这女人既然在同安德鲁斯的关系上撒了谎，你说他同她住了一个星期，这另一句话你凭什么信？我倒觉得他不过前一夜才到。”

“唉，俺啥也不知道，先生。可他同她准是进展得飞快，是不是？”巴特勒夫人朝爱德华·帕金爵士谄媚地飞了个眼儿。“男人家脸皮薄着哩，大人。俺这辈子见过的男人多了，大人，俺这话是有根有据的。”

爱德华·帕金爵士把脸扭开，微微皱起，仿佛有些恶心。“这位好女人问完了没有，亨利爵士？”

“问完了，大人。”

===Original===

Mr. Braddock rose. “That, my lord, is the case for the defence.”

“Have you any witnesses to call, Mr. Petty?”

“No, my lord.”

“Gentlemen of the jury, it is growing late, but by the law of England I am not allowed to discharge you until the case is finished. I am obliged to keep you together, though, no doubt, proper accommodation will be afforded you. But I am for myself perfectly willing to go on to finish the case before we separate. I have been accustomed to bear fatigue of this kind and am willing to bear it. The foreman will consult with his brethren and collect their wishes.”

There was a brief nodding of heads and the foreman intimated that they wished to finish the case. Sir Edward Parkin leant back in his seat, took a liberal helping of snuff, smoothed his white hands with some complacency and began his summing up. The officer with an impatient sigh removed his ear from the door. He had in past assizes experienced the bitter boredom of Mr. Justice Parkin’s meticulous care and accuracy. Only occasionally did he put his ear to the door to gain some indication of the progress of the judge’s charge.

===Chinese===

布拉多克先生起立。“大人，辩方的案子到此审毕。”

“你还有证人要传吗，佩蒂先生？”

“没有了，大人。”

“陪审团的先生们，时候不早了，但按英格兰的法律，案子不审结，我不能放你们回去。我不得不把你们留在一处，当然，会为你们安排妥当的住处。不过就我个人而言，我很愿意在散伙之前把案子审完。这般辛苦我惯于承受，也乐于承受。请陪审长（foreman）同众位弟兄商议，把大家的意愿收拢来。”

几颗脑袋匆匆点了几点，陪审长表示他们愿意把案子审完。爱德华·帕金爵士朝椅背一靠，满满地吸了一大撮鼻烟，颇自得地把白手抚了抚，开始他的总结陈词。警官不耐烦地叹了口气，把耳朵从门上挪开。往年的巡回审上，帕金法官那份一丝不苟、锱铢必较所带来的酷烈无聊，他是领教过的。他只是偶尔把耳朵贴一贴门，探一探法官训示的进度。

===Original===

“If you accept the evidence of the revenue officers that these men landed with a cargo on the night of February 10, and that in a fight which ensued Rexall was killed, it is unnecessary to fix the guilt of firing the shot on any one man. By the law of England they are all equally guilty of murder. The prisoners, in answer to the charge, have returned a complete denial and five of the prisoners have brought evidence to show that they were in a different place when the fight, described by the officers of the Crown, took place. Gentlemen, with regard to the credibility of the prisoners’ witnesses I would have you bear in mind …

“The evidence for the prosecution rests not on the bare word of the officers alone. One of the prisoners’ companions, on whose information the officers are said to have acted, appeared in the witness box. You must decide for yourselves upon his credibility, but I would point out that his story is similar in every point to that given by the officers …

“There remains, gentlemen, the body, and here an unexpected line has been taken by five of the prisoners. They have accused one of their number of having committed the murder as a climax to a series of quarrels with the officer Rexall. They have adopted part of the evidence of the prosecution in their own defence. Medical evidence leaves no doubt of the cause of Rexall’s death, and the bullet found in his body is similar to those in the possession of these men. No evidence has been brought by this prisoner in his defence, but until a late stage of the trial he was unrepresented by counsel, and you can judge for yourself of his mental state. I would point out to you that it is for the prosecution to prove a case of guilt. The prisoners’ statements are not evidence, and the prosecution have not attempted to prove the man Tims guilty alone. He and his companions in this respect must be judged together …

“You are not concerned with the past, and the evidence of the witness Andrews dealing with the life of crime lived on the ship *Good Chance* must not be taken into consideration. You are not to try the prisoners on their bad characters, nor are you to try them on the good characters which have been given to them by certain witnesses for the defence—you are to try whether they be guilty of the crime with which they are charged. It has been stated that they are good fathers, good husbands, good sons, but if they were angels and if the evidence as to the crime were clear and satisfactory, it would be your duty to return a verdict accordingly …

“An ill-advised attempt has been made by one of the prisoners to influence your verdict by threats. I can promise you, gentlemen, that whatever your verdict you will have the full protection of the law …”

The officer drooped like an undignified and top heavy flower. The candles in the Court were burning low in their sockets, but Mr. Justice Parkin, with the stage all his own, talked on …

===Chinese===

“倘使你们采信缉私官员们的证据，认定这些人在二月十日夜里随一批私货登岸，又在随后一场搏斗中打死了雷克索尔，那么就无须把开枪之责指实到任何单独一人身上。按英格兰的法律，他们个个同等犯了谋杀罪。被告们对控罪一概全盘否认，其中五人还提出了证据，表明控方官员所述那场搏斗发生之时，他们在另一处地方。先生们，关于被告证人的可信程度，我要请你们记住一点……”

“控方的证据，并非仅凭官员们的空口一言。被告们的一个同伙——据说官员们正是依他的密报行事——已出庭作证。他的话可信与否，须由你们自行裁断；但我要指出，他的说法与官员们的证词，在每一点上都如出一辙……”

“还剩下尸首一项，先生们；在这一项上，五名被告采取了一条出人意料的路线。他们指控同伙中有一人犯了这桩凶杀，作为同雷克索尔官员一连串口角的顶点。他们把控方证据的一部分拿来作了自家辩护之用。医学证据对雷克索尔的死因不容置疑，从他体内找到的子弹，同这些人持有的子弹同类。这名被告没有提出任何证据为自己辩护，但直到审判的后期，他都没有律师代理，他的神智状态如何，你们自可裁断。我要向你们指出：证明有罪，是控方的责任。被告的陈述不算证据，而控方也从未试图单独证明蒂姆斯一人有罪。就这一点而论，他同他的同伙必须一体裁断……”

“过去的事与你们无干，证人安德鲁斯关于 *好机遇号* 船上犯罪生涯的证词，不得纳入考虑。你们不可就被告们的恶行劣迹审断他们，也不可就辩方若干证人所称道的善行美德审断他们——你们要审断的只是：他们是否犯了被控之罪。有人陈述说，他们是慈父，是好丈夫，是好儿子；但纵然他们是天使，只要关于罪行的证据清楚而可信，你们依职责也必须相应作出裁决……”

“曾有一名被告试图以威胁左右你们的裁决，此计甚为失当。我可以向你们保证，先生们：无论你们作出何种裁决，你们都将得到法律全力的保护……”

警官像一朵不成体统、头重脚轻的花，蔫了下去。法庭里的蜡烛已在烛座里燃得低低的，帕金法官占着满台的戏，兀自讲个不停……

===Original===

Through Andrews’s sleep came first a hum of talk, then a distant burst of cheering. He opened his eyes. Through a window he could see that it was dark. Groups of talking people passed him and paid him no attention. The door of the Court stood open. He sat up and cleared sleep from his eyes with the back of a hand. Sir Henry Merriman and Mr. Farne came from the Court. Mr. Farne was talking with gentle insistence, his hand on the older man’s arm. “We shall never put down smuggling in the Courts,” Mr. Farne said. “There is only one way—to remove the duty from spirits.”

Sir Henry Merriman stared at the ground. “No,” he said, “I am growing old. I must retire and give room for younger men. You, Farne.”

“That is nonsense,” Mr. Farne said. “No man could have made that jury convict.”

===Chinese===

一片低低的谈话声先透进安德鲁斯的睡眠，随后是一阵遥远的欢呼。他睁开眼睛。隔着窗，他看出天已经黑了。三三两两谈着话的人从他面前走过，没人理会他。法庭的门敞开着。他坐起身，用手背抹去眼里的睡意。亨利·梅里曼爵士和法恩先生从法庭里出来。法恩先生温言细语却又执意地谈着，一只手搭在长者的臂上。“在法庭上，咱们永远剿灭不了走私，”法恩先生说。“只有一条路——把烈酒的税撤掉。”

亨利·梅里曼爵士怔怔地望着地面。“不，”他说，“我老了。我该退了，给年轻些的人让路。比如说，你，法恩。”

“这是胡说，”法恩先生说。“换了谁也没法叫那个陪审团定罪。”

===Original===

Andrews slowly rose to his feet. “Do you mean to say,” he said, “that those men are acquitted?”

Mr. Farne turned. “Yes,” he said shortly. “Listen. The whole town’s cheering them.”

“Don’t go,” Andrews implored. “Tell me what am I to do. Have they been released?” Mr. Farne nodded.

“You’ve cheated me,” Andrews cried. “You got me to give evidence and now—don’t you understand that you’ve let them loose on me?”

===Chinese===

安德鲁斯慢慢站起来。“你是说，”他说，“那些人开释了？”

法恩先生转过身。“是的，”他干巴巴地说。“听。全城都在为他们欢呼。”

“别走，”安德鲁斯哀求。“告诉我我该怎么办。他们放出来了吗？”法恩先生点了点头。

“你们骗了我，”安德鲁斯喊道。“你们叫我去作证，如今——你们不明白吗，你们是把他们放出来咬我了？”

===Original===

Sir Henry raised eyes that seemed blurred with weariness. “I have already promised you,” he said, “that you shall be protected as long as you stay in this town. I should advise you to leave for London, however, as soon as you can. I admit that certain threats were made against you. Give Sussex a wide berth and you will be safe.”

“How can I get to London? I have no money.”

“Come to me tomorrow,” Sir Henry said. “You shall be given money.” He turned his back on Andrews. “Farne,” he said, “I am tired. I shall go to bed now. Listen. Isn’t it rather bitter, that cheering? If we had won there would have been less enthusiasm. You remember the Duke of Northumberland, who declared for Jane Grey—‘the people press to see us, but not one saith God speed’?”

“I won’t let you go like this,” Andrews cried. “That cheering only means defeat to you. It will be death to me if I’m seen. How can I get away from here?”

===Chinese===

亨利爵士抬起眼睛，眼里像蒙着一层疲惫。“我已经答应过你，”他说，“只要你留在这镇上，就会有人保护你。不过我劝你尽快动身去伦敦。我承认，有人对你放过狠话。离萨塞克斯（Sussex）远远的，你就安全了。”

“我怎么去得了伦敦？我没有钱。”

“明天来找我，”亨利爵士说。“会给你钱的。”他转过身去背对安德鲁斯。“法恩，”他说，“我乏了。这就去睡。你听。那欢呼声，是不是有点苦涩？假使我们赢了，反倒不会这样热烈。你还记得诺森伯兰公爵（the Duke of Northumberland）吗？他拥立了简·格雷（Jane Grey）——‘百姓争相挤着来看我们，却没有一个人说一句愿上帝保佑’？”

“我不能就这样放你走，”安德鲁斯喊道。“那欢呼对你们不过是一场败仗，对我，一旦被看见，就是死。我怎么才能离开这里？”

===Original===

“I have given orders to the runners,” Sir Henry said. “They will see you back to the hotel. Two men will be stationed there to accompany you at any time through the town. If I were you I should catch the first coach to London in the morning.” Mr. Farne pushed Andrews on one side and the two men moved away.

Andrews turned to the officer. “You see,” he said, “that’s their gratitude. I did my best for them, didn’t I, and I’ve risked my life, but what do they care?”

“And why should they care for an informer like you? I’m sure I don’t,” he beamed at Andrews genially. “I’d let your friends get you, but orders is orders. Come this way.”

Escorted by way of a back door and a succession of dirty lanes Andrews reached the White Hart through the stables.

===Chinese===

“我已吩咐了捕快（runners），”亨利爵士说。“他们会送你回旅馆。另有两人驻在那里，随时陪你穿行全镇。我要是你，明早就搭头一班马车去伦敦。”法恩先生把安德鲁斯拨到一旁，两人走远了。

安德鲁斯转向那警官。“你瞧，”他说，“这就是他们的良心。我为他们尽了力，不是吗？我把命都押上了，可他们管吗？”

“他们凭什么要管你这么一个告密的？反正俺可不管，”他朝安德鲁斯和和气气地一咧嘴。“换了我，就放你那些朋友来收拾你。不过命令是命令。这边走吧。”

走一道后门，穿一条又一条龌龊的小巷，在押送之下，安德鲁斯打马厩那边进了「白鹿」客栈。

### IX / 九

===Original===
Andrews stood in the room where the previous night he had held Sir Henry’s mistress in his arms and watched with tired curiosity one star. He held in his hand a note which a winking waiter had given him. It was from Lucy and read “Henry has gone to bed. You can come to me. You know my room.” He had done what Elizabeth desired him to do, and in spite of the note he held, he told himself that it was for Elizabeth’s sake that he had done it. Didn’t I, he thought, renounce this morning with perfect sincerity this very reward? I did what I did then for Elizabeth and why should I not take any small benefits which come after? I had no thought for this when I stood in the witness box. It was an interesting moral point.

Carlyon now could come and go where he liked. Nothing, Andrews thought with apprehension, could prevent him strolling that very evening into the White Hart. It was so exactly the kind of thing for Carlyon to do that Andrews looked with a sudden start behind him. The door was shut. He longed to bolt it. As for this letter it could not be denied that he would be safer that night in Lucy’s bed than in his own. That was a reason which no one could deny. It would be to save myself, he told the star to which he instinctively addressed words meant for Elizabeth, for no other reason. I do not love her. Never will I love anyone but you. I swear to that. If a man loves one, he cannot help still lusting after others. But it was love not lust, I promise, that strengthened me this morning.

After all, he said to the star, I shall never see you again, and must I therefore never know another woman? I cannot come to you, for they will be watching for me there, and you do not love me. I should be a fool… and he stopped speaking to himself, struck by the astonishing knowledge of how deeply his heart longed to be a fool. Reason, reason, reason. I must cling to that, he thought. Reason and his body seemed to act together in a somewhat evil partnership. In fear of his own heart he began to play on fear for his own safety, and that fear seemed strangely less strong than was its wont. And then he turned to the thought of Lucy and the feel of her body pressed to him and her close promises the night before. He imagined her naked and in disgusting attitudes, and tried to whip his body into a blind lust which would forget for a time at least the dictates of his heart. Yet strangely even his lust seemed less strong. What have you done to me? he cried despairingly at the lonely star.

===Chinese===
安德鲁斯（Andrews）站在那间屋子里——前一天夜里，他曾在那里把亨利爵士（Sir Henry）的情妇搂在怀里——倦怠而好奇地望着一颗星。他手里拿着一张字条，是个挤眉弄眼的侍者递给他的。字条是露西（Lucy）写的：“亨利已经睡下了。你可以来我这里。你知道我的房间。”他已经照伊丽莎白（Elizabeth）的心愿做了那件事；尽管手里攥着这张字条，他还是对自己说，他那样做是为了伊丽莎白。今天上午，我不是怀着十二万分的真诚，亲口放弃过这份报偿么，他想？我当时那样做是为了伊丽莎白，那么随后送上门来的小小好处，我为什么不能受用？我站在证人席上的时候，可没有想过这个。这倒是一个有意思的道德问题。

如今卡莱昂（Carlyon）爱到哪儿就能到哪儿。安德鲁斯惴惴地想，没有什么能拦住他今晚就踱进「白鹿」（the White Hart）。这恰恰是卡莱昂会做的那种事，一想到这里，安德鲁斯猛地回头去看。门关着。他恨不得去插上门闩。至于这封信，无可否认，今夜睡在露西的床上，总比睡在自己的床上安全。这是一个谁也没法反驳的理由。这只是为了救我自己，他对那颗星说——他不由自主地，把本要对伊丽莎白讲的话讲给了它——再没有别的缘故。我不爱她。除了你，我永远不会爱任何人。对此我起誓。一个人若爱着一个，也免不了仍旧贪恋旁人。可今早支撑住我的，我保证，是爱，不是欲。

说到底，他对那颗星说，我反正再也见不到你了，难道因此我就永远不该再认识别的女人？我无法到你那儿去，因为那里有他们守着，而你又不爱我。那我岂不成了傻子……说到这里，他忽然中止了自言自语：他惊异地发现，自己心底是何等深切地巴望着做一回傻子。理性，理性，理性。我必须抓住它，他想。理性和他的身体仿佛结成一伙不大干净的搭档，行动一致。他害怕自己的心，便拨弄起自身安全上的恐惧来；那恐惧却奇怪地不如往常那样有劲了。随后他的念头转到了露西身上，转到她贴着他的身体的感觉、昨夜她凑在耳边的许诺。他想象她赤身露体、摆出种种淫态，想把自己的身体抽打成一种盲目的欲火，好叫它至少一时忘掉他心底的谕令。可奇怪的是，连他的欲火似乎也不那么旺了。你把我怎么了？他朝那颗孤星绝望地喊道。

===Original===
It was then that he heard someone twist cautiously the handle of the door. He forgot star, Elizabeth, Lucy, everything but his own safety. In one stride he reached the oil lamp which lit the room and turned it out. The room was still too light or seemed so to his hammering nerves from the wash of moonlight which entered at the window. It was too late to hide behind the door, so Andrews pressed his back against the wall and cursed himself for being weaponless. What a sentimental fool he had been to leave his knife behind him at the cottage. Where were the two runners, he wondered, who were supposed to guard him? Drunk in bed in all probability. He watched the door handle with fascination. It was of white marble and glimmered, touched by the crest of the moon’s wave, with deceptive distinctness. Again it twisted round with surprising silentness and then flew outwards like a thrown ball. An oil lamp stood in the passage outside and its light cast a kind of mocking halo round the head of the cockney Harry, who stood in the doorway, his face thrust forward and moving from side to side, like that of a snake.

Andrews pressed himself still harder against the wall, and Cockney Harry sidled into the room. As though he was aware that the light in the passage put him at a disadvantage he shut the door behind him. “Andrews,” he whispered. His eyes were not yet used to the dark, and the silence made him uneasy. He too put his back against the wall, opposite the place where Andrews stood, as if he feared attack. Then he saw Andrews. “So there you are,” he said. Andrews clenched his fists in preparation for an unexpected spring, but the smuggler saw the movement and flashed a knife warningly in the moon’s ray. “Stay where you are,” he whispered.

“There are runners in this hotel,” Andrews also lowered his voice. “What do you want?”

===Chinese===
就在这时，他听见有人小心翼翼地拧动门把手。星星、伊丽莎白、露西，他全忘了，只剩自身的安危。他一个大步跨到点着的那盏油灯跟前，把它捻灭。屋里还是太亮——至少他那颗咚咚狂跳的神经觉得如此——月光从窗口泼了进来。躲到门后已经来不及，安德鲁斯便把脊背贴上墙，咒骂自己手上没个家伙。他竟把刀丢在了小屋里，真是多愁善感的傻子。那两个本该看守他的捕快（runners）哪儿去了？他纳闷，八成在床上醉着。他着了魔似的盯着门把手看。那是白大理石的，月亮浪头的峰尖扫过它，泛着一层骗人的清晰光泽。把手又一次悄没声儿地转起来，转得叫人吃惊，随后像抛出的球一样向外飞去。外面的走廊里点着一盏油灯，灯光给伦敦佬哈里（Cockney Harry）的脑袋罩上了一圈嘲弄似的光环；他站在门口，脸朝前探着，左右摆动，活像一条蛇的头。

安德鲁斯把自己往墙上贴得更紧，伦敦佬哈里侧着身子溜进屋来。他似乎明白走廊的灯光让自己吃亏，随手把身后的门带上了。“安德鲁斯，”他低声唤。他的眼睛还没适应黑暗，那份寂静叫他心里发毛。他也把背贴上墙，正对着安德鲁斯站的地方，好像怕人偷袭。随后他看见了安德鲁斯。“原来你在这儿，”他说。安德鲁斯攥紧双拳，防备对方冷不丁扑上来，可那走私者看出了他的动作，就着月光一晃刀子，算是警告。“待在原地别动，”他低声说。

“这家客栈里有捕快，”安德鲁斯也压低了嗓音。“你要干什么？”

===Original===
“I’m not afeared of the runners now,” the man said. “But look ’ere,” he added plaintively, “why d’you want to quarrel? I’m ’ere to do you a service, strite I am.”

“To do me a service?” Andrews repeated. “Do you forget who I am?”

“Oh, I don’t forget ’ow you squeaked on us, but one good turn deserves another. You didn’t squeak on me this afternoon, and you might ’ave done easy.”

“It wasn’t for love of that face of yours,” Andrews said. His fists remained clenched against any sudden attack.

===Chinese===
“如今这帮捕快我可不怕了，”那人说。“可是你瞧，”他委屈巴巴地补了一句，“你干吗要跟我呛？我是来给你办事的，真格的。”

“给我办事？”安德鲁斯重复道。“你忘了我是谁了？”

“嗬，你怎样告的密，我可没忘，可一好还得一好嘛。今儿下晌你没告我，其实你要告还不容易。”

“可不是为了你那张脸好看，”安德鲁斯说。他的拳头仍旧攥着，防备任何突然的袭击。

===Original===
“You ain’t very griteful,” Harry complained. “Don’t you want to ’ear my news?”

“What news?”

“Of Carlyon an’ the others.”

“No, I’ve finished with them,” he said and added, as always with a curious aching heart, slowly, as though in an effort to overcome with finality each ache, “I never want to see that man again.”

“Ah, but ’e ain’t finished with you. Nor with yer ladybird.”

===Chinese===
“你这人可真不领情，”哈里抱怨道。“不想听听我的消息？”

“什么消息？”

“卡莱昂和其余那些人的。”

“不，我跟他们一刀两断了，”他说，又添上一句——照例带着一种奇怪的、作痛的心绪，说得慢慢的，仿佛要用决绝的语气压下每一阵疼痛——“那个人，我再也不想见了。”

“唉，可他没跟你完，也没跟你那位小娘子完。”

===Original===
Andrews started forward. “What do you mean?”

“Now keep back,” Harry flashed his knife again. “What I mean is they feel they been cheated by ’er—cheated shimeful.”

“Carlyon wouldn’t do anything to her, I know he wouldn’t.”

“Ah, but there’s Joe. ’E says she ought to ’ave a fright, an’ Carlyon agrees to that, but ’e don’t know what Joe and ’Ake calls a fright. They are all off to give it ’er tomorrer or the next dy.”

“You are lying, you know you are lying,” Andrews panted a little like a dog thirsty or out of breath. “This is a trap to get me to go back there, so that you’ll catch me. But I won’t, I won’t go back I tell you.”

===Chinese===
安德鲁斯往前一冲。“你什么意思？”

“退后，退后，”哈里又晃了晃刀。“我的意思是，他们觉得叫她给骗了——骗得好不丢脸。”

“卡莱昂不会对她怎么样的，我知道他不会。”

“唉，可还有乔（Joe）呢。乔说该吓唬吓唬她，卡莱昂也点了头，可他不知道乔和黑克（Hake）嘴里的吓唬算个什么名堂。明儿，要不后天，他们就要一齐出动去找她了。”

“你撒谎，你知道你在撒谎，”安德鲁斯有点喘，像一条渴极的、或是跑乏了的狗。“这是个圈套，想哄我回那儿去，好让你们抓住我。可我不去，我告诉你，我不回去。”

===Original===
“Why, that’s why I’m ’ere—to warn yer against goin’, in case you were thinkin’ of it. They’ll all be there. Carlyon’ll kill you as soon as look at you. Though ’Ake says as killin’s too good. ’E says they oughter ’ave some fun with you first.”

“Well, you can tell them that I’m never going back there. It’s no use laying that trap for me.”

“Good. Now I’ve warned yer an’ we’re quits. Next time,” Harry spat on the floor expressively and again flashed the steel of his knife in the moonlight, “don’t you expect me to be friendly.” He gave the impression of sliding across the floor. The white marble handle again flew outwards and the smuggler disappeared. Up the street the clock of St. Anne’s Church beat out with irritating deliberation the half after eleven.

===Chinese===
“咦，这正是我来这儿的原因——来劝你别去，就怕你动了那个念头。他们全都要在场。卡莱昂见你一面就能要你的命。不过黑克说，杀死你还太便宜了你。他说得先拿你开开心。”

“哼，你可以告诉他们，我永远不回那儿去。给我下这种圈套是白费劲。”

“好。如今话我给你捎到了，咱们两清。下一回，”哈里朝地板上意味深长地啐了一口，刀刃又在月光里闪了一闪，“可别指望我再跟你客气。”他整个人像是从地板上滑了出去。白大理石把手又一次向外飞开，走私者不见了。街那头，圣安妮教堂（St. Anne’s Church）的钟慢条斯理地敲出十一点半，敲得人心烦。
===Original===
Like a dream the man had entered and like a dream he had gone. Why could he not have been one more degree a phantom and become a vision only? Now inevitably a turmoil was roused in the mind. Carlyon would not harm a woman, Andrews thought. It is only a trap to catch me. But then was it likely that they would plan such a trap for me, a coward? They could not expect to do anything but repel him by danger. Again he repeated to himself that she was safe, that Carlyon would see to that, but still he could not dispel from his mind the thought of Joe and Hake. Tomorrow or the next day. If he were to leave tonight he could warn her in time, and they could both escape. But that was only if it were not a trap. Perhaps even now Harry, Joe, Hake, Carlyon and the rest were preparing to meet him on the downs. And yet how good, how glorious it would be, to be coming down the hill at dawn, to wait perhaps for the first sign of smoke to show that she was awake, to tap on the door and see recognition lit in her eyes. She would have to welcome me, he thought. I have earned that, for I have done all that she told me to do. In a medley of the stories of his childhood he imagined—“I have climbed the hill of glass and Gretel waits.” And then, he thought, I would help her get some breakfast and we would sit together in front of the fire. And I would tell her everything. His momentary exhilaration died and left the cold truth, danger to himself and her and more than that the knowledge that she would greet him as a not too welcome friend. Neither I nor any other man will ever approach her. What was the use of risking his life—miserable, debased it might be, but only he knew how infinitely precious—in return for what? A kind word. He did not want kind words. Let them give her a little pain. He had suffered. Why should not everyone in the world suffer? It was the common lot. Carlyon would see that they did not go too far.

As his fingers tightened in perplexity he felt still in his hand Lucy’s note. Here was someone who would give him more than kind words and yet exact no sense of responsibility. All his reason commanded him to go to her, only his heart, and that hard abstract critic for once allied to his heart, opposed. I shall be safe with her tonight, he thought, and tomorrow Carlyon and the others will have gone off over the downs and the road to London will be safe. Why, if he went to Elizabeth now, he would have no money for their escape. You mustn’t be dependent on her money, reason added, striking a noble attitude. That decided him. Why, even honour forbade the dangerous course.

===Chinese===
那人像梦一样来，又像梦一样去了。为什么他不索性再虚幻一分，化作一个纯然的幻影呢？此刻，心绪里的一场翻搅已在所难免。卡莱昂不会伤害一个女人，安德鲁斯想。这不过是捉我的一个圈套。可话又说回来，他们怎么会为我这么一个懦夫设计这样的圈套呢？他们能指望的，无非是拿危险把他吓退罢了。他又对自己念了一遍：她是安全的，卡莱昂会照应这一点；可乔和黑克的影子还是从他心里驱不散。明天，要不后天。倘若他今夜动身，还来得及警告她，两人便能一同逃走。可那得假定这不是个圈套。说不定就在此刻，哈里、乔、黑克、卡莱昂和其余的人，已经在丘陵上候着同他相会了。然而，天一亮便下山去，那该有多好，有多光彩；或许先等一等，等第一缕炊烟升起，表明她已醒来；再轻叩房门，看见她眼里亮起认出的光。她总得欢迎我，他想。这是我挣来的，因为她吩咐的事我全照办了。在儿时故事搅成的乱糟糟的合唱里，他暗自描画——“我已爬上了玻璃山，格蕾特尔在等着。”然后呢，他想，我帮她张罗点早饭，我们一同坐在炉火前。我把一切讲给她听。那一阵短暂的兴奋熄了，剩下冰冷的实情：他和她都危在旦夕；还不止此——他明知道，她迎接他，会像迎接一位并不怎么受欢迎的客人。无论是我还是任何别的男人，永远都不该走近她。那么拿性命去冒险——这条命或许卑贱，或许下作，可唯有他自己知道它无限珍贵——又能换来什么？一句好话。他不要好话。就让他们叫她受一点痛吧。他受过痛了。为什么世上的人就不该都受痛？这本是众人的同命。卡莱昂自会看着他们，不至于闹得过火。

他的手指苦恼地收紧，这才觉出露西的字条还在手里。这里有的是一个人，给他的不止一句好话，却并不向他讨要什么责任感。他的全部理性都命令他到她那儿去，唯有他的心——还有那位冷峻而抽象的批评家，这回头一遭同他的心联起手来——表示反对。今夜同她在一起，我便安全了，他想；明天，卡莱昂和其余的人都会动身翻过丘陵，上伦敦的路就太平了。况且，倘若此刻去伊丽莎白那里，他连两人逃亡的钱都没有。你可不能指望她的钱过活，理性摆出一副高贵的姿态，又补了一句。这下他拿定了主意。瞧，连荣誉都不容许那条危险的路。

===Original===
He passed through the dark passage and up the stairs, slowly, still a little doubtful and reluctant. In one of the rooms which now faced him Sir Henry Merriman slept. There was even a little danger, he now realised, in this course, danger of being stranded without money in this perilous Sussex. He knew which was Lucy’s room and cautiously he turned the handle and went in. He still held her note, as though a passport, in his hand.

“Here I am,” he said. He could not see her, but one hand stumbled on the foot of a bed.

There was a small sigh, a yawn, and through the darkness a sleepy whisper, “How late you are.”

His hand felt down the bed till he reached a cool sheet and beneath it he felt her body. He snatched his hand away as though it had touched a flame. The note fell from it to the floor. O, if he could surrender to his heart for once and not his body, and if he could go now before it was too late. Three hours’ walk over the downs beneath the moon and he would be home again.

===Chinese===
他穿过黑黢黢的走廊，上楼去，走得很慢，仍旧有点迟疑，有点不情愿。此刻正对着他的某间屋子里，亨利·梅里曼爵士（Sir Henry Merriman）正在酣睡。他此刻才明白，走这条路甚至也有几分危险——在这险象环生的萨塞克斯（Sussex），落得身无分文、进退无路的危险。他知道露西的房间是哪一间，小心翼翼拧动把手，走了进去。她那张字条还在他手里攥着，像一纸护照。

“我来了，”他说。他看不见她，一只手却磕磕绊绊摸到了床脚。

一声轻轻的叹息，一个呵欠，黑暗里传来一句睡意惺忪的耳语：“你来得可真晚。”

他的手顺着床摸下去，摸到一片凉凉的床单，床单底下，他触到了她的身体。他猛地缩回手，像碰着了火苗。字条从手里飘落在地板上。哦，倘若这一次他能顺从自己的心，而不是自己的身体；倘若他能趁事情还不曾太迟，现在就走。三个钟点，就着月光走过丘陵，他就能重新到家。

===Original===
“Where are you?” she said. “I can’t see in the dark. Come here.”

“I only came to say…” he said and hesitated. His heart had spoken, given courage by an image of Elizabeth as she had faced Carlyon, his cup raised to her lips, and his body had cut his words short, for his hand retained the feel of her body.

“That you were going again?” she asked. “You fool.”

He felt his flesh rising to her whisper.

“Will you ever get a chance like this again?” she murmured with an air of unfeigned carelessness. “You know what you are missing, don’t you?”

===Chinese===
“你在哪儿？”她说。“黑地里我看不见。过来。”

“我只是来告诉……”他说着，迟疑了。他的心开了口——伊丽莎白面对卡莱昂、卡莱昂把杯盏举到她唇边的那个形象给了它勇气；可他的身体把他剩下的话拦腰截断，因为他的手上还留着她身体的触感。

“告诉人家你又要走了？”她问。“傻子。”

他觉出自己的皮肉正应着她这声耳语躁动起来。

“这样的机会，你往后还会有吗？”她咕哝着，带着一副不掺假的漫不经心。“你错过的是什么，你心里有数，是不是？”

===Original===
He took a step away from the bed. “How common you are,” he said. His hand felt behind him for the door handle, but he could not find it.

“You know you enjoy that,” she answered. She did not seem to argue but rather to advise him gently and dispassionately for his own good. Her quiet irritated and attracted him at the same time. “I’d like to make her squeal,” he thought.

“At least before you go,” she said, “strike a light and see what you miss. Put out your hand.” He obeyed her reluctantly. He felt her fingers touch his. “How symbolical,” she laughed a little. “Here’s a flint and steel. Now strike a light. There is a candle here,” and she guided his hand to a table beside her bed.

“I won’t,” he said.

===Chinese===
他朝床外退了一步。“你多么下作，”他说。他的手在身后摸索门把手，却摸不着。

“你知道你爱听这个。”她答道。她不像在争辩，倒像在好意地、平心静气地劝他，替他着想。她这份平静，叫他又恼火，又被吸引。“我倒想叫她叫出声来，”他想。

“走以前至少，”她说，“打个火，瞧瞧你错过的是什么。把手伸过来。”他不情愿地依了她。他觉得她的手指碰了碰他的。“多有象征意味，”她轻轻笑了一声。“火镰火石在这儿。来，打个火。这儿有支蜡烛。”她引着他的手摸到床边的一张桌上。

“我不打，”他说。

===Original===
“Are you afraid?” she asked curiously. “You’ve turned very pure since last night. Have you fallen in love?”

“Not fallen,” he replied more to himself than her.

“And you boasted so of all the women you’ve known. Surely you aren’t afraid. You ought to be more used to us.”

He turned his back on her. “Very well,” he said. “I’ll strike a light and then I’ll go. I know your sort. You won’t leave a man alone.” Without looking in her direction he struck a light and lit the candle. It made a small yellow patch on the opposite wall and in that radiance he suddenly saw with extraordinary clarity the face of Elizabeth creased by fear till it was ugly, almost repulsive. Then it was blotted out by two other faces, that of Joe, the black bearded mouth open in a laugh, and that of the mad youth Richard Tims, red and angry. Then there was only the yellow radiance again.

===Chinese===
“你怕了？”她好奇地问。“打昨儿夜里起，你变得贞洁得很。莫非坠入了情网？”

“不是坠入，”他答道，倒更像说给自己听。

“你还把认得的女人吹了个遍呢。总不至于怕了吧。你也该把我们这些女人见惯了才是。”

他转过身去背对她。“好极了，”他说。“我打个火，然后就走。你们这类人我见识过，死也不肯放过一个男人。”他目不斜视，打着了火，点着蜡烛。对面的墙上现出一小片黄晕，就在那片光里，他突然无比清晰地看见了伊丽莎白的脸，恐惧把它揉得皱缩难看，几乎可憎。随后这张脸叫另两张脸抹去了：一张是乔的，黑胡子的嘴大张着在笑；另一张是疯癫的少年理查德·蒂姆斯（Richard Tims）的，涨红着，怒气冲冲。接着，又只剩下一片黄晕。

===Original===
“I can’t stay,” Andrews cried, “she’s in danger,” and he swung round candle in hand.

The girl was stretched on the outside of the bed. She had flung her nightdress on the floor. She was slim, long legged with small firm breasts. With a modesty which had no pretence of truth she spread her hands over her stomach and smiled at him.

“Run away then,” she said.

He came a little nearer and with his eyes fixed on her face, so as not to see her body, he began to make excuses, reason, even plead. “I must go,” he said, “someone came to warn me tonight. A girl—I’ve got her into danger. I must go to her. Just now on that wall I thought I saw her scream.”

===Chinese===
“我不能留下，”安德鲁斯喊道，“她有危险，”说着便握着蜡烛转过身来。

姑娘舒展着躺在床沿外。睡衣早叫她甩在了地板上。她苗条，腿长，一对小小的乳房结结实实。她双手掩在腹上，朝他微笑，那副娇羞的样子却压根没打算叫人当真。

“那你逃你的呀，”她说。

他走近了些，两眼死盯住她的脸，免得看见她的身体，开始找托词，讲道理，以至哀恳。“我必须走，”他说，“今晚有人来给我报信。一个姑娘——是我把她拖进危险的。我必须到她那儿去。方才那面墙上，我好像看见她在喊叫。”
===Original===
“You are dreaming.”

“But sometimes dreams come true. Don’t you see—I must go. I got her into this danger.”

“Well go. I’m not stopping you am I? But listen. What difference will it make if you stay here just for half an hour?” She turned over on her side, and his eyes could not help but follow her body as it moved. “She’s cool now,” he thought, “but I could make her warm.”

===Chinese===
“你在做梦。”

“可梦有时候会成真。你还不明白——我必须走。是我把她拖进这个危险的。”

“那就走呗。我又没拦着你，是不是？可你听着。你就在这儿多待半个钟头，又能差得了什么？”她侧过身去，她的身子一动，他的眼睛不由自主地跟着挪。“她这会儿是凉的，”他想，“可我能让她热起来。”

===Original===
“Go then,” she said. “You won’t have another chance, but I don’t care. I’m feeling restless—this damn spring. I’ll go into Harry. He’s old and tired, but I believe he’s more of a man than you.” Although she spoke of going she did not go, but watched him with faintly amused eyes. Andrews moistened his lips, which were dry. He felt thirsty. He no longer tried to keep his eyes off her body. He knew now that he could not move away.

“I’ll stay,” he said. He put his knee on the bed, but her hands held him away.

“Not like that,” she said. “I’m not a harlot. Take off those things.” He hesitated for a moment and glanced at the candle.

“No there must be a little light,” she whispered, a little run of excitement in her tone, “so that we can see each other.”

He obeyed her unwillingly. He felt that he was raising a barrier of time between Elizabeth and any help which he might bring. Even now he could not forget the dream, vision, fantasy, what you will, which he had seen in the candle’s light, it was conquered only when he felt the girl’s body stretched along his own.

===Chinese===
“那就走吧，”她说。“这机会过了可没有第二回，不过我不在乎。我正浑身躁得慌——这鬼春天。我找哈里去。他是老了，乏了，可我相信他比你更像个男人。”嘴上说着走，她却没有走，只用微微含笑的、带点看戏意味的眼睛望着他。安德鲁斯舔了舔发干的嘴唇。他觉得渴。他不再逼着自己把目光从她身体上挪开了。他此刻明白，自己已经挪不动了。

“我留下，”他说。他把膝盖跪上床，她的双手却把他挡在外头。

“不许这样，”她说。“我不是妓女。把那些东西脱了。”他迟疑了片刻，瞟了一眼蜡烛。

“不，得留一点光，”她低声说，声调里掠过一丝小小的兴奋，“好叫我们看得见彼此。”

他不情愿地依了她。他觉得自己正在伊丽莎白与他可能带去的任何救援之间，砌起一道时间的墙。即便此刻，他也忘不掉方才烛光里的那场梦、幻象、心造的影子，随你怎么叫它；直到觉出姑娘的身体贴着自己舒展开来，它才被压服。

===Original===
“Closer,” she said. His fingers closed on her, pinching the flesh. He buried his mouth between her breasts. He could see nothing but he heard her laugh a little. “You cannot hurt me like that,” she said…

He opened his eyes and thought at first how strange it was that a candle should burn with a silver flame. Then he saw that the candle was out and the light was the first of day. He sat up and looked at his companion. She slept with her mouth slightly open, breathing hard. He eyed first her body and then his own with disgust. He touched her shoulder gingerly with his hand, and she opened her eyes. “I should cover that,” he said, and turning his back, put his feet over the side of the bed.

From her voice he judged that she was smiling, but her smile which in the dark had seemed the beckoning of a passionate mystery, he considered now a shallow mechanical thing. He was disgusted with himself and her. He had been treading, he felt, during the last few days on the border of a new life, in which he would learn courage and even self forgetfulness, but now he had fallen back into the slime from which he had emerged.

“Have you enjoyed yourself?” she asked.

“I’ve wallowed,” he said, “if that’s what you mean.”

===Chinese===
“再过来些，”她说。他的手指掐住了她的肉。他把嘴埋进她的双乳之间。他什么也看不见，只听见她轻轻笑了。“这样你伤不着我，”她说……

他睁开眼睛，起初还纳闷，蜡烛怎么会烧出银色的火苗。随后他看清了：蜡烛已经熄灭，那是天光乍亮。他坐起身，望着身边的同伴。她睡着，嘴微微张着，呼吸粗重。他先是厌恶地打量她的身体，又打量自己的。他小心翼翼伸手碰了碰她的肩，她睁开了眼睛。“那个还是遮上为好，”他说着转过身去，两脚下了床。

听声音他判断她在笑；可她那笑容，黑暗里曾像一个炽烈的秘密在招手，如今在他看来只是一样浅薄机械的玩意儿。他厌恶她，也厌恶自己。他觉得，最近这几天，自己一直行走在一种新生活的边界线上，在那样的生活里，他将学会勇气，甚至学会忘我；可如今，他又跌回了刚刚爬出来的那片泥淖。

“你快活了么？”她问。

“我打了个滚，”他说，“如果你问的是这个。”

===Original===
He could imagine her pouting at him and he hated that pout. “Am I pleasanter than all the other women you’ve boasted about?”

“You’ve made me feel myself dirtier,” he answered. But is there no way out of this slime? he thought silently. I was a fool and imagined I was escaping, but now I have sunk so deep that surely I’ve reached the bottom.

“I could kill myself,” he said aloud.

The girl laughed contemptuously. “You haven’t the courage,” she said, “and anyway what of that fair one who’s in danger?”

Andrews put his hand to his head. “You made me forget her,” he said. “I can’t face her after this.”

===Chinese===
他能想见她朝他撅起嘴，他恨那副撅嘴的样子。“跟你吹嘘过的那些女人相比，我更叫你受用么？”

“你让我觉得自己更脏了，”他答道。可这片泥淖当真没有出路么，他默默想。我原是个傻子，还当自己在脱身，如今却沉得这样深，想必已经沉到底了。

“我可以自杀，”他大声说。

姑娘轻蔑地笑了。“你没有那份勇气，”她说，“再说，你那位有危险的美人儿又该怎么办呢？”

安德鲁斯抬手按住头。“你叫我把她忘了，”他说。“出这样的事，我再没脸见她了。”

===Original===
“How young you are,” she said. “Surely you know by this time that the feeling won’t last. For a day we are disgusted and disappointed and disillusioned and feel dirty all over. But we are clean again in a very short time, clean enough to go back and soil ourselves all over again.”

“One must reach an end some time.”

“Never.”

“Are you a devil as well as a harlot?” Andrews asked with interest, but without anger. “Do you mean to say that it’s no use trying to be clean?”

“How often have you felt sick and disgusted and resolved never to sin again?”

===Chinese===
“你多么年轻，”她说。“到了这步田地，你总该明白这种感觉长不了。有那么一天，我们厌恶，失望，幻灭，觉得自己从头到脚脏透了。可是用不了多久，我们又干净了，干净得可以回去，再把自己糟蹋一遍。”

“人总有个到头的时候。”

“永远不会。”

“你既是妓女，又是魔鬼不成？”安德鲁斯饶有兴致地问，倒并不动怒。“照你说，想学干净是白费力气？”

“你有多少回恶心欲呕、深恶痛绝、发誓再不犯罪？”

===Original===
“I can’t count them. You are right. It’s no use. Why can’t I die?”

“How curious. You are one of those people—I’ve met them before—who can’t rid themselves of a conscience. How talkative one becomes after a bout of this. I’ve noticed it often. I thought you were going to rescue that girl of yours from danger. Why don’t you go? It’s ridiculous to sit on the edge of a bed naked and philosophise.”

“It may be a trap and they’ll kill me.”

“I thought you wouldn’t go when it came to the point.”

“You are wrong,” Andrews stood up, “that’s the very reason why I’m going.”

===Chinese===
“数不清了。你说得对。没有用。我怎么就死不了呢？”

“真怪。你正是那样一种人——我从前也见过——良心这东西在身上摆脱不掉。经过这么一场，人多么健谈啊。这一点我常常留意到。我还当你要去把你那姑娘从危险里救出来呢。那你干吗不去？光着身子坐在床沿上谈哲学，可也真够滑稽的。”

“那也许是个圈套，他们会杀了我。”

“我就想，到了节骨眼上，你是不会走的。”

“你错了，”安德鲁斯站起身，“这正是我要走的缘由。”
===Original===
When he left the hotel he took no precautions whatever, but walked down the street with his eyes fixed straight in front of him. He felt no fear of death, but a terror of life, of going on soiling himself and repenting and soiling himself again. There was, he felt, no escape. He had no will left. For certain exalted moments he had dreamed of taking Elizabeth to London, of gaining her love and marrying her, but now he saw that even if he gained that high desire, it would only be to soil her and not cleanse himself. When I had been married to her for a month, he thought, I would be creeping out of the house on the sly to visit prostitutes. The cool air of early morning touched him in vain. He was hot with shame and self-loathing. He longed with a ridiculous pathos for the mere physical purification of a bath.

He reached the downs as a first orange glow lifted above the eastern horizon. Its fragile soaring beauty, like a butterfly with delicate powdered wings resting on a silver leaf, touched him and increased his shame. If he had not seen Lucy but had started direct for the cottage some hours before, how that glow would have heartened him. What a prelude it would have been to his return.

===Chinese===
离开客栈时，他什么防备也没有拿，只管沿着大街走下去，两眼直勾勾地盯着前方。他不怕死，怕的是活着，怕的是这样继续把自己弄脏、忏悔、再把自己弄脏。他觉得，无路可逃。他的意志已经荡然无存。曾有几个意气高扬的时刻，他梦想带伊丽莎白去伦敦，赢得她的爱，娶她为妻；可如今他看清了，即便那崇高的心愿得偿，也不过是把她玷污，而不能洗净自己。等我娶她满一个月，他想，我就会偷偷溜出家门去嫖妓了。清晨清凉的空气白白拂过他的脸。他又羞又憎，浑身发烫。他怀着一种可笑的凄惶，渴望的不过是洗一个澡，得一场单纯属于身体的洁净。

他登上丘陵时，第一抹橙色的光正从东方地平线上升起。那纤弱而飞升的美，像一只翅膀上敷着细粉的蝴蝶停栖在银色的叶上，触动了他，也加重了他的羞惭。倘若他没有去见露西，而是几个钟头前就直接动身往小屋去，这道霞光会给他多大的鼓舞！那本会是他归来的多好的一支前奏。

===Original===
From where he walked it was not yet light enough to see the valley clearly. Only at intervals the red spark of a lighted window would make a crevice in the grey veil, and after he had walked some miles a cock crew. The downs were bare of life, save for the occasional brooding hunched form of a dark tree. He walked, and as he walked the first poignancy of his shame departed and the events of the night slipped a little way into shadow. When Andrews realised this, he stayed for a moment still and strove to drag them back. For this had happened many times before. It was the first stage towards a repetition of the sin, this forgetfulness. How could he ever keep clean if the sense of shame was so short lived? After all I enjoyed myself, he thought against his will, why repent? It’s a coward’s part. Go back and do it again. Why run my head into danger? With an effort he clenched his will and ran, to stifle thought, ran fast until he had no more breath and flung himself down upon the grass.

The grass grew in cool, crisp, salty tufts, on which he leant his forehead. If it were barren of desire and of the need of any action how sweet life would be. If it were only this coolness, this silver sky touched now with green, those unfurling wings of orange. If he could but sit and watch and listen—listen to Carlyon speaking, and watch the enthusiasm in his eyes, with no dangerous echo in his own. It was a strange, unrealisable thing that Carlyon was his enemy. Carlyon was seeking to kill him, and yet his heart still leapt a little at the sound of the name. Carlyon, who was all the things which Andrews wished to be—courageous, understanding, hopelessly romantic, not about women, but about life, Carlyon who hated well because he knew so clearly what he loved—truth, danger, poetry. If I hate him, Andrews thought, it is because I have done him an injury, but he hates me because he thinks I’ve injured life. He tried to laugh—the man was only a romantic fool with an ugly face. That was the real secret of his humility, his courage, even his love of beauty. He was always seeking a compensation for his face, as though an ape in purple and ermine were less an ape. The qualities he had built round himself were dreams only, which Andrews by one act had destroyed. There remained the large body, heavy, however lightly poised, thick wrists, misshapen skull. Strip off Carlyon’s dreams and the remainder is inferior to me, Andrews thought. A sudden longing came that he could trap Carlyon into some unworthy action, not consonant with the dreams which he followed. That would show him they were dreams and not himself.

===Chinese===
在他走的地方，天光还不够亮，看不清谷中的景象。只是隔一阵，亮着灯的窗口便有一个红点子，在灰色的纱幕上撕开一道细缝；走了几英里之后，一声鸡啼。丘陵上空无生灵，只有偶尔一棵黑树蜷伏沉思的身影。他走着，走着走着，羞惭最初的刺痛消退了，夜里的事往暗影里退了些许。安德鲁斯一发觉这一点，便站住片刻，竭力要把它们拽回来。因为这样的事从前已发生过许多回。这种遗忘，正是重犯罪孽的第一步。倘若羞耻感这样短命，他还怎么保持干净？归根结底我痛快了一场，他身不由己地想，忏悔什么？那是懦夫的差事。回去再干一遍就是。何必把脑袋往危险里钻？他用力攥紧意志，跑了起来，为了掐断思虑，飞快地跑，直到喘不上气，才一头扑倒在草上。

草一簇簇地长着，清凉，爽脆，带着盐味，他把额头靠在上面。倘若生活里没有欲望，也不需要任何行动，那生活该有多甜。倘若只有这份清凉，这片此刻染上青绿的银色天空，那些舒展开来的橙色翅膀。倘若他只消坐着，看，听——听卡莱昂说话，看他眼里的热忱，而自己眼中没有危险的回声。卡莱昂竟是他的敌人，这是一件奇怪而无处落实的事。卡莱昂正要杀他，可一听见这个名字，他的心仍旧微微一跳。卡莱昂，他具有安德鲁斯希望自己具有的一切——勇敢，善解人意，无可救药地浪漫——不是对女人，而是对生活；卡莱昂恨得其所，因为他清清楚楚知道自己爱的是什么——真理、危险、诗。我恨他，安德鲁斯想，是因为我伤害过他；而他恨我，是因为他认定我伤害了生活本身。他想笑一笑——那人不过是个相貌丑陋的浪漫傻瓜。那才是他谦卑的真正秘密，他勇气的真正秘密，甚至他爱美的真正秘密。他一直在为那张脸寻求补偿，仿佛一只穿上紫袍貂裘的猿猴就不那么是猿猴了。他围绕自己筑起的那些品质不过是梦，而安德鲁斯一举把它们毁了。剩下的只有那巨大的身躯——纵然举步轻捷，依旧沉重——粗大的手腕，畸形参差的头骨。剥掉卡莱昂的梦，剩下的那点东西还不如我，安德鲁斯想。他突然生出一个渴望：但愿能诱使卡莱昂做出什么配不上他那些梦的卑劣行径。那就能向他证明：那些是梦，而不是他本人。

===Original===
How could one judge a man when all was said but by his body and his private acts, not by dreams he followed in the world’s eye? His father to his crew was a hero, a king, a man of dash, of initiative. Andrews knew the truth—that he was a bully who killed his wife and ruined his son. And myself, Andrews thought, I have as good dreams as any man, of purity and courage and the rest, but I can only be judged by my body which sins and is cowardly. How do I know what Carlyon is in private. But as he spoke he wondered uneasily whether Carlyon might not follow his dreams even when alone. Suppose that after all a man, perhaps when a child, at any rate at some forgotten time, chose his dreams whether they were to be good or evil. Then, even though he were untrue to them, some credit was owing simply to the baseless dreaming. They were potentialities, aspects, and no man could tell whether suddenly and without warning they might not take control and turn the coward for one instant into the hero.

Carlyon and I are then on the same plane, he thought, with a wistful longing for belief. He follows his dreams and I do not follow mine, but the mere dreaming is good. And I am better than my father, for he had no dreams, and that part of him that men admired came not from following an ideal but from mere physical courage. But how he longed now for that mere physical valour, which would give him the power to fling himself blind-eyed upon the breast of his dream. He sometimes imagined that if courage could be granted him for a moment only to turn his back on fear, his dreams would have strength to seize him in their current and sweep him irrevocably on, with no need of further decision or further gallantry.

===Chinese===
说到底，评判一个人，除了凭他的身体和他私下的行为，难道能凭他在世人眼前追随的梦么？他父亲在他的船员眼里是英雄，是王者，一个有闯劲、有胆识的人。安德鲁斯知道真相——那是个逼死妻子、毁掉儿子的恶霸。至于我自己，安德鲁斯想，我的梦不比谁差——纯洁、勇气，等等——可评判我的，只能是这具犯罪的、怯懦的身体。我又怎么知道卡莱昂私下里是什么样。可话一出口，他就不安地疑惑起来：卡莱昂兴许连独自一人的时候也在追随他的梦。倘若一个人终究——也许在孩提时，至少在某个被遗忘的时刻——选定过自己的梦，无论那梦是善是恶。那么，即便他后来背弃了那些梦，单凭那无凭无据的做梦，也该记他一功。那些梦是潜藏的可能，是一个个侧面，谁也说不准它们会不会突然地、毫无征兆地掌了权，把懦夫在一瞬间变成英雄。

这么说，卡莱昂和我在同一个平面上，他想，带着一种渴望相信的怅惘。他追随他的梦，我不追随我的，可单是做梦这件事就是好的。而且我比父亲强，因为他没有梦，他身上叫人钦佩的那一部分，并非出自对一个理想的追随，而只是出自单纯的体魄之勇。可他此刻多么渴望那种单纯的体魄之勇啊——有了它，他便有力量闭着眼睛扑进自己梦的怀抱。他有时想象，只要勇气肯赐给他一瞬，让他转背抛开恐惧，他的梦便会有力量把他卷进它们的湍流，一往无前地带走他，无须再作什么决定，也无须再逞什么英勇。

===Original===
He rose and with a little melodramatic gesture opened his arms as though he would entice courage to his heart, but all that came was a cold sweep of early wind. He walked on. Why could he not, as Lucy said, kill his conscience and be content? Why if he was given these aspirations, softened and blurred by sentiment as they were, was he not given sinew to attain them? He was the son of his mother, he supposed. Her heart had been trapped by vague romantic longings. His father when he desired something which could not be attained by other means had the power of showing himself as a sort of rough, genial fellow—a sea dog of the old Elizabethan tradition. He was of Drake’s county and he spoke Drake’s tongue. The sea had even given him a little of Drake’s face and manner, the colour, lines, aggressive beard, loud voice, loud laugh, what those who did not know him in his black moods called “a way with him.” Tears of anger, self-pity and some of love pricked Andrews’s eyes. If I could revenge you on the dead, he thought. Is there no way to hurt the dead? Yet he knew that that foolish sentimental heart would not have desired revenge. Was it not even possible to please the dead, he wondered, and so swiftly it seemed to his superstitious mind a supernatural answer, came the thought “Do not do as your father and ruin a woman.”

Still walking swiftly in the direction of Hassocks he swore silently that he would not. “I will only warn her,” he said, “and go.” Only by not seeing her again he felt could he prevent her ruin.

And yet how different it would have been if Carlyon had been his father. It did not seem odd to him so to think of the man who was seeking to kill him. Carlyon would have satisfied his mother’s heart, and he himself would have been born with will and backbone. He remembered his first meeting with Carlyon.

===Chinese===
他站起身，带着几分舞台腔张开双臂，仿佛要把勇气招引到自己心口；可是来的只有一阵清早寒风的扫掠。他继续往前走。他为什么不能像露西说的那样，杀了良心求个心安？既然给了他这些向往——尽管被感伤泡软了、磨糊了——为什么却不给他实现它们的筋骨？他想，他是母亲的孩子。母亲的心曾被缥缈的浪漫渴望困住。他父亲若想要什么而别无他法可得，便有本事把自己扮成一种粗豪爽利的好人——伊丽莎白时代老派的一条「海狗」。他出身德雷克（Drake）的郡，说的是德雷克的乡音。大海甚至给了他几分德雷克的面相与气派：那肤色，那皱纹，那咄咄逼人的胡须，大嗓门，大笑声——没见过他阴沉模样的人，管这叫「自有一套服人的气派」。愤怒、自怜，还有几分爱的泪水，刺得安德鲁斯眼睛发酸。倘若我能替你向死人复仇，他想。难道就没有办法伤害死人么？可他知道，那颗愚蠢的感伤的心，并不会想要复仇。甚至有没有可能让死者欢喜呢，他纳闷着；一个念头应声而至——快得在他迷信的心里俨然是一个超自然的回答——「不要步你父亲的后尘去毁掉一个女人。」

他仍旧朝哈索克斯（Hassocks）的方向疾走，心里默誓决不那样做。“我只是去警告她，”他说，“然后就走。”他觉得，只有不再见她，才能防止她被毁。

然而，倘若卡莱昂是他父亲，一切又会多么不同。这样去想那个正要杀他的人，他并不觉得奇怪。卡莱昂本可以满足他母亲那颗心的期待，而他自己也会生来就有意志，有脊梁。他记起初见卡莱昂的那一遭。

===Original===
He was walking by himself away from the school. He had one hour of freedom and exhilarated by it ran up the hill beyond the school, the sooner to escape the sight of the red brick barrack-like buildings, the sooner to see the moors stretching away, sweep beyond sweep of short heather, into the sunset. He ran with his eyes on the ground, for then he always seemed to move faster. He knew from experience that when he had counted two hundred and twenty-five he would be within a few feet of the summit. Two hundred and twenty-one, twenty-two, twenty-three, twenty-four, twenty-five. He raised his eyes. A man stood with his back to him, in much the same way as he had stood a few days before at the turn of the road beyond Hassocks. He was dressed in black and as then he gave the impression of bulk poised with incongruous lightness. He was staring at the sunset, but when he heard a step behind him he turned with remarkable swiftness, as though footsteps were associated in his mind with danger. Andrews saw then for the first time the broad shoulders, short thick neck, low receding apelike brow and the dark eyes that in a flash tumbled to the ground the whole of the animal impression which the body had raised. The eyes could on occasion, laugh, be merry, but their prevailing tone, Andrews found later, was a brooding sadness. They were smiling, however, when he first saw them with a kind of happy wonder.

“Have you seen it?” Carlyon had said with a hushed, trembling ecstasy and outflung finger, and Andrews had looked beyond him at a sky tumultuous with flame, an angry umber, rising from the grey ashes of the moor, spumed up in tottering pinnacles into the powdery blue smoke of the sky.

They stood in silence and stared at it, and then the stranger turned to him and said, “The school. I’m looking for the school.” It was as though he had mentioned the word prison to an escaped convict. “I’ve come from there,” Andrews said. “It’s down there.”

===Chinese===
他独自一人从学校走出来。他有一个钟点的自由，为这自由兴奋着，便跑上学校后面的小山，好早点逃开那些红砖营房似的楼房的视线，早点望见绵延而去的荒原，一浪接一浪的矮石楠，直铺进落日里去。他跑步时眼睛盯着地面，因为那样他总仿佛跑得更快。凭经验他知道，数到二百二十五，他就离山顶只有几步了。二百二十一，二十二，二十三，二十四，二十五。他抬起眼睛。一个男人背朝他站着，那站姿同几天前哈索克斯外大路拐弯处那个人站立的模样几乎一模一样。他一身黑衣，同那时一样，给人一个庞然的身躯却举重若轻的印象。他正凝望着落日，可一听见身后有脚步声，便以惊人的迅捷转过身来，仿佛脚步声在他心里同危险连在一起。安德鲁斯这才第一次看清那宽阔的肩膀、粗短的脖颈、低平后削的猿人似的额角，还有那双黑眼睛——一霎之间，它们把这副身躯唤起的整套兽类印象掀翻在地。那双眼睛偶尔也会笑，也会欢快，但安德鲁斯后来发现，它们的主调是一种沉思的悲哀。不过他初次望见它们时，它们正含着笑，带着一种快活的惊奇。

“你看见了么？”卡莱昂当时说，声音压得低低的，颤着一股狂喜，手指直直指出去；安德鲁斯便顺着他指的方向望去，只见满天翻腾的火焰，一股怒冲冲的赭褐，从荒原灰白的余烬里升起，喷涌成摇摇欲坠的尖峰，直上天空粉蓝的烟霭。

两人默默站着，凝望那片天光，随后陌生人转身对他说：“那个学校。我正要找那个学校。”那口气，就像对一个逃犯提起“监狱”二字。“我就是从那儿来的，”安德鲁斯说。“就在下面。”

===Original===
“One can’t see the sun set from there,” Carlyon remarked, and had the air in those few words of condemning the whole institution, masters, boys, buildings. He frowned a little and said contemptuously, “Do you belong there?” Andrews nodded.

“Do you like it?” Andrews hearing the tone gazed at the stranger with a peculiar fascination. Others had asked him that question as it were rhetorically, assuming a fervent assent. They generally added some jolly reference to beatings and a dull anecdote of their schooldays. But the stranger spoke to him as though they were both of one age, with a slight contempt as though there would be something ignoble in answering “yes.”

“I hate it,” he said.

“Why do you stay?” the question, quietly put, was stunning to the boy in its implications of free will.

“It’s worse at home,” he said. “My mother’s dead.”

“You should run away,” the stranger said carelessly and turning his back stared again at the sunset. Andrews watched him. At that moment his heart, barren of any object of affection, was ready open to hero worship. The man stood in front of him with his legs a little apart as though balancing himself upon the spinning globe. A sailor, Andrews thought, remembering that his father stood so.

===Chinese===
“在那儿看不见日落。”卡莱昂说，就这寥寥几个字，却仿佛把那整个机构——教师、学生、房子——全判了罪。他微微皱眉，鄙夷地问：“你是那儿的？”安德鲁斯点点头。

“你喜欢那儿吗？”听出这语气，安德鲁斯带着一种说不出的着迷凝望这陌生人。别人问过他这话，可都像在说客套话，笃定他会热烈称是；他们往往还要快活地提两句挨打的事，再讲一桩自己学生时代的无聊轶闻。可这陌生人对他说话，仿佛两人同庚，话里带着微微的轻蔑，仿佛答一声“喜欢”便有点下作了。

“我恨那儿，”他说。

“那你干吗还待在那儿？”这一问题问得平平静静，可它暗含的自由抉择，却震得那孩子发懵。

“家里更糟，”他说。“我母亲死了。”

“你应该逃走。”陌生人随口说，转身又凝望落日去了。安德鲁斯望着他。那一刻，他那颗心里空空荡荡、没有所爱的对象，正敞开着随时欢迎英雄崇拜。那人站在他面前，双腿微微叉开，仿佛在旋转的地球上稳住自身。一个水手，安德鲁斯想，记起父亲就是这样站着的。
===Original===
After a little the man turned again and seeing that the boy was still there asked him whether he happened to know a boy at the school called Andrews.

Andrews looked at him in amazement. It was as though a figure from a dream had suddenly stepped into reality and claimed acquaintanceship with him. “I’m Andrews,” he said.

“That’s strange,” the man said, watching him with a mixture of apprehension and curiosity, “you are pale. You don’t look strong. Unlike your father. I was your father’s friend,” he said.

The past tense caught Andrews’s attention. “I’m glad you are not his friend now,” he said. “I hate him.”

“He’s dead,” Carlyon said.

There was a pause and then Andrews said slowly, “I suppose you’d be shocked if I said I was glad.”

===Chinese===
过了一会儿，那人又转过身来，见那孩子还站在那儿，便问他碰巧认不认得学校里一个姓安德鲁斯的男孩。

安德鲁斯惊愕地望着他。仿佛一个梦里的人物忽然踏进了现实，前来与他攀识。“我就是安德鲁斯，”他说。

“真怪，”那人说，一边打量他，神情里疑虑与好奇混在一处，“你脸色苍白，看着不结实，不像你父亲。我从前是你父亲的朋友，”他说。

那个过去时态引起了安德鲁斯的注意。“我很高兴你现在不是他的朋友了，”他说。“我恨他。”

“他死了。”卡莱昂说。

顿了顿，安德鲁斯慢慢地说：“要是我说我高兴，你大概会吓一跳吧。”

===Original===
The stranger laughed. “Not in the least. I imagine that he’d be a particularly unlovely character on shore. He was a great sailor though. Let me introduce myself—my name’s Carlyon, skipper and owner of the *Good Chance*, your father’s ship.” He held out his hand. Andrews took it. The grip was firm, brief and dry.

“How did he die?” he asked.

“Shot. You knew what your father was?”

“I guessed,” Andrews said.

“And now,” Carlyon asked, “what do you want to do?” He suddenly made a twisted embarrassed motion with his hands. “Your father left me everything.” He added quickly, turning a little away, “Of course you have only to ask.
